# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Number of papers in this paper set: 327

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Powers, S. T. (2018). The Institutional Approach for Modeling the Evolution of Human Societies. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutionally coordinated punishment and reward mechanisms can stabilize cooperation and increase group efficiency in public goods games, but only if sufficient resources are allocated to these mechanisms. In structured populations with hard selection, there is a risk that groups will underinvest in punishment/reward, leading to a collapse of cooperation and group efficiency. This risk is mitigated if the benefits of public good investment experience diminishing returns, which stabilizes both cooperation and efficiency. The model also shows that individuals tend to prefer rewards over punishments, even when punishment would be more efficient. The findings are based on simulation results, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the presence and design of institutional punishment and reward mechanisms can have a strong, but context-dependent, effect on group efficiency in public goods games. The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive when sufficient resources are allocated to it, but negative or null if groups underinvest due to individual incentives. The model highlights the importance of game structure (e.g., group size, migration, diminishing returns) in moderating these effects. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, but only if the institutional context ensures adequate investment in enforcement; otherwise, efficiency may not improve or may even decline.

- source: Wu, Z. W., Xu, Z. J., & Zhang, L. Z. (2014). Punishment Mechanism with Self-Adjusting Rules in Spatial Voluntary Public Goods Games. *Communications in Theoretical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The introduction of a punishment mechanism in a spatial voluntary PGG, where players only know their own payoff and update strategies via a self-adjusting rule, can significantly increase average payoff (efficiency) when the cost of punishment is low. This effect is especially pronounced at low values of the multiplication factor r (mpcr), where, without punishment, cooperation and payoff would otherwise be low. The model demonstrates that as punishment cost decreases, the region of high average payoff expands. Memory length also affects outcomes: moderate memory can further improve average payoff, but very high memory may reduce it. The results are robust across initial conditions and show that punishment can transform low-efficiency environments into high-efficiency ones under the right parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial voluntary PGGs, enabling punishment (especially with low punishment cost and fixed punishment effectiveness) can substantially increase average efficiency compared to the control (no punishment) case, particularly when the mpcr is low. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the cost of punishment and the value of mpcr. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that lowering punishment cost or enabling punishment in low-mpcr environments will yield large efficiency gains. The model also highlights the importance of memory length as a moderator. The results are most directly applicable to spatial, voluntary, mixed-strategy PGGs with local information and self-adjusting update rules.

- source: Sun, X. P., Liu, X. Z., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). The impact of reputation-driven locally variable synergy factors on public goods games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing manipulators—who both punish defectors and reward cooperators—into a spatial public goods game can sustain cooperation and increase average group payoff, even when the synergy factor (r) is low and cooperation would otherwise fail. The model shows that for a range of parameter values (punishment/reward efficiency, manipulation cost), manipulators and cooperators can coexist, and the group achieves higher payoffs than in the absence of punishment/reward. When punishment is sufficiently efficient (beta/gamma high), the system can reach the all-cooperator state, maximizing group payoff. If the cost of manipulation is too high or punishment/reward is inefficient, cooperation collapses and payoffs drop. Reward withdrawal (manipulators reducing rewards when surrounded by cooperators) reduces the average payoff of cooperators and narrows the payoff gap between manipulators and cooperators. The model demonstrates that the combined effect of punishment and reward is robust but sensitive to parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-signal evidence that enabling punishment (and reward) in a spatial public goods game increases group efficiency and average payoff, especially when the control (no punishment/reward) condition would result in low cooperation and low payoff. The effect is moderated by the efficiency of punishment (punishment impact per cost), the cost to punishers/rewarders, and the synergy factor (mpcr equivalent). The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, enabling punishment (especially when combined with reward) will generally increase efficiency, provided the punishment is not too costly and is sufficiently effective. The model also highlights that the presence of defectors and the structure of the population (spatial lattice) are important, but the main prediction is robust across a range of parameter values. This paper should be weighted heavily when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar spatial PGG designs.

- source: Zhuang, Q., Wang, D., Fan, Y., & Di, Z. R. (2012). Evolution of cooperation in a heterogeneous population with influential individuals. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a spatial PGG with influential individuals, both punishment and reward mechanisms (administered by influential individuals) can promote cooperation and increase mean payoff compared to the baseline with no such interventions. Rewarding cooperators is more effective than punishing defectors in terms of both promoting cooperation and increasing mean payoff, especially at low multiplication factors (low MPCR). The cost of punishment or reward is paid by society, not by the influential individuals. The presence of influential individuals as mere exemplars (always cooperating, no sanctioning power) actually reduces cooperation and does not improve efficiency. The model demonstrates that, for a given intervention cost, reward leads to higher group efficiency than punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct simulation evidence that, in a spatial PGG with influential individuals, enabling punishment increases group efficiency (mean payoff) compared to the baseline with no punishment, but enabling reward increases efficiency even more. The effect of punishment on efficiency is positive, but smaller than that of reward, for a given cost. The findings are most relevant for settings with a small fraction of influential individuals, low MPCR, and where the cost of punishment/reward is paid by society. The results support the prediction that introducing punishment will increase efficiency relative to control, but that reward may be an even more effective intervention. The model is theoretical/simulation-based, so quantitative predictions should be interpreted with caution.

- source: Levine, D. K., & Modica, S. (2016). Peer discipline and incentives within groups. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment, when feasible and not prohibitively costly, enables groups to sustain full contribution to public goods and achieve high efficiency, even in large groups. The model provides conditions under which peer discipline is feasible and optimal, and shows that the efficiency gains from punishment depend on the cost and effectiveness of the punishment and audit technology. The model also shows that, in the absence of peer punishment, only small groups can achieve efficient outcomes, while large groups fail to contribute. The results are robust to various extensions, including renegotiation, spillover costs, and generalized auditing procedures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling peer punishment in public goods games increases efficiency, especially as group size increases, provided that punishment and audit costs are not too high. The model gives explicit formulas for group utility as a function of game parameters, including player count, marginal per-capita return, punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency (without punishment), enabling punishment will increase efficiency, particularly in larger groups and when the punishment technology is effective and not too costly. The model also highlights the importance of the cost structure of punishment and auditing, and the quality of monitoring signals, for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment.

- source: Hwang, S. H., & Bowles, S. (2012). Is altruism bad for cooperation?. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper models a public goods game with costly punishment, where individuals have both unconditional altruism and reciprocity motives. It finds that increasing unconditional altruism can reduce the willingness to punish free riders, which in turn lowers the threat of punishment and thus reduces equilibrium contributions and group surplus (efficiency) over a substantial range of parameter values. The negative effect of altruism on efficiency is larger when the reciprocity motive is strong or when the fraction of reciprocal altruists in the population is high. For very high levels of altruism, contributions and efficiency rise again, as altruism alone is sufficient to sustain cooperation. The model provides explicit equilibrium expressions for contributions and surplus, and characterizes the parameter ranges where altruism is 'bad for cooperation' and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the interplay between social preferences (altruism, reciprocity) and the punishment mechanism. Specifically, it shows that punishment can increase efficiency, but this effect is weakened or even reversed if group members are highly altruistic, because altruism reduces the willingness to punish defectors. The negative effect is amplified when the reciprocity motive is strong. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment is not monotonic in social preference parameters and may be lower (or even negative) in more altruistic or more reciprocal groups. The model also provides explicit formulas for equilibrium contributions and surplus as a function of game design parameters (player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.), which can inform quantitative prediction.

- source: Cong, R., Li, K., Wang, L., & Zhao, Q. C. (2016). Cooperation induced by wise incentive allocation in spontaneous institution. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with optional institution membership, the optimal regime for cooperation and group efficiency is achieved when the institution balances punishment and reward. Pure punishment or pure reward is suboptimal: too much punishment collapses the institution, while too much reward allows institutional defectors to dominate. The efficiency of punishment and reward (i.e., their impact per unit cost) increases the range of conditions under which cooperation prevails. The model provides explicit conditions for when institutional cooperators outcompete defectors, and shows that the stationary distribution of strategies (and thus group efficiency) is maximized at intermediate values of the punishment/reward split.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on the balance between punishment and reward, as well as the effectiveness of each. It suggests that enabling punishment alone may not always increase efficiency; the presence and strength of reward, and the cost-effectiveness of both, are crucial moderators. For prediction tasks, the model implies that intermediate punishment (with some reward) is optimal for efficiency, and that both the cost and impact of punishment and reward should be considered. The findings are directly relevant for settings where institutional incentives are endogenous and both punishment and reward are possible.

- source: Huang, S. T., Dong, Y. L., & Zhang, B. Y. (2024). THE OPTIMAL INCENTIVE IN PROMOTING COOPERATION: PUNISH THE WORST AND DO NOT ONLY REWARD THE BEST. *Singapore Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper provides a theoretical analysis of public goods games with institutional incentives, showing that: (1) Punishing only the lowest contributor (relative punishment with high selectivity) is the most effective way to sustain full cooperation and maximize group welfare for a given punishment budget. (2) Rewarding only the highest contributor is not effective; to promote cooperation, the reward must be distributed such that even lower contributors have a chance to receive it. (3) When both reward and punishment are available, group welfare is maximized by using as much reward as possible and only the minimal punishment needed to sustain full cooperation. (4) The paper gives explicit formulas for the minimum incentive size (punishment and/or reward) required to achieve full cooperation, and shows that, above this threshold, the cooperative Nash equilibrium is globally stable and group efficiency is maximized.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It provides explicit theoretical conditions for when punishment (and/or reward) will lead to full cooperation and maximal group welfare, as a function of the number of players, the marginal per-capita return, and the size and targeting of incentives. The results suggest that, for a given game design, introducing punishment targeted at the lowest contributor will sharply increase efficiency if the punishment is just above the critical threshold. The paper also clarifies that the efficiency gain from punishment is maximized when the punishment is minimal but sufficient, and that reward should be used as much as possible if available. These findings can be directly mapped to the prediction task, especially for settings where the control game (no punishment) has low efficiency and the treatment introduces targeted punishment.

- source: Yang, Z. H., & Yang, Y. L. (2024). Evolutionary dynamics of tax-based strong altruistic reward and punishment in a public goods game. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game, both strong altruistic punishment and reward are unable to sustain cooperation when implemented individually and funded only by voluntary contributions, due to their high cost. However, when a tax is imposed on all participants and the proceeds are used to fund either punishment or reward institutions, cooperation and group efficiency are greatly enhanced. The model shows that with sufficient tax compensation, strong altruistic punishers or rewarders can coexist with cooperators, eliminating defectors and increasing group welfare. Furthermore, tax-based strong altruistic reward leads to higher levels of cooperation and efficiency than tax-based strong altruistic punishment under otherwise identical conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that institutional (tax-based) punishment and reward mechanisms can substantially increase group efficiency in public goods games, compared to traditional voluntary punishment/reward or no-sanction conditions. The results suggest that, for a given set of game design parameters (e.g., player count, MPCR, cost/effectiveness of punishment/reward), introducing a tax-funded punishment or reward institution will increase the average efficiency of the group, with reward institutions being even more effective than punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to settings where punishment/reward is implemented institutionally and funded by mandatory contributions (taxes), and less so to peer-to-peer or voluntary sanctioning. The results are based on evolutionary game theory and numerical simulations, not empirical experiments.

- source: Yao, Y., & Chen, S. S. (2014). Multiplicative noise enhances spatial reciprocity. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game, both strong altruistic punishment and reward are unable to sustain cooperation when implemented in the traditional (non-tax-based) way, due to their high cost. When a tax-based mechanism is introduced—where all players pay a tax that is redistributed to punishers or rewarders—these strategies become viable, leading to stable coexistence of cooperators and punishers/rewarders and the elimination of defectors. This results in higher group efficiency and welfare. Tax-based strong altruistic reward leads to even higher cooperation and efficiency than tax-based strong altruistic punishment under equivalent tax conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the introduction of a tax-based institutional mechanism for funding punishment or reward can transform the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games from negative or neutral to strongly positive. The results suggest that, for a given control efficiency (without punishment), enabling tax-based punishment or reward will substantially increase efficiency, with reward being even more effective than punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with strong institutional support and compulsory tax collection, and less so to voluntary or peer-based punishment. The model is deterministic and assumes infinite, well-mixed populations, so predictions should be adjusted for finite or noisy environments.

- source: Sasaki, T. (2014). The Evolution of Cooperation Through Institutional Incentives and Optional Participation. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with optional participation, institutional punishment (penalties) is highly effective at promoting full cooperation and maximizing group welfare, even when the same level of punishment would only produce bi-stability (coordination problem) under compulsory participation. Optional participation allows the population to escape from the inefficient defection equilibrium, making cooperation globally stable for a wide range of punishment strengths. In contrast, institutional rewards combined with optional participation do not robustly promote cooperation or welfare: they often lead to non-participation and only marginally improve group welfare over the baseline. The model provides explicit analytical thresholds for when each regime occurs, and shows that the minimal punishment needed to achieve full cooperation decreases as group size increases.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public goods games with optional participation and institutional punishment, enabling punishment will substantially increase efficiency (group welfare) compared to the control (no punishment) condition. The effect is robust across a wide range of group sizes, rounds, and incentive strengths, provided the punishment is above a threshold that decreases with group size. The model also predicts that institutional rewards are much less effective in this context. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially when optional participation and institutional punishment are present. However, the results are theoretical and assume infinite populations and well-mixed groups, so empirical generalizability may be limited.

- source: Shen, Y., Yin, W. K., Kang, H. W., Zhang, H. G., & Wang, M. (2022). High-reputation individuals exert greater influence on cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper develops and analyzes a theoretical model of a public goods game with tax-based punishment and reward mechanisms. It shows that: (1) Tax-based rewards given directly to cooperators are more effective at promoting cooperation and group payoff than rewards given to punishers. (2) When rewards are used to support punishers, this can undermine the effectiveness of punishment and reduce overall cooperation and efficiency, as punishers and cooperators end up in competition. (3) The effect of punishment and reward on efficiency is highly sensitive to the way these mechanisms are implemented, with some parameter regimes leading to lower group payoff than the baseline. (4) Increasing taxes (used as rewards for cooperators) generally increases the proportion of cooperators and system efficiency, but excessive rewards to punishers can be detrimental.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games depends critically on the design of the reward and punishment mechanisms, especially whether rewards are given to cooperators or punishers. For prediction, it suggests that enabling punishment does not always increase efficiency: if rewards are used to support punishers, efficiency may decrease compared to control. If tax-based rewards are given to cooperators, efficiency is more likely to increase. The model also provides explicit payoff functions and equilibrium conditions that can be used to estimate efficiency under different parameterizations (e.g., player count, MPCR, punishment cost, reward structure). However, the results are theoretical and may not capture all behavioral nuances seen in experiments.

- source: Cui, P. B., Wu, Z. X., & Zhou, T. (2022). Small world can alleviate the social dilemmas originating from self-regulation or community policing issues. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the introduction of prosocial punishment in public goods games, especially when implemented on small-world networks, can substantially increase group efficiency (average payoff) by suppressing defectors and supporting the dominance of cooperative strategies. The effect is strongest when the network structure balances local clustering (which supports network reciprocity) and shortcuts (which allow punishers to reach defectors). Both the strength of punishment (fine) and the sensitivity of punishment implementation are important moderators. Regular lattices support cooperation but mainly favor cooperator-driven punishers, while random networks suppress punishment and cooperation. Small-world networks enable both high cooperation and high efficiency, with an optimal range of network randomness maximizing the effect. The model provides direct predictions for how efficiency changes as a function of punishment parameters and network structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games increases efficiency, especially when the game is played on small-world networks with appropriate parameter settings (moderate randomness, sufficient punishment fine, and intermediate feedback sensitivity). The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency (without punishment), introducing punishment will lead to a substantial increase in efficiency if the network structure allows for both local clustering and shortcuts. The results also indicate that the effect of punishment is moderated by the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), punishment cost, and the network topology. The paper's detailed mapping of efficiency and strategy frequencies across parameter spaces can be used to inform quantitative predictions of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions.

- source: Zhang, J. L., & Cao, M. (2020). Strategy Competition Dynamics of Multi-Agent Systems in the Framework of Evolutionary Game Theory. *Ieee Transactions on Circuits and Systems Ii-express Briefs*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with punishment, insurance (speculation), and loner options, the evolutionary outcome (and thus group efficiency) depends on the relationship between the punishment fine (alpha), insurance cost (lambda), group size (N), and the public goods multiplier (r). If punishment is strong enough (i.e., r > 2 - 2*alpha and 1 - r/N - alpha < 0), full cooperation is the only stable equilibrium, leading to maximal group efficiency. If punishment is weaker, or if insurance is cheaper than the expected fine, defection or speculation can dominate, reducing group efficiency. The presence of loners can also destabilize cooperation, leading to cycles or dominance of non-participation. The model provides explicit conditions for when each regime occurs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, given the game design parameters. It shows that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting efficiency depends on the size of the punishment fine relative to the public goods multiplier and group size. If the punishment is strong enough (alpha high, r high, N not too large), enabling punishment can shift the system from low-efficiency (defection or cycles) to high-efficiency (full cooperation). However, if punishment is weak or can be circumvented by cheap insurance (speculation), or if the loner option is attractive, punishment may not improve efficiency and may even be ineffective. The model is most informative for settings matching the theoretical assumptions (well-mixed, large populations, no repeated encounters, continuous strategy evolution).

- source: Sui, X. K., Wu, B., & Wang, L. (2018). Rationality alters the rank between peer punishment and social exclusion. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the evolutionary advantage (interpreted as efficiency in promoting cooperation and group payoff) of peer punishment versus social exclusion in PGGs is not fixed, but depends on the intensity of selection (rationality), the cost and effectiveness of punishment and exclusion, and whether participation is compulsory or voluntary. Under weak selection, peer punishment can be favored if it is efficient or exclusion is costly. Under strong selection, social exclusion with low cost dominates. The presence of loners (voluntary participation) does not change the condition for which punishment is favored, but reduces the difference in their abundances. The rank between peer punishment and social exclusion can change as rationality changes, and this sensitivity is mapped for a range of parameter values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in PGGs, especially regarding how the cost and effectiveness of punishment and exclusion, group size, and rationality (selection intensity) interact. It shows that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency is not uniform: it can be positive or negative depending on whether punishment is efficient and/or exclusion is costly, and on the rationality of agents. The results are most directly applicable to well-mixed, theoretical PGGs with the specified parameters. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the efficiency effect of punishment is conditional on the cost/benefit structure and the rationality of players, and that the presence of loners (voluntary participation) does not fundamentally alter the ranking condition but can affect the magnitude of effects.

- source: Wang, Z., Xu, Z. J., Huang, J. H., & Zhang, L. Z. (2010). Maintenance of cooperation induced by punishment in public goods games. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a punishment mechanism into the classical public goods game prevents the collapse of cooperation and increases average payoffs for both cooperators and defectors, compared to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is present for both perfectly rational and boundedly rational agents. The degree of rationality (K) also modulates the equilibrium, but punishment always leads to higher system payoffs and more cooperation than the control. The results are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation evidence that, in a standard PGG with fixed group size (N=6), a punishment mechanism (with specified cost and fine) increases average payoffs (i.e., efficiency) compared to the no-punishment baseline. The effect is robust to changes in the rationality parameter K. The findings support the prediction that enabling punishment in a similar game design will increase efficiency, especially when the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low due to defection. The specific parameterization (N=6, r=3.6, c=1.25, punishment fine and cost) is informative for quantitative prediction.

- source: Zhang, B. J., Cui, Z. G., & Yue, X. H. (2019). Cluster evolution in public goods game with fairness mechanism. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a well-mixed PGG, adding a fairness mechanism and peer punishment (with costs shared among cooperators) can achieve full cooperation and maximum efficiency in small clusters. In larger clusters, full cooperation is difficult, but increasing the fairness factor (weight on fairness utility) and decreasing the cost of punishment both increase the equilibrium number of cooperators and group utility. The model provides explicit formulas for the utilities of cooperators and defectors with and without punishment, and shows that punishment is only effective (i.e., leads to higher group efficiency) when the number of cooperators exceeds a threshold. Simulation results confirm that lower punishment cost and higher fairness factor lead to higher group efficiency and larger stable clusters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in PGGs with peer punishment, efficiency (group payoff relative to the cooperative optimum) increases when punishment is enabled, especially when punishment cost is low and the fairness factor is high. The model allows explicit calculation of group efficiency under different parameter settings, including player count, punishment cost, and fairness factor. For the prediction task, the paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, with the effect size moderated by punishment cost and the fairness factor. The results are most directly applicable to well-mixed, continuous-contribution PGGs with no communication, and where punishment costs are shared among cooperators.

- source: Eldakar, O. T., Gallup, A. C., & Driscoll, W. W. (2013). WHEN HAWKS GIVE RISE TO DOVES: THE EVOLUTION AND TRANSITION OF ENFORCEMENT STRATEGIES. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can increase group efficiency (fitness) in public goods game environments, but the pathway depends on the type of punisher. Selfish punishers can invade selfish populations and reduce cheating, thereby increasing group payoff and paving the way for altruistic punishers, who further increase group efficiency. The effectiveness of punishment in raising group payoff is higher when punishment costs are low, group size is small, and the number of rounds (iterations) is high. The model directly analyzes how these design dimensions affect the ability of punishment to increase group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment in a public goods game can increase group efficiency, especially when punishment costs are low, group size is small, and the number of rounds is high. The model is directly based on a standard PGG and manipulates key design dimensions, making its findings highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that the efficiency gain from punishment is larger when punishment is less costly and when group structure allows for stronger group selection (smaller groups, longer association). The findings are most applicable to settings where punishment leads to exclusion or strong consequences for defectors.

- source: Sui, X. K., Wu, B., & Wang, L. (2017). Multiple tolerances dilute the second order cooperative dilemma. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper provides a unified theoretical framework for public goods games with multiple tolerant punishment strategies. It shows that: (1) Less tolerant punishers (those who punish with fewer defectors present) are always more abundant and thus more evolutionarily successful than more tolerant ones. (2) The presence of punishment strategies increases group efficiency (measured by the abundance of cooperation and punishers) when the fine for defectors exceeds a threshold determined by group size, enhancement factor, and punishment cost. (3) Larger group sizes can facilitate the evolution of punishment and thus higher group efficiency, but only when the enhancement factor and punishment cost are small. (4) The paper provides explicit analytical conditions for when punishment prevails, and these are validated by simulations. (5) The main outcome is the stationary abundance of strategies, which is directly linked to average payoffs and group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. It provides explicit analytical conditions for when punishment increases the abundance of cooperation and group efficiency, as a function of group size, enhancement factor (mpcr), punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness. The results show that punishment can increase efficiency, especially in larger groups when costs are low, and that less tolerant (more responsive) punishment is more effective. The findings can be used to predict the efficiency of games with and without punishment, given the game design parameters. However, the results are derived under evolutionary dynamics with weak selection and large populations, so care should be taken when applying them to small groups or different update rules.

- source: Sigmund, K., Hauert, C., Traulsen, A., & De Silva, H. (2011). Social Control and the Social Contract: The Emergence of Sanctioning Systems for Collective Action. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Punishment (peer or pool) can stabilize cooperation and increase group efficiency in public goods games, especially when participation is voluntary. (2) Pool punishment with second-order punishment is particularly effective, leading to stable, high-efficiency cooperative regimes. (3) Peer punishment can also support cooperation, but is less robust and can be undermined by second-order free riders. (4) Compulsory participation (no option to abstain) undermines the effectiveness of punishment, resulting in dominance by defectors and low efficiency. (5) The costs of punishment (especially pool punishment) can reduce efficiency if not balanced by increased cooperation, but with sufficient rounds or strong imitation, efficiency gains are realized. (6) The structure of the punishment mechanism (peer vs. pool, presence of second-order punishment) and the option for voluntary participation are key moderators of the efficiency effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games increases efficiency, especially when participation is voluntary and when pool punishment with second-order punishment is available. The model shows that the design dimensions—such as player count, number of rounds, marginal per-capita return, punishment cost and effectiveness, and whether participation is voluntary—critically shape the efficiency outcome. In particular, pool punishment with second-order punishment and voluntary participation are predicted to yield the highest efficiency. Peer punishment can also increase efficiency, but is less stable and more vulnerable to free-riding. If the control game (no punishment) has low efficiency, enabling punishment (especially with the above features) is likely to substantially increase efficiency. However, if punishment is costly and not well-targeted, or if participation is compulsory, efficiency gains may be limited or negative.

- source: Wang, Q., Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2020). Evolutionary Dynamics of Cooperation in the Public Goods Game with Individual Disguise and Peer Punishment. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment increases cooperation and group payoff (efficiency) in public goods games, but this effect is undermined if defectors can cheaply disguise themselves to avoid punishment. When the cost of disguise is high, or when punishment is strong (high fine, low cost), punishment is effective at promoting cooperation and high group payoff. Second-order punishment further stabilizes cooperation and punishment strategies, even in the presence of disguise. In finite populations, the stationary distribution of strategies shows that low disguise cost leads to defection (low efficiency), while high disguise cost or strong punishment leads to cooperation or punishment (high efficiency). Thus, the efficiency gain from punishment depends on the interplay between punishment parameters and the cost/effectiveness of disguise.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends on the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the fine imposed, and the possibility/cost of disguise by defectors. If the control game (no punishment) has low efficiency due to high defection, enabling punishment will increase efficiency only if punishment is strong enough and disguise is not too cheap. If disguise is cheap, punishment may have little or no effect on efficiency. The model also shows that second-order punishment can further increase efficiency by stabilizing punishment strategies. For prediction, the paper supports that lower punishment cost, higher punishment effectiveness, and higher fine on defectors all increase the efficiency gain from punishment, but this is moderated (and can be negated) by the ability of defectors to disguise at low cost.

- source: Dejong, S., Tuyls, K., & Verbeeck, K. (2008). Fairness in multi-agent systems. *Knowledge Engineering Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games and similar social dilemmas, the introduction of punishment (even costly, altruistic punishment) reliably increases cooperation and group payoff, moving outcomes closer to the social optimum. Theoretical and computational models (e.g., Homo Egualis utility, learning automata) and empirical studies are reviewed, all supporting the claim that punishment transforms the equilibrium from inefficient defection to more efficient cooperation. The paper also notes that while punishment increases efficiency, it is not always evolutionarily stable due to the possibility of second-order free-riders. Additional mechanisms such as reputation, volunteering, and network structure can further support cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and review-based support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games and similar environments will increase group efficiency (payoff relative to the cooperative optimum), compared to control games without punishment. The effect is robust across a range of game structures, player counts, and repeated interactions, as long as punishment is possible and salient. The paper also highlights that the magnitude of the effect may depend on the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the presence of reputation mechanisms, and the network structure of interactions. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when compared to the Nash equilibrium of defection in the absence of punishment.

- source: Wang, S. X., Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Tax-based pure punishment and reward in the public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both decentralized punishment and reward can drive a networked public goods game to high cooperation, but the minimal cumulative cost required depends on the initial cooperation level. If the initial fraction of cooperators is low, reward is more cost-effective; if it is high, punishment is more cost-effective. The study provides analytical and simulation results for the optimal incentive protocols and the conditions under which full cooperation is stable. The main outcome is the cumulative cost of incentives needed to reach a target cooperation level, not group efficiency or total payoff per se.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in networked public goods games. While it does not report group efficiency directly, it analyzes the cost (incentives spent) required to achieve high cooperation, which is closely related to efficiency when incentive costs are subtracted from group payoffs. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of punishment (in terms of cost to achieve cooperation) depends on initial conditions and the structure of the game (network, group size, synergy factor). For prediction tasks, the results imply that punishment is not always the most efficient intervention; its relative efficiency compared to reward depends on the starting state of the group. The detailed modeling of incentive cost and cooperation dynamics can inform parameterizations of efficiency in similar networked PGG environments.

- source: Perc, M. (2016). Phase transitions in models of human cooperation. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment into spatial public goods games can enable the survival and dominance of cooperative strategies under harsher conditions (lower multiplication factors) than in the absence of punishment. The effectiveness of punishment depends on the fine and cost parameters, with phase transitions (sometimes discontinuous) between different strategy compositions. The addition of reward and the interplay of positive and negative reciprocity further enrich the dynamics, leading to complex patterns such as cyclic dominance. However, the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes; instead, it focuses on the prevalence of cooperation and the conditions under which cooperation can persist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that punishment can promote cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when the punishment fine is sufficiently large and the cost is not prohibitive. The models specify how game design parameters (e.g., group size, multiplication factor, punishment fine and cost) affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, because the main outcomes are strategy frequencies and phase transitions rather than explicit efficiency or group payoff, the paper offers only indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. It is most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of punishment-related parameters and for mapping the conditions under which cooperation (and thus higher efficiency) is likely to emerge, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data for direct prediction.

- source: Baker, F., & Rachlin, H. (2002). Teaching and learning in a probabilistic prisoner's dilemma. *Behavioural Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment or reward mechanisms—whether applied individually (peer-to-peer) or collectively (by group consensus)—substantially increases contributions in public goods games. The effect is stronger as the penalty/reward size or effectiveness increases, and as the proportion of punishers/rewarders in the population rises. Both mechanisms are effective, but punishment is found to be marginally more effective than reward in promoting contributions. The model also shows that collective mechanisms (pool punishment/reward) can be more effective than individual ones under certain parameter regimes. Although efficiency is not directly measured, the payoff structure implies that higher contributions lead to higher group payoffs, except for the initial cost to punishers/rewarders. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and network structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public goods game will increase group contributions and, by implication, group efficiency/payoff, relative to a control without punishment. The model explores a wide range of parameter values for punishment cost, effectiveness, and the proportion of punishers, showing that higher punishment effectiveness and more punishers lead to higher contributions. While the paper does not report efficiency as a ratio, the payoff equations and discussion make it clear that group payoff increases with contribution. The results are most directly applicable to networked, repeated PGGs with binary (cooperate/defect) choices and both individual and collective punishment/reward options. The findings should inform predictions that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment will yield higher efficiency, especially when punishment is effective and not prohibitively costly.

- source: Sun, X. P., Bi, Y. Z., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). Effect of distribution of fines on evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Redistributing fines from defectors to cooperators and punishers in a spatial public goods game generally increases the persistence of cooperation and raises the total system payoff, especially when punishment costs are high. When punishment costs are low, the standard pool punishment model (without redistribution) can achieve higher cooperation and higher total payoff, as punishers dominate and defectors are eliminated. The effect of fine redistribution is thus mixed: it is beneficial for efficiency and cooperation under high punishment cost, but can reduce efficiency and cooperation when punishment cost is low. The distribution parameter (alpha) also matters: allocating more fines to punishers (lower alpha) increases cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends on the cost of punishment and the mechanism for handling fines. If fines are redistributed to cooperators and punishers, efficiency losses from punishment can be mitigated, especially when punishment is costly. However, in settings where punishment is cheap, standard punishment (without redistribution) may yield higher efficiency by allowing punishers to eliminate defectors. For prediction, the presence or absence of fine redistribution, the punishment cost, and the fine distribution parameter (alpha) are important moderators. The results suggest that enabling fine redistribution is likely to increase efficiency when punishment is expensive, but may not always do so when punishment is cheap.

- source: Chen, X. J., Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2014). Probabilistic sharing solves the problem of costly punishment. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that probabilistic sharing of punishment duties in a public goods game can solve the problem of costly punishment and promote cooperation, even when punishment is expensive. In well-mixed populations, probabilistic sanctioning transforms the game into a coordination game with two stable equilibria (full cooperation and full defection). In structured populations, sharing punishment duties leads to high cooperation rates, but only if the severity and frequency of punishment are carefully balanced; both too little and too much punishment can reduce cooperation. The main reported outcome is the stationary fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. Theoretical payoff expressions are provided, and the analysis explains why intermediate punishment is optimal for cooperation, but efficiency is not directly measured or reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the way punishment is implemented (shared probabilistically or periodically) can have a major impact on cooperation rates in public goods games. While the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency, the derived payoff expressions and analysis of invasion dynamics suggest that intermediate levels of punishment severity and frequency can maximize group welfare by supporting high cooperation at lower cost. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that both the cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the structure of the population and the sharing of punishment duties, are important moderators of the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, because efficiency is not directly reported, quantitative predictions about efficiency must be inferred indirectly from the cooperation rates and payoff formulas.

- source: Perc, M., & Szolnoki, A. (2012). Self-organization of punishment in structured populations. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Adaptive punishment in spatial public goods games enables the spontaneous emergence of rare, localized punishers who respond to the spread of defection. This mechanism maintains smooth interfaces between cooperators and defectors, invigorates spatial reciprocity, and prevents the emergence of cyclic dominance that would otherwise allow defectors to persist. As a result, adaptive punishment can achieve full cooperation (pure C phase) in parameter regions where, without punishment, only defectors would survive. The cost of punishment, when adaptively deployed, is globally negligible, making the mechanism highly efficient. Compared to steady (non-adaptive) punishment, adaptive punishment is more effective at eliminating defectors and does so with lower cost.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that adaptive, context-sensitive punishment mechanisms in spatially structured public goods games can substantially increase the likelihood of full cooperation and, by implication, group efficiency, even when punishment is costly. The effect is especially pronounced when punishment is inexpensive, but remains positive even for costly punishment. The findings suggest that models or interventions that allow for adaptive, rather than fixed, punishment are likely to yield higher efficiency than those with no punishment or with steady punishment. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, so predictions should be based on the qualitative pattern that adaptive punishment expands the parameter space for high efficiency.

- source: Quan, J., Chu, Y. Q., Liu, W., Wang, X. J., & Yang, X. K. (2018). Stochastic evolutionary public goods game with first and second order costly punishments in finite populations. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing first-order costly punishment into a finite-population public goods game can shift the system from an all-defection equilibrium to a coexistence of cooperation and punishment, provided that both the punishment intensity and the return on investment are large enough. This shift implies higher group welfare and efficiency, as the all-defection state is inefficient. Second-order punishment (punishing cooperators who do not punish defectors) does not dramatically change the evolutionary dynamics except in certain critical parameter combinations, and mainly increases the proportion of punishers among cooperators in coexistence states. The model provides a detailed mapping of parameter regions where cooperation is stochastically stable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in finite-population public goods games, enabling costly punishment (with sufficient intensity and return on investment) can substantially increase the probability of the system being in cooperative or mixed cooperative-punisher states, which are more efficient than all-defection. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and strength of punishment, as well as the return on investment (mpcr), are key moderators of the efficiency gain from punishment. However, the paper does not report direct efficiency ratios, so predictions must be inferred from the stability of cooperative states. Second-order punishment adds little except in narrow parameter regions. The results are most applicable to settings matching the model's assumptions (finite, well-mixed populations, no communication, discrete strategies).

- source: Lv, S. J., Zhang, X. X., Zhong, A. Y., Mi, J., & He, Q. L. (2022). Synergy and discount of punishment in the public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that nonlinear punishment (synergy or discount) significantly affects the evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in public goods games. In well-mixed populations, cooperation cannot be the unique stable equilibrium; both cooperation and defection can be stable depending on parameters and initial conditions. In structured populations (regular networks), cooperation can become the unique stable equilibrium under certain conditions. The effectiveness of punishment (synergy parameter), group size, network degree, and punishment severity all interact to determine whether cooperation is promoted. Synergistic punishment (increasing effectiveness with more punishers) can enlarge the domain of attraction for cooperation, especially in structured populations. The impact of network degree (group size) is non-monotonic and depends on the level of punishment severity. The paper provides explicit replicator equations and phase diagrams but does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how nonlinear punishment mechanisms, group size, network structure, and punishment severity interact to affect the evolutionary stability of cooperation in public goods games. While it does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it shows that synergistic punishment and certain network structures can make cooperation more stable, which would likely increase group efficiency in practice. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of synergistic punishment, lower punishment costs, and structured populations (as opposed to well-mixed) are likely to increase the efficiency gains from enabling punishment. However, the lack of explicit efficiency or payoff data limits direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Xia, C. Y., Zhang, J. J., Wang, Y. L., & Wang, J. S. (2011). Evolution of Cooperation in Public Goods Games. *Communications in Theoretical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates, through theoretical modeling and numerical simulation, that adding punishment to voluntary public goods games in finite populations with bounded rationality increases both the frequency of cooperation and the average payoff of the group. The effect of punishment is especially strong when the loner's payoff is not too high, and the model shows that punishment can suppress defectors and promote higher payoffs for cooperators and punishers. The results are compared to both compulsory PGG and VPGG without punishment, showing that the addition of punishment leads to higher cooperation and average payoffs. The findings are robust to variations in group size, rationality parameter, and loner's payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in public goods games with voluntary participation, enabling punishment mechanisms increases average group payoff (i.e., efficiency) compared to the same game without punishment, especially when the loner's payoff is not too high. The model explicitly includes key design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds (implicitly infinite or repeated), punishment cost and effectiveness, and the presence of loners. The results suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment should be expected to increase efficiency, with the effect moderated by the value of the loner's payoff and the rationality parameter. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with voluntary participation, finite groups, and bounded rationality.

- source: Li, Y. X., & Shen, B. (2013). The coevolution of partner switching and strategy updating in non-excludable public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in non-excludable public goods games, the ability to switch partners asymmetrically (i.e., only being able to sever outgoing ties) reduces the equilibrium level of cooperation compared to excludable (symmetric) PGGs. The addition of costly punishment, where cooperators pay a cost to reduce defectors' payoffs, substantially increases the equilibrium fraction of cooperators. The effect of punishment intensity (amplification factor) is also positive for cooperation. However, all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that costly punishment increases cooperation rates in non-excludable PGGs with dynamic networks, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates typically imply higher efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this relationship or report actual payoffs. The model details (e.g., group size, network structure, punishment cost and effectiveness) are described, but the absence of payoff-based outcomes limits direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Fu, M. J. (2020). A punishment mechanism in the spatial public goods game with continuous strategies. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game with continuous strategies, punishing the minority of lowest contributors (i.e., when the number of punished individuals is less than half the group) increases the average contribution level, while punishing the majority decreases it. Increasing the punishment fine increases cooperation when the punished are a minority, but decreases it when the punished are a majority. The main reported outcome is the average contribution (cooperation level), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment design (number punished, fine size, cost structure) affects average contribution in a spatial continuous PGG. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that punishment can backfire if the cost to punishers is too high relative to the fine, and that targeting a minority of low contributors is more effective for increasing cooperation. These insights may inform the qualitative direction of efficiency effects in similar designs, but quantitative prediction is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Helbing, D., Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabó, G. (2010). Punish, but not too hard: how costly punishment spreads in the spatial public goods game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games, the introduction of punishing cooperators (PC) or punishing defectors (PD) can promote the spread of cooperation, but through different mechanisms. For punishing cooperators, increasing the punishment fine monotonically increases the cooperation level up to a threshold, after which non-punishing cooperators and defectors are eliminated. For punishing defectors, there is a non-monotonic (reentrant) effect: increasing the fine initially increases cooperation, but too high a fine reduces the fraction of punishing defectors, causing cooperation to collapse and the system to revert to defection. The results are presented as phase diagrams and time-evolution of strategy fractions, not as direct efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and type of punishment mechanism, as well as the punishment cost and fine, can have large effects on the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. However, because the outcomes are reported as fractions of strategies and cooperation level, not as group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that enabling punishment (especially by punishing cooperators) can increase cooperation and likely efficiency, but the relationship is mediated by the spatial structure and the specific punishment parameters. The non-monotonic effect for punishing defectors highlights the importance of tuning punishment severity. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment can improve efficiency in structured populations, but the mapping to efficiency must be inferred from cooperation levels.

- source: Kang, H. W., Xu, Y. H., Chen, Q. Y., Li, Z. K., Shen, Y., & Sun, X. P. (2024). The role of reputation to reduce punishment costs in spatial public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a reputation-based punishment mechanism in a spatial public goods game model allows punishers to reduce their punishment costs as their reputation increases. Simulation results show that this mechanism enables the system to achieve global cooperation and expel betrayers even at lower synergy factors, where traditional punishment fails. The model demonstrates that clusters of punishers with high reputation can resist and eliminate betrayers more effectively, and that parameter ranges (synergy factor, punishment cost, fine) determine the system's ability to reach cooperation. However, the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes; instead, it focuses on the prevalence of strategies and the system's convergence to cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that reducing punishment costs via a reputation mechanism can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when the marginal per-capita return (synergy factor) is low. For the downstream prediction task, the findings suggest that punishment mechanisms with lower or dynamically reduced costs (e.g., via reputation) are likely to yield higher efficiency than traditional, high-cost punishment, particularly in low-MPCR environments. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The results are most useful for understanding the qualitative impact of punishment cost and reputation mechanisms on cooperation dynamics.

- source: Nakamura, G. M., Contesini, G. S., & Martinez, A. S. (2019). Cooperation risk and Nash equilibrium: Quantitative description for realistic players. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment into public goods games reduces the risk associated with cooperation, which can promote cooperative behavior among players. However, punishment also reduces the absolute payoffs for all players, and its effect on cooperation depends on the cost-to-benefit ratio and the punishment parameter. There are critical thresholds (phase transitions) where small changes in parameters can shift the system from a cooperative to a non-cooperative regime. The main reported outcome is the density of cooperators, not efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment can alter cooperation rates in public goods games, especially through the lens of cooperation risk and phase transitions. However, it does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff, which are the primary outcomes for the downstream prediction task. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation rates under certain parameter regimes, but may also reduce payoffs and, in some cases, accelerate defection. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides only indirect, mechanistic insight rather than quantitative payoff outcomes.

  findings: The study finds that tolerance-based punishment in a continuous public goods game can promote higher average investment (cooperation) when the punishment fine is greater than the cost and when tolerance is moderate. Too low tolerance leads to low cooperation, while too high tolerance reduces the effectiveness of punishment. The coevolution of investment and tolerance leads to a dynamic equilibrium between high-tolerance/high-investment and low-tolerance/low-investment individuals. The results are robust to mutation and to changes in the enhancement factor (mpcr). However, the paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are limited to behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation (average investment) in continuous public goods games, especially when the punishment fine is greater than the cost and when tolerance is moderate. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that, in similar spatial continuous PGGs, enabling punishment with appropriate cost/fine ratios and moderate tolerance is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated.

- source: Allgaier, K., Scigala, K. A., Trautwein, U., Hilbig, B. E., & Zettler, I. (2020). Honesty-humility and dictator and ultimatum game-giving in children. *Journal of Research in Personality*.

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game with punishment (both pool and peer), time-dependent effects such as oscillations in the enhancement factor (r) and delays in punishment delivery reduce the prevalence of cooperation and increase defection. Peer punishment is more effective than pool punishment at promoting cooperation, but both are less effective when r oscillates with high amplitude or when punishment is delayed. The main outcomes are the frequencies of cooperators, defectors, and punishers, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that instability in game parameters and delays in sanctioning undermine the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It confirms that punishment can promote cooperation in public goods games, especially when punishment is immediate and the enhancement factor is stable. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that in environments with time-varying productivity or delayed punishment, the positive effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, likely on efficiency) is weakened. For prediction, this paper supports the idea that punishment is less effective in unstable or delayed settings, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Moreno, S. F. G. Y., & Gutierrez-Garcia, J. O. (2018). Collective action in organizational structures. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
