  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of hierarchical organizations and the design of punishment mechanisms significantly affect collective action, as measured by aggregate contribution levels. Peer-based (collegial) punishment mechanisms lead to higher contributions than supervisor-based punishment. Organizations with more hierarchical levels achieve higher contributions when supervisor-based punishment is used. Smaller organizational units (lower span of control) also foster higher contributions, especially when only the most egoistic agent is punished. The presence of any punishment mechanism increases contributions compared to no punishment. However, all results are reported in terms of contribution rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms (peer vs. supervisor), the number of hierarchical levels, and the span of control can substantially affect contribution rates in repeated public goods games. However, because the outcomes are reported as contribution levels rather than efficiency or group payoff, the direct predictive value for efficiency is limited. The findings suggest that, in similar agent-based or organizational settings, enabling peer punishment and structuring organizations into smaller units with more levels may increase contributions, which could correlate with higher efficiency if punishment costs are not excessive. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the net efficiency impact, especially considering the costs of punishment.

- source: Xiao, J. F., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2023). Evolution of cooperation driven by sampling punishment. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that sampling punishment can dramatically increase the average cooperation level in both linear (PGG) and nonlinear (CRD) social dilemmas, provided the punishment intensity is above a threshold. Low punishment thresholds and small sample sizes are optimal, as they maximize cooperation and minimize punishment cost. The effect of punishment intensity is nonlinear: below a threshold, it is ineffective, but above the threshold, further increases yield little additional benefit. These findings are robust across both PGG and CRD models. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional punishment mechanisms—specifically, sampling punishment—can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, especially when punishment is sufficiently intense, the threshold for triggering punishment is low, and the sample size is small. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. If the downstream prediction task assumes a strong link between cooperation and efficiency, these results may be indirectly informative, but caution is warranted.

  findings: The study finds that a switching strategy—punishing most defectors and expelling a few—can significantly increase the level of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the cost of punishment, the threshold for switching between punishment and expulsion, and the population density. Lower punishment costs, lower switching thresholds, and intermediate population densities maximize cooperation rates. The model demonstrates that combining expulsion and punishment is more effective at promoting cooperation than either strategy alone. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment (and expulsion) mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, and that the effect depends on design parameters such as punishment cost, threshold, and population density. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that, under certain conditions, punishment and expulsion can increase cooperation, which may correlate with higher efficiency, but this is not demonstrated or quantified in the paper.

- source: Wang, J., Wu, B., Ho, D. W. C., & Wang, L. (2011). Evolution of cooperation in multilevel public goods games with community structures. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that in multilevel PGGs with community structure, the prevalence of cooperation and punishment depends on the strengths of imitation within and between communities. When within-community imitation is weak or between-community imitation is strong, cooperation and punishment are more abundant than defection. If both imitation strengths are strong, defection dominates. The model also finds that, under moderate within-community imitation and small between-community imitation, punishers can become the most abundant strategy while cooperators nearly disappear. The analysis is based on the stationary distribution of homogeneous states (all-cooperator, all-defector, all-punisher) and fixation probabilities, not on direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how punishment and cooperation can be sustained in multilevel PGGs with community structure, depending on imitation dynamics. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff with and without punishment. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that punishment can increase the prevalence of cooperation under certain imitation regimes, which may imply higher efficiency, but this is not directly modeled or quantified. The results are most useful for understanding the conditions under which punishment and cooperation are likely to be sustained, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Li, K., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2016). Cooperation induced by random sequential exclusion. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that the random sequential exclusion (R) mechanism for social exclusion in public goods games can sustain higher levels of cooperation (measured as the stationary frequency of cooperators and excluders) than the standard peer exclusion (P) mechanism, especially under adverse conditions such as high exclusion cost, low exclusion success probability, large group size, and low synergy factor. The advantage of the R mechanism is lost if the scheduling cost for coordination among excluders becomes too high. The results hold in both well-mixed and spatially structured populations. However, the paper does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff, so the findings are about the prevalence of cooperative strategies rather than explicit welfare or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that more efficient or coordinated punishment mechanisms (like random sequential exclusion) can increase the prevalence of cooperative strategies in public goods games, especially when punishment costs are high or group size is large. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it suggests that under certain design dimensions (low scheduling cost, high group size, high exclusion cost), mechanisms that reduce redundant punishment may be more effective at sustaining cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the structure and cost of punishment mechanisms are important moderators, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

  findings: The paper finds that in optional public goods games with punishment, the evolutionary process can converge to two types of equilibria depending on initial conditions and the magnitude of rationality: (1) an equilibrium with only punishers and cooperators, and (2) an equilibrium without punishers. The presence of punishers is vital for the evolution and stability of cooperation. The model also shows that bounded rationality leads to more stable evolutionary outcomes compared to previous models. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment can stabilize cooperation in optional public goods games, especially under bounded rationality. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions or direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that punishment can sustain cooperation, which may imply higher efficiency, but does not quantify this effect or report efficiency outcomes. The model's parameters (e.g., group size, punishment cost, effectiveness) are specified, but efficiency is not analyzed as a dependent variable.

- source: Lazarus, J. (2023). Cooperation and social policy: integrating evidence into practice - Introduction to the Issue. *Global Discourse*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper synthesizes evidence from experimental and real-world studies showing that a variety of factors—including financial incentives, reciprocity, fairness, reputation, group identification, and social norms—can increase cooperation in public goods games and similar social dilemmas. Punishment of free-riding and reward of cooperation are identified as effective mechanisms for increasing cooperation, especially when they are costly to the punisher/rewarder and when implemented by long-term partners. The review also notes that communication, group identity, and information about others' behavior can further enhance cooperation. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or qualitative findings on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a high-level theoretical synthesis of factors that promote cooperation in public goods games, including the role of punishment. It is useful for identifying which game design dimensions are likely to influence cooperation rates, but it does not provide direct evidence or predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation, especially under conditions of group identification, communication, and when punishment is costly to the punisher. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff analysis limits its value for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design dimensions.

- source: Sylwester, K., Hellmann, B., & Bryson, J. J. (2013). Homo Homini Lupus? Explaining Antisocial Punishment. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews and interprets evidence that antisocial punishment—punishing cooperators at a cost to oneself—is a widespread phenomenon in public goods games, especially in certain cultural and ecological contexts. It argues that antisocial punishment is often driven by status competition, aggression, or negative reciprocity, and is more prevalent where the cost of punishment is low, legal institutions are weak, and social competition is high. The review notes that antisocial punishment can undermine the cooperation-enhancing effects of punishment, and that its prevalence varies widely across cultures. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative evidence or predictions about efficiency or group payoff changes due to punishment; instead, it focuses on behavioral mechanisms and cross-cultural patterns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical and contextual background for understanding when and why punishment may fail to increase efficiency in public goods games, especially due to antisocial punishment. It highlights that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly context-dependent and can be negative in environments with high antisocial punishment. However, because the paper is a review and does not report new or meta-analyzed efficiency or payoff data, it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. It is most useful for identifying moderators (e.g., punishment cost, anonymity, cultural context) that should be considered when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, and for warning that punishment can sometimes reduce, rather than increase, group welfare.

- source: Wang, S. X., Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Incentive strategies for the evolution of cooperation: Analysis and optimization. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment, reward, exclusion, or adaptive combinations thereof can stabilize or increase cooperation in public goods games, but the implementation of these strategies is costly and can create second-order dilemmas. Theoretical models show that optimal (time-varying) incentive protocols can minimize the cumulative cost required to reach a desired cooperation level. Punishment is generally more cost-effective than reward for achieving high cooperation. Adaptive hybrid strategies (first carrot, then stick) can outperform pure punishment or reward. The paper also highlights that the structure of the population and the design of the incentive protocol (e.g., thresholds for switching between punishment and exclusion) are important moderators. However, the main outcomes are cooperation rates and incentive costs, not group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how different incentive strategies (punishment, reward, exclusion, and hybrids) affect cooperation rates and the cost of achieving cooperation in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment can promote cooperation, and for predicting how design features (such as the presence of punishment, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the use of hybrid strategies) might influence cooperation rates. However, because the primary outcomes are cooperation rates and incentive costs, not group efficiency or total payoff, the paper is only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The models suggest that punishment can increase cooperation and that cost-effective protocols exist, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions of efficiency changes.

  findings: The paper argues that the human tendency to punish non-cooperators, even at a personal cost, is rooted in evolutionary and neural mechanisms. It reviews evidence from public goods games and other economic experiments showing that punishment can increase cooperation and, in some cases, average group profit. The author discusses the adaptive value of punishment, the neural reward mechanisms involved, and the importance of institutionalizing punishment to maintain social order and cooperation. The paper also highlights complexities and limitations, such as the costliness of punishment, the potential for retaliation, and the need for centralized enforcement in large societies.

For the prediction task, this paper supports the general mechanism by which punishment can improve efficiency, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence or dimension-specific effect sizes. However, because it does not report new empirical data or direct efficiency outcomes, it should be used as contextual and mechanistic support rather than as a source of quantitative effect sizes for efficiency prediction.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2012). Evolutionary advantages of adaptive rewarding. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Adaptive rewarding in spatial public goods games can eliminate defectors and promote high levels of cooperation, outperforming steady (non-adaptive) rewarding. The effectiveness of adaptive rewarding depends on the synergy factor (r), the cost of rewarding (alpha), and the strength of the reward (Delta). When rewards are strong and not too costly, adaptive rewarding can lead to defector-free states, interpreted as maximal efficiency. However, adaptive punishment is even more effective: for the same cost, punishment achieves defector elimination at lower required intensity. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams showing under what parameter regimes each strategy dominates, coexists, or is eliminated. The main efficiency outcome is the prevalence of defector-free states, which is used as a proxy for group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in spatial public goods games, adaptive punishment is more effective than adaptive rewarding at achieving maximal efficiency (defector-free states), given the same cost-to-impact ratio. The results are highly informative for predicting the effect of introducing punishment (or reward) in spatial PGGs: punishment is more likely to yield higher efficiency than reward, especially when the cost of sanctioning is not prohibitive. The findings are most directly applicable to spatial, lattice-based PGGs with local interactions, and may be less directly transferable to well-mixed or non-spatial settings. The paper's phase diagrams and minimum required cost-to-impact ratios for defector elimination can inform quantitative predictions about efficiency under different game design parameters.

- source: Wang, J. W., Dai, W. H., He, J. L., Yu, F. Y., & Shen, X. (2022). Persistent imitation paves the way for cooperation in public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of prior agreements and posterior compensation (punishment of defectors with compensation to cooperators) in a spatial PGG can promote cooperation, but the effect is highly non-linear and context-dependent. Increasing the cost of compensation (punishment) can lead to abrupt transitions in cooperation rates, and the effect of the agreement threshold is non-monotonic, with intermediate thresholds maximizing cooperation. Importantly, even without punishment (compensation), cooperation can persist within effective agreements due to clustering of conditional cooperators. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment-like mechanisms (posterior compensation) and agreement thresholds affect cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, but does not report efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that punishment does not always increase cooperation (and by extension, may not always increase efficiency), and that the effect depends on the cost of punishment and the agreement threshold. The findings highlight the importance of non-linear and threshold effects, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes. The model is most informative for understanding the behavioral mechanisms and possible moderators of punishment effects, rather than for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Quan, J., Zheng, J. J., Wang, X. J., & Yang, X. K. (2019). The effect of increasing returns to scale in public goods investment on threshold values of cooperation under social exclusion mechanism. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of an exclusion-type punishment mechanism (social exclusion) in a PGG with increasing returns to scale can promote the emergence of cooperation, depending on the cost and effectiveness of exclusion, the returns to scale, and the public goods multiplier. Increasing the effectiveness of exclusion, lowering its cost, or increasing the public goods multiplier can shift the system from all-defection to coexistence of cooperation and exclusion. However, simply increasing returns to scale cannot eliminate defection entirely in the presence of exclusion; it only expands the parameter region where cooperation and exclusion coexist. In structured populations, increasing returns to scale can more directly promote cooperation. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the findings are about the prevalence of cooperative strategies rather than direct payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that the presence and effectiveness of an exclusion-based punishment mechanism can substantially increase the prevalence of cooperation in PGG-like environments, especially when the cost of exclusion is low and its effectiveness is high. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, under certain parameter regimes, enabling punishment (via exclusion) is likely to increase efficiency, but this is inferred from the prevalence of cooperation rather than direct payoff data. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is implemented as social exclusion rather than direct fines.

- source: Orr, S. W. (2001). The economics of shame in work groups: How mutual monitoring can decrease cooperation in teams. *Kyklos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds, through theoretical argument and reference to motivation crowding theory, that mutual monitoring and social sanctions (including social disapproval) in public-goods-like group incentive schemes can decrease cooperation among normatively motivated individuals due to crowding out of intrinsic motivation. The effect is non-linear: some monitoring may initially increase cooperation, but excessive or controlling monitoring leads to suspicion and a shift from normative to gain frames, reducing contributions. For individualistic types, monitoring may crowd in normative motivation and increase cooperation. The overall effect of mutual monitoring or social sanctions on group performance is thus frequency-dependent on the motivational composition of the group. The paper does not provide direct predictions or data on efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that in groups with many normatively motivated members, mutual monitoring may reduce cooperation and thus likely reduce efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical caution that enabling mutual monitoring or social sanctions (punishment) in public-goods-like environments may not always increase efficiency or cooperation, especially in groups with many normatively motivated members. The effect of punishment or monitoring is predicted to be non-linear and frequency-dependent: it may increase cooperation among individualists but decrease it among cooperators if perceived as controlling. For the prediction task, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive and may depend on group composition and the perceived nature of monitoring. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency, so its value is mainly in highlighting possible negative or mixed effects of punishment mechanisms, especially social (rather than material) sanctions.

  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that cooperation in social dilemmas, as studied in public goods games and related models, is just one aspect of a broader class of moral behaviors. It reviews how statistical physics and network science have been used to study cooperation, including mechanisms like punishment and reward, and suggests that these methods can be extended to study other forms of moral behavior. The paper references prior work showing that mechanisms such as punishment, reward, and network structure can affect the evolution of cooperation, but does not provide new empirical or simulation results. Efficiency is mentioned as a moral preference, but not as a measured outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for contextualizing which mechanisms and dimensions (e.g., punishment, reward, network structure) are considered important in the literature, and for mapping which game features have been studied in prior simulation and empirical work. However, it does not report or synthesize quantitative effects of punishment on efficiency or payoff, nor does it provide parameter-level evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Forsyth, P. A. I., & Hauert, C. (2011). Public goods games with reward in finite populations. *Journal of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in finite populations, allowing individuals to reward contributors to the public good can initiate and maintain a significant minority of cooperators, even though the majority of the population is typically composed of non-cooperating shirkers. Reward is effective at jump-starting cooperation but is unable to stabilize it in the long run due to exploitation by second-order free-riders. The average group payoff (efficiency) is increased relative to pure defection, but full efficiency is not achieved. The effectiveness of reward is largely independent of group size when key parameters are scaled appropriately. The paper does not provide new results on punishment, but notes that punishment is more effective at stabilizing cooperation once established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence on the effects of reward (not punishment) in public goods games. It shows that positive incentives can increase efficiency above the baseline of mutual defection, but do not achieve full efficiency due to the persistence of second-order free-riders. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, reward alone is insufficient to fully stabilize cooperation and maximize efficiency. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with reward enabled, but only indirectly inform predictions about punishment. The analysis of group size, MPCR, and reward parameters is informative for understanding how these dimensions affect efficiency with reward. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for cases where the treatment is reward (not punishment), and less directly informative for punishment-enabled games.

- source: Yang, L. H., Xu, Z. J., Zhang, L. Z., & Yang, D. X. (2018). Benefits of intervention in spatial public goods games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional intervention, modeled as a central authority forcing some defectors to contribute, can increase the efficiency (average total payoff) of a spatial PGG, especially when the synergy factor is high and the intervention cost is controlled. Weak intervention can paradoxically reduce cooperation and efficiency, but moderate intervention can lead to higher group incomes than the standard PGG. Excessive intervention may reduce cooperation. The effect of intervention is distinct from peer punishment, as the cost is shared by all contributors and there is no direct punisher. The findings are based on simulation results and analysis of average payoffs and cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in spatial PGGs, the introduction of an institutional intervention mechanism (analogous to centralized punishment) can increase group efficiency, but only under certain parameter regimes: moderate intervention degree/intensity and high synergy factor (mpcr). The effect is non-monotonic: weak or excessive intervention can reduce efficiency. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment-like interventions on efficiency, one should consider the strength and cost of the intervention and the underlying mpcr. The results are most applicable to settings with spatial structure, no peer punishment, and institutional (not individual) enforcement.

- source: Smaldino, P. E., & Lubell, M. (2014). Institutions and Cooperation in an Ecology of Games. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model demonstrates that reputational exclusion, when information about behavior is reliable and flows quickly, allows cooperators to achieve much higher payoffs relative to defectors than capacity constraints. However, if reputation is slow to converge or cooperators are rare, capacity constraints are more effective. The ability of cooperators to join many games under reputation is key to their advantage. The model also finds that increasing the productivity of the public goods game (higher multiplier) increases the relative payoff to cooperators under reputation, but not necessarily under capacity constraints. Limiting the number of games an agent can play reduces the effectiveness of reputation-based exclusion. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning (costly punishment), so direct evidence on punishment is not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it models only exclusion-based institutions (capacity constraints and reputation-based entry) rather than costly punishment. However, it is highly informative about the effects of group entry rules and reputation mechanisms on efficiency and cooperation. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only adjacent, but it provides strong evidence that exclusion and reputation can be powerful alternatives to punishment for promoting efficiency, especially when information is reliable and cooperators are common. The findings suggest that the design of group entry and information flow can be as important as punishment in determining efficiency outcomes.

- source: Dong, Y. L., Gavrilets, S., Qin, C. Z., & Zhang, B. Y. (2024). Kinship can hinder cooperation in heterogeneous populations. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods and multi-player PD games with heterogeneous cost-benefit ratios, increasing genetic relatedness does not always increase cooperation or efficiency. For low relatedness, direct reciprocity (punishment via GRIM) can sustain cooperation and high efficiency. For high relatedness, cooperation is a dominant strategy and efficiency is high. However, for intermediate relatedness, some individuals become unconditional cooperators (AllC), which undermines the effectiveness of punishment and allows others to defect (AllD), making full cooperation and maximal efficiency unsustainable. This results in a non-monotonic, U-shaped relationship between relatedness and efficiency. The findings are supported by both subgame perfect equilibrium analysis and agent-based evolutionary simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in repeated public goods games with heterogeneity in cost-benefit ratios, the effect of mechanisms analogous to punishment (i.e., direct reciprocity via GRIM) on efficiency depends critically on the population structure and relatedness. When relatedness is low, punishment can sustain high efficiency; when relatedness is high, cooperation is self-sustaining. However, at intermediate relatedness, the threat of punishment is undermined, and efficiency drops. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of heterogeneity and the degree of relatedness (or analogous parameters) can cause non-monotonic effects of punishment on efficiency, and that punishment is most effective at low relatedness. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in repeated PGG-like games with heterogeneity, but less so for one-shot games or games with explicit costly punishment mechanisms.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2016). Competition of tolerant strategies in the spatial public goods game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing diverse tolerant strategies into the spatial public goods game can significantly increase group efficiency, sometimes leading to defector-free, fully cooperative states. The effect depends on the synergy factor (r), the cost of monitoring (gamma), and the system size. In structured populations, coexistence of multiple tolerant strategies can create synergistic effects that outperform single-strategy or uniform-tolerance scenarios. The paper provides detailed phase diagrams showing which strategy combinations are stable under different parameter regimes. The main efficiency gains occur when tolerant strategies with different thresholds form alliances, crowding out defectors and maximizing group payoff. The results highlight the importance of diversity in tolerance and the structure of interactions for achieving high efficiency in public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial public goods games, the introduction of conditional strategies (tolerant players) that abstain from cooperation when defection is high can substantially increase efficiency, especially when multiple tolerance thresholds are present. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of such conditional strategies (even in the absence of explicit costly punishment) can be as or more effective than standard punishment in raising efficiency, particularly in structured populations. The results are most relevant for games with spatial structure, optional participation, and where monitoring costs are not prohibitive. The paper does not directly manipulate or analyze explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, so its direct applicability to punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled prediction is limited, but it provides a strong adjacent signal about the power of conditional sanctioning and abstention. For prediction, the key moderators are the synergy factor (mpcr), the cost of monitoring (analogous to punishment cost), and the structure of the population.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhang, J. L., & Weissing, F. J. (2014). The role of emotions in the maintenance of cooperative behaviors. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a stress-based cost for defectors in a public goods game can make cooperation evolutionarily stable if the stress intensity exceeds a calculable threshold. This threshold is lower for larger populations and higher public goods multipliers, and higher for larger group sizes. The model provides explicit formulas for the fixation probability of cooperation and the conditions under which cooperation can invade and dominate a population of defectors. The findings are directly relevant to efficiency, as they show under what conditions the group can achieve higher payoffs through sustained cooperation, even in the absence of explicit external punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that internalized costs for defection (modeled as stress or moral emotions) can substitute for explicit punishment in promoting cooperation and group efficiency in public goods games. While it does not study explicit punishment, it shows that any mechanism that imposes a cost on defection (whether internal or external) can shift the evolutionary dynamics toward cooperation, provided the cost is above a threshold determined by game parameters (group size, population size, and MPCR). For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of any effective cost for defection—whether via punishment or internalized emotion—can increase efficiency, and that the required cost decreases with larger populations and higher MPCR. However, the model does not address explicit punishment technology, cost, or identification, so its direct applicability to those dimensions is limited.

- source: Gao, J., Li, Z., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2010). Diversity of contribution promotes cooperation in public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to preferentially contribute more to groups with higher cooperation levels (high-quality groups) significantly promotes the evolution of cooperation in public goods games, both on regular lattices and scale-free networks. The mechanism works by enabling cooperators to cluster and avoid exploitation by defectors, and by increasing the wealth/payoff of cooperators for a given cooperation level. The effect is stronger as the preference parameter (alpha) increases. The paper interprets the reduction of contributions to low-quality groups as a form of indirect, costless group-level punishment, but does not model or analyze explicit costly punishment. Group efficiency or total group payoff is not directly reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is relevant for understanding how endogenous contribution patterns (where cooperators can direct more resources to cooperative groups) affect cooperation and the distribution of payoffs in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of explicit, costly punishment on efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that indirect, costless group-level punishment (via reduced contributions to low-quality groups) can promote cooperation and increase the payoffs of cooperators, but the implications for overall group efficiency are not quantified. For prediction tasks focused on explicit punishment interventions, this paper provides only adjacent evidence, mainly relevant for understanding alternative mechanisms to punishment.

- source: Takács, K., & Janky, B. (2007). Smiling contributions:: Social control in a public goods game with network decline. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical framework for public goods games with endogenous network change and social control mechanisms. It predicts that clustered networks reduce collective action and that the ability to delete ties leads to segregation between contributors and defectors. Social control is operationalized as the ability to send happy or sad smileys, which are intended to induce conformity or selective incentives. The experimental design is described, but no data or results are presented. The main dependent variable in the proposed experiments is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how network structure and non-costly social control (smileys) might affect contribution behavior in public goods games. However, it does not provide empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it study costly punishment as typically defined in the literature. The model suggests that social control (even if non-costly) and network clustering can influence cooperation, but the lack of empirical results and the focus on non-payoff outcomes limit its direct utility for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions. The design dimensions are discussed in the context of the model and proposed experiment, but not tested empirically.

- source: Toriumi, F., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2020). A belief in rewards accelerates cooperation on consumer-generated media. *Journal of Computational Social Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a public goods game with only rewards and meta-rewards (no punishment), cooperation can be sustained if players can observe and respond to the individual meta-rewarding rates of others. When players base their reward-giving on the observed likelihood of receiving a meta-reward from a specific cooperator, stable cooperation emerges. If players cannot condition on this information, cooperation collapses. The benefit-to-cost ratio of rewards and meta-rewards must be sufficiently high for cooperation to be maintained. The model does not include punishment, and efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding the role of reward and meta-reward mechanisms, and highlights the importance of observability and benefit-to-cost ratios for sustaining cooperation. However, since punishment is not modeled, and efficiency or group payoff is not a reported outcome, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding adjacent mechanisms (reward-based), but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Perc, M. (2010). Reward and cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing costly reward in a spatial public goods game can promote cooperation, particularly when the synergy factor (MPCR) is low. However, the effect is non-monotonic: moderate rewards can be more effective than high rewards due to the emergence of cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and rewarding cooperators. At high synergy factors, network reciprocity alone can sustain cooperation, and reward mainly determines which cooperative strategy prevails. The paper notes that, compared to punishment, reward is less efficient at promoting cooperation, especially because rewarding cooperators always pay a cost, making them vulnerable to second-order free-riders. The main reported outcomes are the stationary frequencies of each strategy and the phase diagrams of their coexistence, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of reward (not punishment) on cooperation in spatial public goods games. It does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it suggests that costly reward can promote cooperation under certain conditions, but is generally less effective than punishment. The findings are most relevant for understanding the role of reward mechanisms and the importance of spatial structure, synergy factor (MPCR), and cost-benefit ratios in shaping cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only adjacent: it does not provide direct evidence on punishment or efficiency, but may inform expectations about the relative effectiveness of reward versus punishment and the importance of game parameters.

  findings: The study finds that allocating taxes and rewards based on memory reputation generally increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games, especially in environments with high taxes or high synergy factors. In low-tax, low-synergy environments (favorable to defectors), equal allocation of rewards and taxes is more effective at sustaining cooperation. The model shows that the effectiveness of reward mechanisms depends on the balance between tax and reward costs, memory length, and the synergy factor. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, and does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the structure of incentive mechanisms (tax-funded rewards, reputation-based allocation) can affect cooperation rates, and that the effectiveness of such mechanisms depends on environmental parameters. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide contextual insight into how non-punitive incentive mechanisms interact with game design dimensions.

- source: Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2020). Public Goods Games on Coevolving Social Network Models. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing agents to rewire their social connections when unsatisfied (i.e., when their payoff is less than their contribution) leads to higher average contributions in public goods games on networks, compared to static networks. The transition from low to high contribution occurs at lower enhancement factors when rewiring is allowed, and higher rewiring probabilities further increase average contributions. These effects are robust across both synthetic and real social network structures. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or reward options, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling a form of costless partner switching (exit) can increase average contributions in networked public goods games, which may be relevant for predicting the effects of punishment-like mechanisms that allow players to avoid or sanction defectors. However, because the model does not include explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment is limited. The findings are most relevant for understanding the impact of dynamic network structure (partner choice) rather than punishment per se.

  findings: The study finds that increasing the threshold required to initiate a public goods game (i.e., requiring more cooperators for the game to proceed) leads to higher stable levels of cooperation in a spatially structured population. Introducing a reputation adjustment factor, where defectors lose reputation and cooperators gain reputation, further increases cooperation rates. The model shows that even small reputation effects can shift the population toward cooperation. The effect of noise in strategy updating is complex: higher noise can promote cooperation under high thresholds. However, the paper does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare; all main results are in terms of the fraction of cooperators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms which increase the cost of defection (here, via reputation loss) or require a threshold of cooperation to initiate public goods production can increase cooperation rates in spatial PGGs. However, because the model does not include explicit punishment (costly sanctions) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding how threshold and reputation mechanisms affect cooperation, but should not be used as direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment interventions.

- source: Li, Y. X., Jin, X. G., Su, X. C., Kong, F. S., & Peng, C. B. (2010). Cooperation and charity in spatial public goods game under different strategy update rules. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.

  findings: The study finds that charity (modeled as payoff transfer from egalitarians to the lowest-payoff neighbor) does not enhance cooperation under random selection update rules, but does promote cooperation under threshold selection rules (where only the lowest-payoff individuals update their strategies). The effect of charity is measured by the frequency of cooperation, not by group payoff or efficiency. The model shows that the presence of charity can lower the threshold for the multiplication factor (r) needed to sustain cooperation under threshold selection. The frequency of charity is positively correlated with the variance of payoffs in the population. The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it provides indirect evidence about the effect of a redistribution mechanism (charity) on cooperation rates under different update rules. The findings suggest that the effect of a second-stage redistribution mechanism (distinct from punishment) on cooperation depends on the selection dynamics, but the lack of efficiency or payoff reporting limits its direct utility for the prediction task. The game structure and update rules are described in detail, which may be useful for mapping to similar simulation studies.
