  findings: The paper synthesizes theoretical and modeling work on PGGs in disease and cancer, highlighting how cooperation can be sustained or undermined depending on game parameters, network structure, and the presence of mechanisms like social norms or sanctions. It reviews how public health interventions (e.g., vaccination, mask mandates) can be conceptualized as public goods dilemmas, and how individual decision-making, social structure, and perceived costs/benefits shape compliance. In cancer, it discusses how cooperation among cancer cells can be modeled as a PGG, with implications for treatment strategies. However, the paper does not provide quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze the effects of punishment or sanctions on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual background on how PGGs are used to model cooperation in health-related contexts and discusses mechanisms (including sanctions and incentives) that can promote cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative results on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in PGGs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may inform the qualitative understanding of how design dimensions (e.g., network structure, cost-benefit ratios, threshold effects) can influence cooperation, but it does not supply data or direct evidence to calibrate predictions of efficiency with or without punishment. Its discussion of sanctions is high-level and not tied to specific game parameters or outcomes.

- source: Benchekroun, H., & Van Long, N. (2008). The build-up of cooperative behavior among non-cooperative selfish agents. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated public goods game with no punishment or sanctions, but where agents can condition their behavior on a public 'stock of cooperation' (social history), a continuum of Markovian Nash equilibria exist. These equilibria can support steady-state contribution and welfare levels above the static Nash equilibrium but below the social optimum. The social optimum cannot be stably achieved without punishment. The model provides explicit analytical characterizations of these equilibria and shows that the more patient the agents (lower discount rate), the closer the best equilibrium can approach the social optimum. The paper emphasizes that, unlike models with punishment, the highest sustainable efficiency is strictly less than the social optimum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in public goods games without punishment or sanctions, especially when social history or trust mechanisms are present. It provides strong theoretical evidence that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency can be improved above the Nash baseline if agents coordinate on social-history-based strategies, but the social optimum is not achievable. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment (which is absent here) would allow efficiency to reach or exceed the best equilibrium described in this model, and potentially reach the social optimum. The paper is not informative about the effect of punishment per se, but it provides a theoretical upper bound for efficiency in the absence of punishment, given social history mechanisms.

- source: Archetti, M., & Scheuring, I. (2011). COEXISTENCE OF COOPERATION AND DEFECTION IN PUBLIC GOODS GAMES. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the public goods function (linear vs. nonlinear/threshold) fundamentally changes the equilibrium outcome. In nonlinear/threshold (Volunteer's Dilemma-like) games, cooperation and defection can coexist at a stable mixed equilibrium, and the public good is produced with positive probability, even in one-shot, well-mixed populations without relatedness or repeated play. In linear (NPD) games, only defection is stable in the absence of assortment. The paper provides analytical and numerical results for the equilibrium frequency of cooperation and the probability of public good provision as a function of group size, cost/benefit ratio, and the threshold parameter. No punishment, reward, or external enforcement mechanisms are modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public goods games as a function of the public good production function (linear vs. nonlinear/threshold), group size, and cost/benefit ratio, but it does not address the effects of punishment or sanctions. It provides direct theoretical predictions for efficiency and group payoff in the absence of punishment, but cannot inform the effect of enabling punishment. For downstream prediction tasks focused on the effect of punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it is valuable for understanding baseline efficiency in control conditions without punishment, especially in games with nonlinear or threshold public goods functions.

- source: Kang, H. W., Jin, Z. Y., Li, M. Y., Wang, M., Sun, X. P., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). Discreet investors exert greater influence on cooperation in the public goods game. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The introduction of discreet investors—who pay a cost to obtain information about their neighbors' cooperation and adjust their investments accordingly—leads to higher average payoffs and more robust cooperation compared to standard cooperators, especially at low synergy factors (r). As the cost of information (C_DI) increases, the advantage of discreet investors diminishes, and at high synergy factors, standard cooperators can survive and dominate. The model does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main outcome is average payoff, which is a direct analog to efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide evidence that mechanisms allowing players to condition their contributions on local information (at a cost) can increase average payoffs and cooperation, especially when the cost is low. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant because it does not manipulate or analyze punishment. It may be useful as a conceptual analog for the effects of costly information acquisition or heterogeneity in investment, but not for punishment effects.

- source: Park, C. (2022). Network Characteristic Control of Social Dilemmas in a Public Good Game: Numerical Simulation of Agent-Based Nonlinear Dynamics. *Processes*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary PGGs, the average payoff and the prevalence of cooperation depend strongly on the participation cost, the incentive (interest rate), mutation rate, and the structure of the social network (network density, eigenvector centrality). High participation costs or low incentives lead to nonparticipation and lower average payoffs. High network density can mediate the effects of mutation, sometimes stabilizing cooperation. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, so it does not address the effects of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs or related environments. It is useful for understanding how network structure, mutation, and incentive parameters affect efficiency and cooperation in PGGs without punishment. It cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is modeled or analyzed.

- source: Wang, X. J., & Chen, W. M. (2019). The evolution of cooperation in public good game with deposit. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a deposit mechanism into the PGG can promote cooperation under certain parameter regimes. The equilibrium frequencies of cooperation, deposit cooperation, and defection depend on the deposit size, discount rate, benefit-sharing rule (weak vs. strong altruism), and the threshold for public good provision. Higher thresholds and weak altruism (where contributors also benefit) increase the equilibrium level of cooperation. Lower deposit and discount rates promote deposit cooperation. The model provides explicit formulas for expected payoffs and equilibrium frequencies, but does not address punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments. It is not informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as it does not include any punishment, sanction, or peer enforcement mechanism. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how deposit-based commitment mechanisms and benefit-sharing rules affect cooperation and payoffs in PGGs without punishment.

- source: Quan, J., Liu, W., Chu, Y. Q., & Wang, X. J. (2018). Stochastic dynamics and stable equilibrium of evolutionary optional public goods game in finite populations. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in an optional public goods game (with strategies: cooperate, defect, or opt out), the probability of the group reaching full cooperation, full defection, or universal non-participation depends on the investment return multiplier (r), the fixed income for non-participants (sigma), and the group size (N). Optional participation can prevent the spread of defection and, for certain parameter ranges (large r and sigma), can make full cooperation the most likely outcome. However, for small sigma or r, non-participation or defection may dominate. The model provides quantitative relationships between parameters and the likelihood of each stable state, which directly map to group efficiency. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly informative for predicting efficiency in optional public goods games without punishment or reward. It provides quantitative relationships between group size, number of rounds (implicitly infinite in the Markov process), MPCR (via r and N), and the probability of full cooperation (i.e., efficiency). However, it does not address the effect of punishment, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment. It is most useful as a control or baseline for optional PGGs without sanctions.

- source: Chessa, M., & Loiseau, P. (2024). Enhancing voluntary contributions in a public goods economy via a minimum individual contribution level. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that introducing a minimum individual contribution level (while still allowing for full free riding) in a voluntary public goods game can strictly increase the total contribution and social welfare at equilibrium, provided the minimum is not set too high. The analysis covers both heterogeneous and homogeneous cost cases, and characterizes the equilibrium structure as a function of the minimum. The results show that there is an optimal range for the minimum contribution: if set just above the lowest equilibrium contribution in the baseline game, it increases total contributions without causing contributors to drop out. If set too high, some contributors become free riders and total contributions can decrease. The paper provides operational guidance for setting the minimum but does not address punishment, reward, or other sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not study punishment or sanctions. However, it is highly informative about the effects of restricting the contribution set by imposing a minimum, which is a different intervention. The findings may be useful for understanding how changes to the action space (such as minimums or defaults) affect efficiency, but cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Tu, J. (2018). Contribution inequality in the spatial public goods game: Should the rich contribute more?. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that when the rich contribute more (higher w), the effect on average wealth (group payoff) and cooperation rate depends on the enhancement factor (mpcr, or r/(z+1)). For low enhancement factors, higher w reduces both cooperation and average wealth. For intermediate enhancement, higher w can increase average wealth even as cooperation rate falls, because the extra contributions from the rich compensate for more defectors. For high enhancement, higher w increases both cooperation and average wealth, and reduces wealth inequality. The relationship between cooperation rate and group payoff is not always positive: higher cooperation does not always mean higher group wealth. The results are robust to population size but sensitive to network degree.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not study punishment or sanctions, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games. However, it provides strong evidence that group payoff (efficiency) can be decoupled from cooperation rate, especially when contribution rules are heterogeneous. The findings suggest that interventions that change contribution rules (e.g., progressive contributions) can have complex effects on efficiency, depending on the enhancement factor. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant to punishment, but it is highly relevant to understanding how efficiency responds to other game design dimensions, especially mpcr and contribution heterogeneity.

- source: Fan, R. G., Zhang, Y. Q., Luo, M., & Zhang, H. J. (2017). Promotion of cooperation induced by heterogeneity of both investment and payoff allocation in spatial public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that heterogeneity in both investment and payoff allocation, especially under the mixed-preference mechanism (where both previous payoff and social status influence decisions), promotes higher average payoff and cooperation rates compared to mechanisms based solely on wealth or social status. Increasing the enhancement factor r or reducing the heterogeneity parameter alpha further increases cooperation and average payoff. The structure of the interaction network (BA scale-free) also enhances cooperation and payoff compared to other network types. However, the paper does not include or analyze any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in investment and payoff allocation, and network structure, affect efficiency and cooperation in PGGs without punishment. It should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Sun, C. H., & Duan, H. B. (2015). Impact of shill intervention on the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The introduction of shill agents with a Fixed-Cost-per-Player contribution rule into a PGG on a scale-free network can substantially increase both cooperation rates and average payoffs (social welfare), particularly when shills are preferentially placed on high-degree nodes and the dilemma is severe (low enhancement factor). The effect is strongest for high-degree-preference shill placement (large alpha) and in harsh environments. As the environment becomes more favorable (higher enhancement factor), local cooperators reappear and can coexist with shill cooperators, and distributing shills on low-degree nodes (small alpha) can maximize social welfare. The paper provides detailed simulation evidence on how these effects depend on the enhancement factor, shill fraction, and shill placement, but does not study punishment or sanctioning.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly relevant for predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments with external intervention via shill agents, but not for environments with punishment or sanctioning. The findings suggest that introducing agents with alternative contribution rules (partial free-riders) can increase efficiency in harsh environments, especially if they are placed on network hubs. The results are parameter-dependent and highlight the importance of network structure and agent placement. However, since the paper does not involve punishment, its direct predictive value for punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency is limited. It is most useful for understanding the effects of external intervention and heterogeneity in contribution rules on efficiency.

- source: Rong, Z. H., & Wu, Z. X. (2009). Effect of the degree correlation in public goods game on scale-free networks. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in scale-free networks, assortative degree mixing (where hubs are connected to other hubs) inhibits the emergence and sustainment of cooperation in the PGG, as the core-periphery structure weakens the ability of cooperative hubs to influence peripheral nodes. In disassortative networks (where hubs connect to low-degree nodes), cooperation can persist even at low multiplication factors, as hubs maintain their initial strategies and can influence their leaf neighbors. The main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, with some analysis of average payoff per degree. No punishment or sanctioning is present in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. It is relevant for understanding how network structure (degree correlation) affects cooperation and individual payoffs in PGGs without punishment. The findings may be useful for contextualizing baseline efficiency or cooperation rates in networked PGGs, but do not provide evidence about the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Liu, P. H., & Liu, J. (2017). Contribution diversity and incremental learning promote cooperation in public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both contribution diversity (CD)—where individuals reallocate their contributions based on response rates—and incremental learning (IL)—where individuals imitate those with long-term high payoffs—promote cooperation in PGGs on scale-free networks. CD is more effective at higher mpcr (eta), while IL stabilizes cooperation even when CD is less effective. The combination of CD and IL is most effective. The main reported outcomes are cooperation frequency and the average payoff/fitness difference between cooperators and defectors. No group efficiency or total group payoff is reported. The models do not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs, as punishment is not modeled. It does provide indirect evidence that mechanisms other than punishment (contribution diversity and incremental learning) can promote cooperation and increase the relative payoff of cooperators, especially at higher mpcr. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative. For tasks considering alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms, the findings suggest that structural and learning modifications can substitute for punishment in promoting cooperation, but the effect on group efficiency is not quantified.

- source: Amado, A., Huang, W. N., Campos, P. R. A., & Ferreira, F. F. (2015). Learning process in public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with bounded rationality and finite memory, both the learning process (dynamic vs static) and the public goods enhancement factor (r) strongly affect cooperation rates. In dynamic learning, increasing r leads to a transition from defection to a neutral regime (cooperation ~50%), while in static learning, cooperation can exceed 50% when r > N. Memory size smooths the transition from defection to cooperation. The expected payoff change is positive when r > 1 and cooperation is present, but the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff as a main outcome. No punishment or reward mechanisms are included.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide detailed modeling of how learning, memory, and the enhancement factor r affect cooperation and expected payoffs in standard PGGs. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide useful context for baseline (no-punishment) efficiency as a function of r, memory, and learning process.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Perc, M., Wang, W. X., Lai, Y. C., & Wang, B. H. (2011). Impact of link deletions on public cooperation in scale-free networks. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in multi-group PGGs on scale-free networks, deleting links connected to hubs (network attack) predominantly reduces the fraction of cooperators, especially at low multiplication factors. There is a narrow region where the effect is strictly negative, but outside this region, an intermediate number of link deletions can minimize cooperation. Wealth distributions become less heterogeneous as links are deleted, which is associated with lower cooperation. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, but does analyze total payoffs (wealth) at the individual level.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs or their variants. It is relevant for understanding how network structure and heterogeneity affect cooperation and individual payoffs, but not for predicting the effect of punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task. However, the analysis of wealth distributions may offer indirect insights into how structural features (like network heterogeneity) can moderate payoff outcomes in PGGs.

- source: Huang, Z. G., Wu, Z. X., Wu, A. C., Yang, L., & Wang, Y. H. (2008). Role of collective influence in promoting cooperation. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in public goods games on heterogeneous networks, both the structure of the network and the way individuals distribute their contributions (collective influence parameter alpha) strongly affect cooperation and payoffs. When individuals prefer to contribute more to smaller groups (alpha < 0, social loafing), cooperators receive higher net payoffs, and cooperation is more likely to persist, especially in scale-free networks. The paper provides detailed analysis of individual net payoffs (P_ii) as a function of network degree and alpha, showing that social loafing can make cooperation individually rational for a larger fraction of players. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides useful context for how network structure and contribution allocation rules (collective influence) affect cooperation and payoffs in PGG-like environments. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may be informative for understanding baseline efficiency and cooperation in the absence of punishment, especially in networked or heterogeneous group settings.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, X. K., & Wang, X. J. (2018). Spatial public goods game with continuous contributions based on Particle Swarm Optimization learning and the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO) learning rule can promote higher average cooperation levels in spatial public goods games with continuous contributions, compared to the Fermi rule and Genetic Algorithm learning. The network structure matters: a nearest-neighbor coupled network (1-D) is more conducive to cooperation than a square lattice (2-D). The memory/imitation parameter (c9) has a complex, non-linear effect on cooperation, depending on the investment enhancement factor (r). However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show how network structure and learning rules affect average cooperation levels in spatial continuous PGGs. It may inform expectations about baseline cooperation in the absence of punishment, but not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Li, A. M., Wu, T., Cong, R., & Wang, L. (2013). One step memory of group reputation is optimal to promote cooperation in public goods games. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with dynamic group formation based on group reputation (which is determined by individual memory of past cooperation), cooperation is maximized when individuals have short memory (one-step). Moderate levels of expectation about group reputation and sufficient opportunities to change groups (network dynamics) also promote cooperation. Too high or too low expectations, or too long memory, reduce cooperation. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings about memory, reputation, and group switching may be relevant for understanding other mechanisms that promote cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Ramalingam, A., Morales, A. J., & Walker, J. M. (2018). Varying experimental instructions to improve comprehension: Punishment in public goods games. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games, mechanisms that allow individuals to preferentially imitate high-reputation neighbors (high reputation reasoning ability) promote the emergence and stability of cooperation. However, when reputation is updated using second-order rules (taking into account both the individual's and the worst neighbor's actions), cooperation is inhibited compared to first-order rules, especially when the magnitude of reputation change is large. Heterogeneity in reasoning ability (both endogenous and exogenous) can reduce cooperation, particularly when the variance or degree of heterogeneity is high. The main reported outcome is the cooperation rate; average payoffs are discussed in relation to strategy and reputation clusters but not as a primary outcome. No punishment or sanctioning is modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how reputation-based social learning and information structures affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The reported outcomes (cooperation rate, reputation distribution, and some average payoffs by type) are not directly translatable to group efficiency as defined in the prediction task. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may provide contextual insight into how non-punitive social mechanisms (like reputation) affect cooperation.

- source: Kang, H. W., Zhou, X. J., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2021). Influencer propagation model promotes cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing influencers with extra gain (parameter alpha) and varying their proportion (theta) can significantly promote the emergence and stability of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when the synergy factor (r) is low. The model shows that cooperative influencer groups can resist invasion by defectors and spread cooperation through the network. However, as r increases, the effect of influencers on promoting cooperation diminishes, and high values of alpha and theta can eventually stabilize both cooperative and betrayal groups. The main outcome is the proportion of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how leadership, influencer effects, and heterogeneity can promote cooperation, but does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings may be useful for adjacent questions about mechanisms that promote cooperation, but not for the specific prediction task of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency.

- source: Shi, D. M., & Wang, B. H. (2017). Critical mass of public goods and its coevolution with cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that cooperation is maximized at an intermediate value of the critical mass threshold (cm), with both too low and too high thresholds leading to lower cooperation. The nature of the public good (consumable vs. reusable) and the depreciation rate parameter (Gamma) affect the optimal threshold and the dynamics of cooperation. The results are robust across different network structures. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or any sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings about critical mass and cooperation density may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing a continuous range of cooperation degrees (strategy diversity) in public goods games leads to higher average cooperation, especially when all players have equal initial wealth. The presence of strategy diversity prevents long-term exploitation and leads to the extinction of defectors at relatively low mpcr values.   findings: The paper finds that in multiplex public goods games with limited and partible resources, cooperation is more likely to be maintained when at least one network layer is scale-free (high degree diversity) and when agents use a 'greedy-first' updating rule (imitating strategies in the higher-payoff layer).   dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the coexistence of imitation and aspiration dynamics in structured populations can have nontrivial effects on cooperation. For fixed update rules, introducing a moderate fraction of aspiration-driven individuals into an imitation-driven population can cause a 'plunge of cooperation' at intermediate synergy factors. When both strategies and update rules coevolve, only one update rule eventually dominates, and the stationary cooperation level depends on which rule prevails. Aspiration dynamics can promote cooperation when the synergy factor is low (poor synergy), but undermine it when the synergy factor is high. However, the study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings about the effects of update rules (imitation vs aspiration) on cooperation rates may be of indirect interest for understanding behavioral dynamics, but are not directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with or without punishment. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Xie, K., Liu, X. W., Chen, H., & Yang, J. (2022). Preferential selection and expected payoff drive cooperation in spatial voluntary public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial VPGG, introducing an expected payoff threshold and preferential selection in strategy updating can significantly increase the cooperation rate under certain parameter regimes. Specifically, positive expected payoff thresholds (p_e) and higher multiplication factors (r) promote cooperation, while the effect of the preferential selection parameter (a) depends on its sign and value. The cooperation rate is robust to a for positive p_e, but both a and r matter for negative p_e. The main dependent variable is the cooperation rate; no efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on punishment or efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be contextually useful for understanding how non-punishment mechanisms (like aspiration levels and neighbor selection) affect cooperation rates.

- source: Wang, Y. J., & Chen, T. (2015). Heuristics guide cooperative behaviors in public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the presence and strength of 'cooperators with independent thought' (CIs) can strongly promote and stabilize cooperation in a spatial PGG. When the heuristic capability of CIs exceeds that of 'defectors with independent thought' (DIs), cooperation dominates; when the reverse is true, defection dominates. If both have equal heuristic capability, coexistence occurs. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is not informative for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the model. - source: Wang, X. F., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2014). Do not aim too high nor too low: Moderate expectation-based group formation promotes public cooperation on networks. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to leave groups that do not meet their expectation for cooperativeness and to form new ties elsewhere can substantially increase the frequency of cooperation in public goods games on networks. The effect is strongest when individuals have a moderate expectation threshold (neither too high nor too low). The time scale ratio of selection to interaction also matters: more frequent interaction relative to selection promotes cooperation. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how endogenous group formation and network rewiring based on individual expectations can affect cooperation rates, but not efficiency or payoff. The findings may be indirectly informative for settings where group structure is endogenous, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The absence of punishment and the lack of payoff-based outcome reporting limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing a depreciation mechanism (where the value of the public good is reduced if not all cooperate) promotes cooperation compared to the standard PGG. There is an optimal intermediate value of the depreciation parameter (beta) that maximizes cooperation, and this optimal range broadens as the multiplication factor (r) increases. No punishment or sanctioning is studied, and efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.

- source: Liu, A. Z., Zhang, Y. L., Chen, X. J., & Sun, C. Y. (2017). Randomness and diversity matter in the maintenance of the public resources. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that both greater diversity (more available contribution levels) and greater randomness (higher mutation, smaller population, weaker selection) increase the average investment in the public goods game. Under weak selection, average investment is high and insensitive to diversity or randomness. Under strong selection, average investment is low and insensitive to diversity or population size, but increases with mutation. Under intermediate selection, average investment increases with diversity and mutation, and decreases with population size. No payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported, and no punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is only relevant for understanding how diversity in contribution options and evolutionary randomness affect average investment in the absence of enforcement mechanisms. It provides no evidence about the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency.

- source: Li, J., Wu, T., Zeng, G., & Wang, L. (2012). Selective investment promotes cooperation in public goods game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that selective investment (where cooperators invest in only a fraction of their neighbors) robustly promotes cooperation in networked public goods games. Lower selectivity parameter (alpha) accelerates the emergence and maintenance of cooperation compared to the classical model where cooperators invest in all groups. Social-preference and wealth-preference patterns (where investment is biased toward high-degree or high-payoff neighbors) hamper the speed and stability of cooperation compared to random selection. Anti-preference patterns (investing in less connected or lower-payoff neighbors) can further accelerate cooperation. Theoretical analysis on double-star networks supports these simulation results. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how network structure and selective investment affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The results may be useful for contextualizing the baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, but do not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency.

- source: Broom, M., Lafaye, C., Pattni, K., & Rychtár, J. (2015). A study of the dynamics of multi-player games on small networks using territorial interactions. *Journal of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the fixation probability of cooperators in the public goods game is generally less than 1/N (where N is the number of players), indicating that cooperation is disfavored under the birth-death process in these small networked populations. The fixation probability is strongly influenced by the mean temperature (a measure of how often individuals are replaced), with higher temperatures amplifying selection and lower temperatures suppressing it. The specific graph structure (network topology) has some effect, but mean temperature is a more important determinant. The results are derived for small graphs (3-4 players), and the framework is generalizable to larger graphs in principle. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the models.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. It does, however, provide theoretical insights into how network structure and group size affect the evolutionary success of cooperation versus defection in public goods games. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, but it may provide contextual background on how structural features (such as player count and group formation) influence cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Congleton, R. D., & Vanberg, V. J. (2001). Help, harm or avoid? On the personal advantage of dispositions to cooperate and punish in multilateral PD games with exit. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in three-person repeated joint production games with exit, conditional cooperation and the ability to exit dysfunctional teams allow for higher average payoffs (efficiency) than unconditional defection. However, in short-lived enterprises, targeted punishment of defectors is necessary to sustain cooperation and high payoffs. When targeted punishment is strong enough, even in short-lived games, cooperation and efficiency are restored. The results are robust in both static and evolutionary (replicator dynamic) simulations. The study provides direct evidence that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms are key moderators of the efficiency gains from enabling punishment in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that enabling targeted punishment in small-group repeated public goods games with exit increases efficiency, especially when the number of rounds is small. The effect of punishment is more positive when the punishment is strong (high impact per cost) and can be targeted at defectors. The results suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of targeted punishment, the number of rounds, and the ability to exit are critical moderators of the efficiency effect. Control efficiency (without punishment) is higher when exit and conditional cooperation are possible, but enabling punishment further increases efficiency, especially in short games. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency.

- source: Gao, S. P., & Liang, J. L. (2020). The effects of resource limitation and consensual incentives on cooperation. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a nonlinear multiplayer public goods game with a threshold and resource limitation, the introduction of consensual incentives (punishment or reward, decided collectively) can promote cooperation and increase group payoff (efficiency), provided that the population's preference for incentives is sufficiently high. Without incentives, the system always evolves to full defection (zero efficiency). The effect of incentives is not monotonic in resource abundance: there is an intermediate resource level where the positive effect of incentives is minimized, and both scarcity and abundance of resources can promote cooperation when incentives are present. The model also shows that a lower consensus threshold for employing incentives and a higher preference for incentives both increase the basin of attraction for full cooperation. Consensual reward can be more effective than punishment in promoting cooperation when preferences for incentives are high.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that enabling punishment (or reward) in a nonlinear, threshold-based public goods game can shift the system from full defection (zero efficiency) to full cooperation (high efficiency), provided that the population's preference for incentives is above a critical value. The effect is sensitive to the consensus threshold for employing incentives, the abundance of resources, and the type of incentive. For prediction, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially if it is collectively decided and the population is willing to use it) is likely to increase efficiency dramatically compared to the control (no punishment) case. However, the effect may be minimized at intermediate resource levels. The model is analytic and does not provide empirical effect sizes, but the qualitative direction is clear: enabling punishment (or reward) increases efficiency in this class of games.

- source: Noailly, J., van den Bergh, J. C. J. M., & Withagen, C. A. (2009). Local and Global Interactions in an Evolutionary Resource Game. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that local punishment mechanisms in a spatial CPR game allow for the emergence and persistence of cooperative and mixed-strategy equilibria, which are associated with higher aggregate payoffs and efficiency than the all-defector equilibrium. The frequency of efficient equilibria increases with higher sanction levels and larger population sizes, and spatial clustering of cooperators and enforcers is crucial for sustaining cooperation. The model demonstrates that spatial structure and local enforcement can overcome the limitations of non-spatial models, leading to more diverse and efficient outcomes. Sensitivity analysis shows that increasing the cost or effectiveness of sanctions, or increasing population size, generally increases the prevalence of efficient (cooperative or mixed) equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in spatially structured public-goods-like games increases group efficiency and aggregate payoff, especially when punishment is local and sufficiently strong. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, the introduction of local punishment will often lead to higher efficiency, particularly in larger groups and with higher sanction levels. The results also highlight the importance of spatial structure and local interactions, which may moderate the effect of punishment compared to non-spatial models. The paper's parameter sensitivity analysis can inform predictions about how changes in punishment cost, effectiveness, and group size affect the efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Noailly, J., Withagen, C. A., & van den Bergh, J. C. J. M. (2007). Spatial evolution of social norms in a common-pool resource game. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a spatial CPR game, the introduction of enforcers (who punish defectors) leads to the emergence and persistence of cooperative equilibria, which are associated with higher aggregate payoffs and more efficient resource use. The frequency of such efficient equilibria increases with the sanction rate (punishment effectiveness) and is further enhanced when resource dynamics (feedback from resource depletion) are included. Simulations show that, for a wide range of parameters, punishment increases the likelihood of equilibria with cooperation and higher payoffs, compared to scenarios without punishment. The spatial structure (local punishment) is crucial for these results, as it allows cooperators and enforcers to protect each other and sustain cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in a spatial CPR (public goods-like) game increases the frequency of efficient, high-payoff equilibria, especially when the punishment is sufficiently strong and resource feedbacks are present. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (with nontrivial cost and effectiveness), local interaction, and resource dynamics should be expected to increase treatment efficiency relative to control. The results are most directly applicable to settings with local punishment, spatial structure, and evolutionary imitation dynamics, but the qualitative direction (punishment increases efficiency) is robust across parameter sweeps. The paper does not provide precise quantitative predictions for efficiency, but shows that the effect is substantial and positive under a wide range of conditions.

- source: Kol'veková, G., Raisová, M., Zoricak, M., & Gazda, V. (2021). Endogenous Shared Punishment Model in Threshold Public Goods Games. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The endogenous shared punishment model, where agents vote on the level and cost of punishment, achieves higher mean payoffs (efficiency) than both the baseline (no punishment) and the cooperation (full punishment) models. In the standard scenario (group size 4, MPCR 0.5, threshold 60, endowment 30), the endogenous shared punishment model yields a weighted mean reward of 40.09, compared to 33.42 for the baseline and 35.04 for the cooperation model. The provision rate is 73.8% for the endogenous shared punishment model, 24.7% for the baseline, and 98.2% for the cooperation model. The endogenous shared punishment model achieves this efficiency at a very low cost of punishment, as pro-punishment votes and punishment costs decline over time as cooperation stabilizes. Adding 'activists' (altruistic contributors) further increases provision rates and mean payoffs. The model's results are robust to variations in group size, threshold, MPCR, and learning parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a low-cost, endogenous, shared punishment mechanism in threshold public goods games can substantially increase efficiency (mean group payoff) compared to both no-punishment and full-punishment regimes. The efficiency gains are achieved even when the provision rate is not maximized, due to the low cost of punishment and the stabilization of cooperation. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in TPGG-like environments, especially when punishment is endogenous, low-cost, and shared. The results are most applicable to games with group size 4, MPCR 0.5, and threshold 60, but the model explores a range of parameters. The paper's agent-based approach and focus on payoff outcomes make it highly informative for the prediction task.

- source: Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2016). How to keep punishment to maintain cooperation: Introducing social vaccine. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the meta-norms game, costly punishment and meta-punishment alone are insufficient to robustly maintain cooperation and high group efficiency, due to the vulnerability to second-order free-riding. However, the coercive introduction of a small number of always-defecting agents (the 'social vaccine') stabilizes the punishment system, prevents the drift toward non-punishing cooperators, and maintains a highly cooperative regime. This results in average payoffs for the group that are close to those of a fully cooperative group, even in parameter regions where cooperation would otherwise collapse. The effect is robust to changes in population size, temptation to defect, and punishment cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with meta-norms (second-order punishment), the presence of a small number of persistent defectors can dramatically increase the efficiency of the punishment regime, maintaining high group payoffs where they would otherwise collapse. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the stability and efficiency of punishment-enabled games may depend not only on the formal punishment parameters but also on the presence of persistent norm violators. In the absence of such 'social vaccine' agents, efficiency gains from punishment may be fragile or short-lived, especially in longer games or with low punishment costs. The findings are most directly relevant to games with meta-punishment and compulsory participation, and may be less directly transferable to standard linear PGGs without meta-norms.

- source: POLLOCK, G. B. (1988). POPULATION-STRUCTURE, SPITE, AND THE ITERATED PRISONERS-DILEMMA. *American Journal of Physical Anthropology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that punishment-based strategies (TFT/NTFT) can maintain high group efficiency and suppress defection when the population is structured into multiple groups or dyads, allowing intergroup competition. The effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation and high payoffs is reduced as the scope of spite (the number of groups a defector can affect) increases or as population structure becomes less partitioned. In the limit of a single group (no structure), punishment cannot prevent defection and efficiency collapses. The analysis generalizes to n-person games, showing that the same logic applies: partitioned populations with group-level competition allow punishment to sustain cooperation and high efficiency, while unstructured populations do not.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments will increase efficiency (group payoff) relative to control, but only when the game design includes sufficient population structure (multiple groups, limited overlap in group membership, or mechanisms for intergroup competition). If the game is a single group or highly overlapping, punishment will not improve efficiency. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the marginal per-capita return, group size, and population structure can be specified, and they highlight the importance of these dimensions as moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Sethi, R., & Somanathan, E. (2003). Understanding reciprocity. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment and reciprocity can support efficient outcomes in both repeated and sporadic interactions, but only under certain conditions. In repeated games, strategies that support cooperation (and thus efficiency) can be stable if complexity costs or implementation errors are considered, but many inefficient strategies can also be stable. In one-shot or sporadic interactions, punishment can induce cooperation and improve efficiency, but this stability is fragile: populations of punishers can be invaded by non-punishing cooperators or defectors, and the cost of punishment can erode its effectiveness. In public goods and common pool resource games, the presence of reciprocators willing to punish can lead to higher group payoffs, but only if monitoring and punishment costs are not too high and if group composition allows reciprocators to coordinate. The evolution of mixed populations (reciprocators and defectors) is common, and the efficiency gains from punishment depend on the game structure and population dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games can increase efficiency relative to no-punishment baselines, but only under specific conditions: punishment must be sufficiently effective (high impact per cost), not too costly, and the population must contain enough reciprocators or the ability to assort. The effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive; in some models, punishment does not prevent the evolution of inefficient outcomes, and the stability of efficient cooperation is sensitive to the cost and observability of punishment, as well as to the possibility of second-order free-riding. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, group size, and the ability to observe or assort by type. Control efficiency (without punishment) is often low, but the introduction of punishment can raise efficiency if these moderating conditions are met.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Liu, Z. X., Sun, Q. L., & Chen, Z. Q. (2015). Altruism in multiplayer snowdrift games with threshold and punishment. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Theoretical results show that introducing punishment for defectors in threshold N-person snowdrift games increases the equilibrium fraction of cooperators, especially when punishment is always applied regardless of group success. Stronger punishment and higher cooperation thresholds further increase cooperation. However, the model does not include costs for administering punishment, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant for understanding how punishment rules and thresholds affect cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in threshold public goods-like games, especially when punishment is always applied and is strong. However, because the model assumes costless punishment and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment regime, threshold, and punishment strength affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency in some settings, but the lack of punishment cost and direct efficiency outcomes limits predictive value for efficiency.

- source: Chung, N. N., Chew, L. Y., & Lai, C. H. (2013). Influence of network structure on cooperative dynamics in coupled socio-ecological systems. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study extends a previous analytical model of social sanction (ostracism) in common pool resource extraction to populations with complex network structures. It finds that, contrary to some prior work, cooperation and resource sustainability are promoted by higher network connectivity when social sanction is present. In networks with fewer social ties, defectors can cluster and avoid sanction, leading to lower cooperation and lower equilibrium resource levels. The model shows that the equilibrium resource level (a proxy for group efficiency) is higher in well-mixed or highly connected networks, and lower in sparsely connected ones. The findings are robust across different network topologies (random, scale-free, community-structured) and network sizes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in public-goods-like common pool resource games with social sanction (ostracism), increasing the average number of social ties (network degree) increases the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and sustaining higher resource levels (efficiency). For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, all else equal, games with higher player connectivity or more opportunities for sanctioning will see a greater positive effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper does not provide direct experimental data, but its results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in networked or structured populations.

- source: Hua, S. J., & Liu, L. J. (2023). Governance of risky public goods under the threat of ostracism. *Physica D-nonlinear Phenomena*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a collective risk dilemma (CRD) model, local exclusion (ostracism) is more effective than local punishment in promoting group achievement (i.e., the fraction of groups that reach the collective target), especially at low risk levels. Local sanctioning institutions (both punishment and exclusion) are more effective than global ones. The advantage of exclusion over punishment is most pronounced when the risk of collective failure is low. When risk is high, both exclusion and punishment lead to high group achievement. The results are based on theoretical analysis and simulation of evolutionary dynamics, not on direct measurement of efficiency or group payoff, but group achievement is a closely related outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in threshold public goods environments with risk of collective loss (CRD), enabling local punishment or exclusion mechanisms increases group achievement (a proxy for efficiency) compared to no sanctions, with local exclusion (ostracism) being especially effective at low risk. The results suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment (especially local, exclusionary forms) is likely to increase efficiency or group success, particularly when the risk of failure is not too high. However, the findings are based on group achievement rather than explicit efficiency ratios, and are derived from theoretical models rather than empirical data. The model parameters (e.g., group size, cost of exclusion, risk level) are specified, allowing some mapping to prediction dimensions.

- source: Whitmeyer, J. M. (2004). The group control catastrophe. *Sociological Perspectives*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in a simulated environment where an external agent can sanction group members (individually or collectively) to induce compliance (a public good), increasing monitoring efficacy does not always monotonically increase compliance. Instead, for intermediate monitoring efficacy, compliance can collapse to zero, creating a bifurcation with two possible equilibria (full compliance or full defection), depending on initial conditions (e.g., the number of initial cooperators). The 'catastrophe' is robust across a range of group sizes, sanction levels, internal control efficacies, and production function shapes (except linear). The equilibrium with high compliance yields higher payoffs for group members than the low-compliance equilibrium. The cost to the agent of obtaining compliance can be minimized by using only one type of sanction (individual or collective) and by leveraging initial cooperation to reach the high-compliance equilibrium, especially at moderate monitoring efficacy. However, the model does not directly report efficiency in the sense of group payoff relative to the maximum possible.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (sanctions) on group compliance and payoffs in public-goods-like environments is highly non-linear and can depend critically on monitoring efficacy, initial conditions, and the structure of sanctions. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling punishment does not guarantee higher efficiency or group payoff: under some parameter regimes (especially intermediate monitoring efficacy), punishment can lead to catastrophic drops in compliance and thus lower payoffs. The model also highlights the importance of initial cooperation and the potential for multiple equilibria. However, because the paper does not directly report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, and because it is a simulation/theory paper rather than an empirical study, its findings should be used to inform qualitative expectations about non-monotonic and path-dependent effects of punishment, rather than to provide quantitative predictions.

- source: Gardner, A., & West, S. A. (2004). Cooperation and punishment, especially in humans. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment can promote and stabilize cooperation in public-goods-like environments, leading to higher group fitness (efficiency), provided that individuals who punish tend to receive more cooperation. The cost of punishment decreases as cooperation increases, making punishment more sustainable in cooperative populations. Relatedness between individuals is not required for punishment to be favored; instead, the key is a positive association between an individual's punishment and the cooperation she receives. The model predicts that punishment is more easily maintained than initially evolved, and that small group size and effective punishment (where the cost of being punished exceeds the cost of cooperation) facilitate the evolutionary stability of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in public-goods-like games can increase efficiency, especially when punishment is effective (i.e., the penalty for being punished is greater than the cost of cooperation) and when there is a mechanism (such as behavioral adjustment) that links punishment to increased cooperation received. The findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing punishment should increase group efficiency, particularly in smaller groups and when punishment is not too costly to the punisher. However, the results are derived from theoretical models rather than empirical data, so quantitative predictions require caution. The paper also highlights that the effect of punishment is not dependent on relatedness or repeated interactions, but on the structure of behavioral associations.

- source: Janssen, M. A. (2015). A behavioral perspective on the governance of common resources. *Current Opinion in Environmental Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: chat
  findings: The paper finds that, contrary to the predictions of standard economic theory, groups can achieve higher efficiency and avoid the tragedy of the commons when allowed to communicate or implement costly punishment. Both lab and field experiments show that these mechanisms increase group earnings and reduce overuse of resources. The review also notes that centralized sanctioning mechanisms may be more effective and sustainable than peer punishment. However, the effectiveness of punishment and other interventions is context-specific, depending on factors such as group size, communication, trust, and cultural background. The paper also discusses the phenomenon of 'crowding out,' where externally imposed rules can sometimes reduce performance compared to voluntary agreements.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment in public-goods-game-like environments generally increases group efficiency or earnings, especially when compared to baseline games without punishment or communication. It also highlights that the effect of punishment is moderated by contextual factors such as group size, communication, and the nature of the sanctioning institution (peer vs. centralized). While the review does not provide quantitative estimates, it offers strong qualitative evidence that punishment mechanisms, when available, tend to improve efficiency outcomes, but the magnitude and sustainability of these effects depend on the specific game design and social context.

- source: Li, Q. R., Li, Y. Q., Li, K., Chen, L., Zheng, Q., & Chen, K. (2020). Study on the influence of subjective well-being on travel mode selection. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing both punishment and reward mechanisms, based on subjective well-being thresholds, greatly increases the proportion of travelers choosing the cooperative (low-carbon) option. Punishment is found to be more effective than reward in stabilizing high levels of cooperation. The ease of triggering incentives (by narrowing the threshold interval) further boosts cooperation. However, the study does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare; all results are in terms of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment (and to a lesser extent, reward) mechanisms can substantially increase cooperation rates in a PGG-like setting. However, because the model and results focus on cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency outcomes. The model structure and parameterization (e.g., cost and effectiveness of punishment/reward) are informative for understanding how design dimensions might affect cooperation, but not directly efficiency.

- source: Yan, R. Y., Xu, M. Q., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). Evolution of cooperation under pure institutional reward and punishment in collective-risk social dilemma games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing institutional pure punishment or pure reward into a collective-risk social dilemma game can promote cooperation, but with important differences.
