Pure reward leads to stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, with the domain of coexistence expanding as reward intensity increases. Pure punishment produces more complex dynamics, including monostable, bistable, tristable, and quadristable outcomes, and can more effectively suppress free-riding when punishment intensity is high or the implementation threshold is low. In finite populations, lower thresholds for implementing incentives (reward or punishment) promote cooperation, while higher thresholds favor defection. The cost and intensity of incentives modulate these effects. However, the paper does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the evolutionary stability and prevalence of cooperative strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of institutional punishment on cooperation in collective-risk public goods games, especially regarding how punishment intensity, cost, and implementation threshold affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence on group efficiency or total payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to inferring that higher cooperation rates (under certain punishment regimes) are likely to be associated with higher efficiency, assuming the mapping from cooperation to efficiency is known. The results are most relevant for settings with institutional (not peer) punishment, nonlinear threshold public goods, and no direct cost of cooperation for punishers/rewarders. The findings suggest that lower punishment thresholds and higher punishment intensity (relative to cost) are more likely to promote cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if cooperation is strongly linked to group payoff.

- source: Prietula, M. J., & Conway, D. (2009). The evolution of metanorms: quis custodiet ipsos custodes?. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based simulations of a public-goods-like dilemma, the addition of metanorms (punishing those who do not punish defectors) is necessary for the emergence of a behavioral norm against defection. When only first-order punishment is available, defection dominates. When metanorms are present, defection rates drop and punishment rates rise, indicating norm emergence. Decoupling the strategies for punishing defectors and non-punishers does not prevent norm emergence and can reduce the overall level of punishment required. In group-structured games with unequal power, metanorms alone do not produce a norm against defection; only with decoupled sanctioning structures does the strong group enforce a norm against the weak group's defection, leading to group dominance rather than universal cooperation. The outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral strategy values (boldness, vengefulness), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the presence of punishment and especially metanorms (second-order punishment) can dramatically reduce defection rates in public-goods-like environments, even when punishment is costly. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral measures (defection, punishment) rather than efficiency or group payoff, so direct quantitative prediction of efficiency is not possible. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (especially with metanorms) is likely to increase efficiency in analogous empirical games, but the magnitude and exact efficiency outcomes are not reported. The paper also highlights that group structure and the sophistication of the punishment mechanism (e.g., decoupling punishment types) can moderate the effect, sometimes leading to group dominance rather than overall cooperation.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, X. K., & Wang, X. J. (2018). Continuous spatial public goods game with self and peer punishment based on particle swarm optimization. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both self-punishment (modeled as a fitness penalty for contributing less than peers) and peer punishment (costly punishment of low contributors by others) substantially increase the average cooperation level in a continuous spatial PGG. Lower tolerance for low contributions and higher punishment intensity both promote higher cooperation. The effect is robust across a range of memory and imitation parameters. However, the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so it is unclear whether the increased cooperation translates into higher group efficiency, especially given the costs of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that both self-punishment and peer punishment mechanisms can increase average cooperation rates in continuous spatial PGGs, especially when tolerance is low and punishment intensity is high. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The cost of punishment is modeled and subtracted from individual fitness, but the aggregate effect on group payoff or efficiency is not reported. Thus, while the paper supports the idea that punishment increases cooperation, it does not directly inform whether punishment increases or decreases efficiency in these settings.

- source: Xu, M., Zheng, D. F., Xu, C., Zhong, L. X., & Hui, P. M. (2015). Cooperative behavior in N-person evolutionary snowdrift games with punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing costly punishment into an N-person snowdrift game (a close variant of the public goods game) can suppress non-cooperators and promote a cooperative population, but only under certain conditions: low cost-to-benefit ratio, large group size, and strong punishment effectiveness. In these cases, the system reaches a 'frozen' phase with only cooperators and punishers. If the punishment is weak, the cost-to-benefit ratio is high, or the group size is small, punishers are eliminated and the system remains a mix of cooperators and non-cooperators. The model is analyzed using replicator dynamics and confirmed by simulation. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the elimination of non-cooperators implies higher group performance in those parameter regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in N-person public-goods-like games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the steady-state elimination of non-cooperators in the presence of strong punishment, large group size, and low cost-to-benefit ratio suggests that efficiency would be higher in these regimes when punishment is enabled. Conversely, when punishment is weak, costly, or the group is small, punishment does not improve cooperation and likely does not improve efficiency. The findings are most relevant for settings where the game structure is similar to the N-person snowdrift game (cost shared among cooperators, task completed if at least one cooperator exists), which is close but not identical to the standard public goods game.

- source: Nhim, T., Richter, A., & Zhu, X. Q. (2019). The resilience of social norms of cooperation under resource scarcity and inequality - An agent-based model on sharing water over two harvesting seasons. *Ecological Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that strong punishment (high punishment strength) and high social capital (frequent encounters) support the emergence and resilience of cooperation in a common-pool resource dilemma. However, resource scarcity, high variability in resource inflow, and increased inequality (in land or skill) undermine cooperation, making collapse of social norms more likely. The system tends toward either full cooperation or full defection, with little evidence for stable mixed equilibria. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the collapse of cooperation is interpreted as a breakdown of socially optimal resource use.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment can sustain cooperation in public-goods-like resource dilemmas, but its effectiveness is strongly moderated by resource scarcity, variability, and inequality. The model is not a direct PGG but is structurally similar, with a focus on seasonal water allocation and peer punishment. The main outcome is the presence or absence of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff, so the paper is most useful for predicting the conditions under which punishment will be effective at sustaining cooperation, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency gains. The findings suggest that in environments with high scarcity, variability, or inequality, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency (if any) is likely to be weaker or absent.

- source: Qian, J., Sun, X., Zhang, T. D., & Chai, Y. T. (2022). Authority or Autonomy? Exploring Interactions between Central and Peer Punishments in Risk-Resistant Scenarios. *Entropy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that both central and peer punishment can promote group collaboration in risk-resistant (threshold) scenarios, with peer punishment generally having a stronger effect, especially as risk increases. Central punishment can suppress the effect of peer punishment at all risk levels, while peer punishment only constrains central punishment at high risk. Resource wastage from punishment is generally small compared to the loss from group failure, but increases with peer punishment intensity and risk. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff in the standard sense, focusing instead on collaboration rates and resource wastage.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It supports the idea that punishment (both central and peer) increases group collaboration in threshold public goods-like environments, and that the effectiveness and interaction of punishment types depend on risk level. However, because the main outcome is collaboration rate (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the model is theoretical/simulation-based rather than empirical, its direct predictive value for efficiency is limited. The resource wastage analysis suggests that high peer punishment can lead to diminishing returns or inefficiency at high risk. The model's all-or-nothing threshold structure and explicit manipulation of punishment cost and effectiveness are informative for mapping to game design dimensions.

- source: Lehmann, L., Rousset, F., Roze, D., & Keller, L. (2007). Strong reciprocity or strong ferocity? A population genetic view of the evolution of altruistic punishment. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity (costly helping plus costly punishment) can evolve in spatially structured populations if helping and punishment are tightly linked (no recombination) and relatedness is sufficiently high. In this case, strong reciprocity can invade even when rare. When helping and punishment are unlinked (recombination is strong), neither costly helping nor costly punishment can invade from rarity unless punishment yields a direct fitness benefit to the actor. The model shows that kin selection (genetic or cultural) is necessary for the evolution of strong reciprocity. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide direct predictions about group efficiency or payoff, but rather about the evolutionary stability and spread of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the evolutionary preconditions for the emergence of punishment and cooperation in public-goods-like settings, especially the importance of linkage between helping and punishment, population structure (relatedness), and direct fitness effects. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about group efficiency or payoff with and without punishment, nor does it report outcomes directly comparable to experimental public goods games. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting that punishment is unlikely to increase cooperation or efficiency in anonymous, unstructured, or one-shot settings unless there is kinship or direct benefit to punishers. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Henrich, J., & Henrich, N. (2006). Culture, evolution and the puzzle of human cooperation. *Cognitive Systems Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper argues that punishment, especially when combined with cultural learning mechanisms (such as conformist and prestige-biased transmission), can stabilize costly cooperative norms in human groups. It reviews theoretical models showing that the effectiveness of punishment in maintaining cooperation depends on the cost of punishing relative to the cost of being punished, and that social norms enforced by punishment can persist even when the cooperative act is individually costly. The paper also discusses how group-level selection can favor groups with more effective cooperative norms, and how cultural evolution allows for rapid adaptation of cooperation strategies. However, the paper does not provide empirical or quantitative findings on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on the mechanisms and evolutionary logic.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment can stabilize cooperation in public goods-like environments, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly relative to the cost of being punished. It highlights the importance of social norms, cultural learning, and group structure in moderating the effects of punishment. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the magnitude of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. Its value for prediction lies in its mechanistic insights and identification of key moderators (e.g., punishment cost/effectiveness, group size, norm strength), but not in providing direct outcome estimates.

- source: Declerck, C. H., Boone, C., & Emonds, G. (2013). When do people cooperate? The neuroeconomics of prosocial decision making. *Brain and Cognition*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper proposes a dual-process neural framework for prosocial decision-making in social dilemmas, including public goods games and related paradigms. It reviews evidence that extrinsic incentives (such as punishment) and trust signals independently increase cooperation. Neuroimaging studies show that punishment threats activate cognitive control regions and increase cooperative behavior, particularly among self-regarding or Machiavellian individuals. The review also highlights that individual differences (e.g., social value orientation, Machiavellianism) moderate the impact of punishment and incentives on cooperation. However, the paper does not directly report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and neural responses.
  prediction_guidance: This review provides strong theoretical and mechanistic support for the idea that punishment increases cooperation in public-goods-game-like environments, especially for self-regarding individuals. It suggests that the effect of punishment is likely to be more pronounced when extrinsic incentives are salient and among participants with self-regarding preferences. However, because the review does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its value for prediction is primarily in identifying moderators (e.g., individual differences, presence of incentives, trust signals) and in supporting the general mechanism by which punishment increases cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is best used to inform the likely direction and moderators of punishment effects, but not to calibrate the magnitude of efficiency changes.

- source: Eckel, C., & Gintis, H. (2010). Blaming the messenger: Notes on the current state of experimental economics. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that a large body of experimental evidence supports the existence of other-regarding preferences, such as altruistic punishment, in games like the public goods game with punishment. It reviews findings that low contributors are frequently punished, even in late rounds and in stranger treatments, indicating that punishment is used to enforce cooperation. The authors also discuss the robustness of these behaviors to changes in stakes and context, and the importance of social norms. However, the paper does not present new data or directly analyze efficiency or group payoff; instead, it focuses on the behavioral mechanisms and theoretical implications.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation in public goods games, but it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency effects. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and robustness of punishment and cooperation, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment is likely to increase cooperation, but does not allow for direct inference about efficiency or payoff changes.

- source: Sasaki, T., & Uchida, S. (2014). Rewards and the evolution of cooperation in public good games. *Biology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public good games, voluntary reward funds can evolve and lead to efficient cooperation, overcoming coordination problems that typically prevent the evolution of cooperation in large, anonymous groups. The model shows that, regardless of initial conditions, rewarders can invade a population of defectors if the reward fund's return is sufficiently high, leading to a cascade where cooperators eventually dominate. The resulting group payoff and average fitness are higher with rewards than without, and the system can reach full cooperation or stable mixed equilibria depending on the risk and threshold parameters. The paper contrasts this with institutional punishment, which is argued to be less effective or more difficult to initiate in large groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing a reward mechanism in threshold public good games will increase group efficiency and average payoff, especially in settings where coordination is otherwise difficult. While it does not directly analyze punishment, it offers indirect evidence that reward mechanisms can be more effective than punishment in some coordination contexts. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling a reward mechanism (with sufficient effectiveness and sustainability) should increase efficiency relative to control. The model's explicit treatment of group size, threshold, risk, and reward parameters can inform how these dimensions moderate the effect of rewards on efficiency.

- source: Yang, L. H., Xu, Z. J., Zhang, L. Z., & Yang, D. X. (2018). Strategy intervention in spatial voluntary public goods games. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This simulation study finds that a centralized intervention mechanism (distinct from peer or pool punishment) can increase the average total payoff of the group and reduce income inequality, provided the intervention is sufficiently strong (i.e., the product of intervention degree and intensity exceeds a threshold). Weak intervention can actually reduce cooperation and group payoff compared to no intervention. The model provides explicit parameter ranges for when full cooperation and maximum efficiency are achieved. The intervention cost is shared by all, and the mechanism is implemented by a central authority, not by individuals punishing each other.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that centralized, institutionally-enforced interventions (akin to pool punishment but implemented as forced contributions rather than direct payoff reduction) can increase efficiency in spatial voluntary PGGs, but only if the intervention is strong enough. The effect is non-monotonic: weak intervention can be counterproductive. The findings are most relevant for settings with voluntary participation, spatial structure, and centralized enforcement. The explicit mapping between intervention parameters and efficiency outcomes can inform predictions about the efficiency impact of institutional punishment-like mechanisms, especially when the control (no intervention) efficiency is low. However, the mechanism is not standard punishment, so direct transfer to peer punishment settings should be cautious.

- source: Min, Y., Du, Y. C., & Jin, C. (2018). The effect of link rewiring on a coevolutionary common pool resource game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to rewire their links away from defectors (link rewiring) in a CPR game can prevent the collapse of cooperation and resource depletion, especially in sparsely connected networks and when resource inflow is high. The model uses a costless social ostracism mechanism (reputation-based exclusion) rather than costly punishment. The main reported outcomes are the fraction of cooperators and resource abundance, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that adaptive network structure (partner switching) can be a powerful mechanism for sustaining cooperation, but the effects depend on network density and ecological parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of costly punishment on efficiency in public goods or CPR games. Instead, it analyzes a costless ostracism/reputation mechanism and the impact of adaptive network rewiring. While the findings are relevant for understanding how social structure and reputation can support cooperation, they do not inform predictions about the efficiency effects of enabling costly punishment. The model and results are adjacent to the prediction task, offering indirect insight into the role of network adaptation and costless sanctions, but not the direct impact of costly punishment on efficiency.

- source: Du, J. M., Wu, B., & Wang, L. (2014). Climate collective risk dilemma with feedback of real-time temperatures. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The model demonstrates that coupling real-time environmental feedback (temperature) to payoffs in a threshold public goods game can promote and stabilize cooperation. When the public good threshold is not met, temperature rises, which reduces all players' payoffs (environmental punishment). This feedback leads to cyclical dynamics where cooperation rebounds as payoffs fall, stabilizing temperature near a reference level. The model outperforms versions without feedback in both temperature control and cooperation rates. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, and the 'punishment' is environmental rather than peer-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The 'punishment' mechanism is environmental (temperature rise reducing payoffs for all), not a peer or institutional punishment option. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but shows that environmental feedback can stabilize cooperation and temperature. The findings suggest that automatic, group-level sanctions (environmental feedback) can promote cooperation, but the results may not transfer directly to settings with explicit punishment options. Use this paper for contextual or mechanistic insight, not for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Song, Q. Q., Li, Z. P., Fu, C. H., & Wang, L. S. (2011). Optional contributions have positive effects for volunteering public goods games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing an option for low contributions in volunteering public goods games (VPG) increases the average payoff of the population (i.e., efficiency), compared to the standard VPG with only a single contribution level. This effect is observed in both well-mixed and spatially structured populations, and is especially pronounced when the multiplication factor (r) is above a threshold that depends weakly on group size. The mechanism is that the low-contribution option induces more players to cooperate, which in turn increases the number of high contributors as well, leading to higher overall payoffs. The results are based on theoretical analysis and extensive simulations of evolutionary dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in VPG-like public goods games, allowing optional (especially low) contributions can increase group efficiency compared to a single, higher contribution requirement. While the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, it is highly informative for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the contribution structure (e.g., all-or-nothing vs. continuous, or optional vs. required). The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, introducing more flexible contribution options (even without punishment) can increase efficiency, especially when the MPCR is above a moderate threshold. However, the paper does not address the effects of punishment, so it is not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Liu, L. J., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Evolution of Public Cooperation in a Risky Society with Heterogeneous Assets. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that asset heterogeneity (a large gap between rich and poor) hinders cooperation, regardless of the temptation to defect. Increasing the value of individual assets or the risk of collective loss promotes cooperation. High thresholds and low risk can destroy cooperation, especially when asset inequality is high. The proportion of poor in the population also affects cooperation: when the poor are a small minority, they tend to free ride; when they are a majority, more contribute. The model does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or group payoff. However, it is informative about how asset heterogeneity, risk, and threshold affect cooperation rates in threshold public goods games with collective risk. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may provide contextual insight into how structural features (like risk and heterogeneity) affect cooperation in the absence of punishment.

- source: Janus, T., & Lim, J. J. (2009). Sticks and carrots: Two incentive mechanisms supporting intra-group cooperation. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper proves that in a repeated three-player prisoner's dilemma, the introduction of either collective punishment (Nash reversion by all after any defection) or reward mechanisms (transfers contingent on cooperation) can sustain full cooperation as a subgame perfect equilibrium, even when bilateral incentive compatibility constraints are violated. This means that group efficiency (welfare) can be maintained at the cooperative level under broader conditions than in the absence of such mechanisms. The results are general and apply to settings where players interact repeatedly and can observe each other's actions, and where the game is either infinite or has an unknown end point.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that enabling punishment (or reward) mechanisms in repeated social dilemma games can increase group efficiency by sustaining cooperation, even when cooperation would not be sustainable without such mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially collective punishment with Nash reversion) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, provided players are sufficiently patient (high discount factor) and can observe actions. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it is a repeated three-player prisoner's dilemma), so the results are most directly applicable to small-group, repeated, fully observable settings. The paper does not provide empirical effect sizes or parameterized predictions, but it does establish that the direction of effect is positive for efficiency when punishment is enabled.

- source: Haag, M., & Lagunoff, R. (2006). Social norms, local interaction, and neighborhood planning. *International Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of repeated cooperation enforced by punishment depends critically on the network structure and the distribution of individual patience (discount factors). In maximally connected cliques, punishment is most effective due to perfect monitoring, and full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) is sustainable if all individuals are sufficiently patient. However, when some individuals are impatient (low discount factor), cliques can lead to cascades of defection and lower welfare. In such cases, less connected networks (e.g., grids) can isolate the effects of uncooperative types, allowing more tolerant individuals to absorb free riding and maintain higher overall welfare. The optimal network design thus balances the benefits of effective punishment (which requires connectivity and observability) against the costs of social conflict from connecting intolerant and uncooperative types. The paper provides explicit formulas for expected welfare under different network designs and type distributions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in repeated local public goods or Prisoner's Dilemma environments as a function of network structure, monitoring, and heterogeneity in patience. It shows that punishment is most effective (and efficiency is highest) in small, maximally connected groups with perfect monitoring and homogeneous patience. As heterogeneity increases, or as monitoring becomes more local/imperfect, the efficiency gains from punishment can be reduced or even reversed, with less connected networks sometimes yielding higher welfare. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by network connectivity, monitoring structure, and the distribution of patience among players. However, the results are theoretical and assume rational, forward-looking agents using trigger strategies; they do not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Matsushima, H. (2012). FINITELY REPEATED PRISONERS' DILEMMA WITH SMALL FINES: THE PENANCE CONTRACT. *Japanese Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper proves that with a suitable contract that imposes a fine for deviation in the final period, full cooperation (i.e., maximal efficiency) can be uniquely sustained in finitely repeated prisoners' dilemma games. The required fine can be small relative to total payoffs if the game is long enough. The penance contract is robust to renegotiation, unlike trigger contracts. The result generalizes to other two-player games with similar payoff structure. The findings are theoretical and show that the design of the punishment mechanism (timing, size, and contingency) is crucial for achieving efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in two-player finitely repeated social dilemma games, the introduction of a well-designed punishment mechanism (specifically, a contract that imposes a fine for final-period deviation) can move the group from inefficient (all-defect) equilibria to fully efficient (all-cooperate) equilibria. The effect is robust to renegotiation if the punishment is contingent on final-period behavior and the fine is large enough to outweigh the gain from deviation. The result is directly relevant for predicting that enabling such a punishment mechanism will increase efficiency to the maximum possible, provided the contract is enforceable and the fine is sufficiently large. However, the result is limited to two-player, finitely repeated, contractible games and does not address larger groups or public goods games directly.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2017). Asymmetric social norms. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games with heterogeneous players (random productivity), efficient outcomes may require some players to defect in certain matches. In such settings, standard public monitoring is insufficient to sustain efficiency, but contagious punishment strategies—where players switch to permanent defection after observing a deviation—can support high payoffs if the group is sufficiently patient, punishment is severe enough, and productivity differences are not too large. The paper provides explicit conditions under which these strategies are equilibrium and efficiency is achieved, and derives closed-form expressions for continuation payoffs. Thus, punishment (in the form of contagious, community-based sanctions) can enable high efficiency even in complex, asymmetric environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms (specifically, contagious punishment) can increase efficiency in repeated social dilemma games, even when the efficient outcome involves some defections due to heterogeneity. The results are most directly applicable to repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with random productivity, but the mechanisms and equilibrium conditions are relevant for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency in public-goods-like environments with heterogeneity. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or direct mappings to standard PGG parameters, but it does clarify that punishment can sustain high efficiency if players are patient, punishment is strong, and heterogeneity is moderate. For prediction, this suggests that enabling punishment in heterogeneous repeated games is likely to increase efficiency, provided the punishment is sufficiently severe and the group is not too heterogeneous.

- source: Quan, J., Yang, X. K., Wang, X. J., Yang, J. B., Wu, K. B., & Dai, Z. L. (2019). Withhold-judgment and punishment promote cooperation in indirect reciprocity under incomplete information. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of punishment in indirect reciprocity models with incomplete information generally increases cooperation rates, but its effect on efficiency (average payoff) depends on the social norm and parameter settings. In some cases, especially when punishment is frequent and costly, the net effect on social welfare is negative, even as cooperation rises. Social norms that allow observers to withhold judgment (not update reputation) in ambiguous cases are more robust, especially under private reputation and noisy/incomplete information, and can maintain both high cooperation and high average payoff. The effect of punishment is thus context-dependent: it can either improve or harm efficiency depending on the interplay of punishment cost, observation probability, and the social norm in use.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments is not universally positive: while punishment reliably increases cooperation, it can reduce efficiency if the cost of punishment is high or if punishment is overused. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related (donation game with indirect reciprocity and group-level outcomes). The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, one must consider not only the presence of punishment but also its cost, the effectiveness of punishment, the information structure (public vs private reputation), and the social norm governing reputation updates. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not always a reliable predictor of treatment efficiency (with punishment), as punishment can sometimes lower group welfare despite increasing cooperation.

- source: Baker, S., & Choi, A. H. (2018). Reputation and Litigation: Why Costly Legal Sanctions Can Work Better than Reputational Sanctions. *Journal of Legal Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that legal sanctions (punishment) can be more efficient than reputational sanctions (boycotts) in deterring undesirable behavior, even when both are costly. This is because legal sanctions have an inframarginal effect: increasing damages not only deters more lawsuits at the margin but also increases the penalty for existing lawsuits, improving deterrence at lower cost. Reputational sanctions lack this inframarginal effect, so increasing their severity is less efficient. The efficiency advantage of legal sanctions is greatest when courts are accurate and when litigation outcomes are public. When court error is high or when consumers have independent, reliable information about quality, reputational sanctions become more important. The model predicts that the optimal regime combines both types of sanctions, but leans toward legal sanctions unless litigation is highly error-prone or costly. The analysis is theoretical and does not provide experimental or empirical estimates, but it offers clear comparative statics and policy implications for the efficiency of punishment mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (legal sanctions) in repeated moral hazard environments can increase efficiency relative to relying solely on reputational sanctions, especially when punishment is not too costly or error-prone and when outcomes are public. The inframarginal effect of punishment means that its efficiency benefit can be greater than that of reputational sanctions, which only deter at the margin. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment should increase efficiency, particularly when the punishment mechanism is accurate and visible. However, if punishment is highly error-prone or costly, or if reputational information is highly reliable, the efficiency gain from punishment may be reduced or even reversed. The model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution, but the qualitative comparative statics are clear and relevant.

- source: Melkonyan, T., Zeitoun, H., & Chater, N. (2022). The cognitive foundations of tacit commitments: A virtual bargaining model of dynamic interactions. *Journal of Mathematical Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, in dynamic games without external enforcement, the possibility of tacit commitment to punishment (as formalized by the virtual bargaining equilibrium) enables self-interested agents to achieve efficient, mutually beneficial outcomes that are not predicted by standard subgame perfect equilibrium. In games like the finitely repeated Prisoner's Dilemma, Centipede, and repeated Bertrand competition, VBE supports cooperation, collusion, or turn-taking, enforced by credible threats of punishment (even if costly). This leads to higher group payoffs and efficiency compared to the standard prediction. However, if players cannot commit to future punishment (SVBE), the threat is not credible and efficiency collapses to the Nash/subgame perfect level.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling credible punishment (even if costly to the punisher) can substantially increase group efficiency in repeated or sequential social dilemma games, relative to control conditions where punishment is not possible or not credible. The model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, but the logic applies to adjacent environments (e.g., repeated PD, Centipede, repeated Bertrand) that share key features with public goods games. The findings suggest that, when game design allows for credible commitment to punishment, efficiency is likely to be much higher than in control games without such commitment. The effect is robust to the absence of external enforcement or non-standard preferences. However, the model assumes agents reason as virtual bargainers; if they do not, or if commitment is not possible, efficiency gains disappear. The paper is most informative for settings where punishment is possible, costly, and can be credibly pre-committed.

- source: Yan, F., Chen, X. J., Qiu, Z. P., & Szolnoki, A. (2021). Cooperator driven oscillation in a time-delayed feedback-evolving game. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment (probabilistic detection and fine for defectors) can stabilize cooperation and maintain the common resource at sustainable levels, but only if the product of detection probability and fine (pβ) is sufficiently large relative to the cost of cooperation or the reinvestment rate. When punishment is strong, the system converges to a stable, efficient state with high cooperation and resource sustainability. When punishment is weak, defectors dominate and the resource is depleted. If cooperator reinvestment is proportional to endowment and there is a time delay in environmental feedback, the system can exhibit persistent oscillations in cooperation and resource levels, especially when punishment is moderate and resource growth is not too fast or too slow. The critical threshold for punishment effectiveness and the impact of time delay are analytically characterized. The model provides explicit conditions under which punishment improves or fails to improve group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for how institutional punishment (with known cost and effectiveness) affects group efficiency in a common-pool resource game with feedback dynamics. The results are most relevant for adjacent PGG-like environments with renewable resources and environmental feedback. The model gives explicit formulas for the stationary group payoff/resource level as a function of punishment parameters, player count, resource growth, and reinvestment structure. For prediction, the key insight is that increasing punishment effectiveness (higher pβ) generally increases efficiency up to a threshold, above which full cooperation and sustainability are achieved. However, if punishment is too weak, efficiency remains low. Time delay in feedback can destabilize the system and induce oscillations, but does not always reduce average efficiency. The findings are most transferable to settings with institutional punishment, renewable resources, and feedback between group action and group payoff.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2025). Cooperation in temporary partnerships. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with random matching and temporary partnerships, combining direct punishment (within partnerships) and cyclical community punishment (across partnerships) can support full cooperation (efficiency) as a subgame perfect equilibrium, but only for sufficiently patient players (high discount factor). The milder cyclical punishment is less effective at deterring deviations in equilibrium (requires higher patience) but more effective at incentivizing punishment off-equilibrium (punishment is less costly, so more credible). In contrast, the classic grim punishment can sustain cooperation for less patient players when the temptation payoff is low, but fails when temptation is high. The length of partnerships (number of repeated rounds with the same partner) affects these thresholds: longer partnerships make cooperation harder to sustain for impatient players but make punishment more credible for patient players. The paper provides explicit mathematical conditions for these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how the structure of repeated interactions (number of rounds per partnership, random rematching frequency), the type of punishment available (direct, community, grim), and player patience (discount factor) interact to determine whether punishment can sustain efficient (fully cooperative) outcomes. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling direct punishment (via temporary partnerships) can increase the set of conditions under which efficiency is sustainable, but only if players are sufficiently patient. The effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency depends on the interplay between the temptation payoff, the cost/structure of punishment, and the frequency of rematching. The results are most directly applicable to adjacent settings (repeated PD with random matching), but the logic may transfer to PGG-like environments with similar punishment structures. The paper does not provide empirical estimates or lab data, but its explicit equilibrium conditions and comparative statics are informative for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency.

- source: Aramendia, M. (2006). Asymmetric finite punishments in repeated games. *Economics Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in repeated games, including social dilemmas and Cournot oligopoly, AFP strategies—where punishment is finite and asymmetric—can sustain any feasible and individually rational payoff profile when players are sufficiently patient. The punishment phase is designed so that nondeviators are always better off than the deviator, and after a finite number of periods, cooperation resumes. This approach is less restrictive than some standard equilibrium refinements and can support efficient outcomes (e.g., cartel profits) even in cases where other concepts (like WRP) cannot. The results are illustrated with examples including the prisoner's dilemma and Cournot oligopoly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that finite, asymmetric punishment strategies can sustain efficient, cooperative outcomes in repeated games, even when standard trigger or minimax punishments are not credible or too harsh. For the prediction task, it suggests that the design of the punishment phase (finite duration, asymmetry favoring nondeviators) can be critical for supporting high efficiency, especially as the number of players increases or when renegotiation-proofness is problematic. However, the results are theoretical and do not provide direct quantitative predictions for specific experimental public goods games, but they do indicate that efficiency can be maintained with appropriately designed punishment even in large groups or complex environments.

- source: Isakov, A., & Rand, D. G. (2012). The Evolution of Coercive Institutional Punishment. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment can evolve and be sustained when leaders (kings) can punish subjects for low tribute, especially when subjects are intelligent (accurate learners) and update their strategies quickly relative to kings. Strong punishment leads to high tribute payments but reduces subject payoffs and overall efficiency, except in the idealized case where punishment is never actually used. The model predicts that confusion or slow learning among subjects can collectively benefit them by undermining the effectiveness of coercive punishment. Increased global awareness (subjects learning from other kingdoms) destabilizes coercion and can collapse institutional punishment. The model is robust to changes in population size and punishment strength bounds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that institutional punishment, when used coercively in asymmetric settings, can increase compliance (tribute payment) but generally reduces group efficiency and aggregate payoffs compared to non-punishment or noncoercive equilibria. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that enabling punishment in similar asymmetric games will likely decrease efficiency unless compliance is perfect and punishment is never actually used. The effects are moderated by learning dynamics (intelligence, update speed) and the degree of information subjects have about other groups. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is imposed by a central authority and the 'public good' is tribute to that authority, rather than a symmetric PGG.

- source: Lim, I. S., & Capraro, V. (2022). A Synergy of Institutional Incentives and Networked Structures in Evolutionary Game Dynamics of Multiagent Systems. *Ieee Transactions on Circuits and Systems Ii-express Briefs*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment and network structure can interact synergistically to promote prosocial behavior and increase group efficiency (mean payoff) in a trust game. Specifically, the combination of even modest punishment and network structure can achieve full cooperation and maximal efficiency in parameter regions where neither mechanism alone would suffice. The required punishment level to achieve full cooperation is lower in structured populations than in well-mixed populations. The mean payoff is maximized at the threshold punishment level that induces full cooperation; increasing punishment beyond this point does not further increase efficiency and may reduce it due to the cost of maintaining the incentive system. The paper provides explicit analytical boundaries for these effects as a function of the trust return (r), network degree (k), and punishment size (p).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a networked trust game (and by extension, in adjacent public-goods-like games) will increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is not too costly and the network structure is not too sparse. The results suggest that the efficiency gain from punishment is greater in structured populations than in well-mixed ones, and that the combination of network structure and punishment can achieve high efficiency at lower punishment levels than punishment alone. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the network structure (degree), the effectiveness and cost of punishment, and the underlying return to cooperation. The findings are most directly applicable to trust games but are likely to transfer to public goods games with similar strategic structure.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2018). Evolution of global cooperation and ethnocentrism in group-structured populations. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops an analytical model of a group-structured population where individuals can adopt one of four strategies (global cooperator, ethnocentrist, traitor, egoist) and interact via a simplified prisoner's dilemma. Without any promoting mechanism, global cooperation is never favored by selection, regardless of migration pattern or group number; ethnocentrism is promoted, especially by global migration. When leader punishment is introduced (leaders punish defectors), global cooperation can be favored and can become more abundant than ethnocentrism if the punishment fine is sufficiently high. Efficiency-oriented leader punishment is more effective than equality-oriented punishment. The number of groups, migration rate, and mutation rate all moderate these effects. The main outcomes are derived as conditions for strategy abundance, which are directly linked to group payoff and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in group-structured prisoner's dilemma-like environments, punishment mechanisms (especially leader/third-party punishment with sufficient fines) can transform a setting where global cooperation is not favored into one where it is. The effect is more pronounced for efficiency-oriented punishment. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the payoff-based selection conditions are directly interpretable as efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence and type of punishment, the cost/fine of punishment, and the structure of the population (number of groups, migration/mutation rates) are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment. In the absence of punishment, efficiency is low (global cooperation not favored); with strong leader punishment, efficiency can be high (global cooperation favored).

- source: Brandt, U. S., & Svendsen, G. T. (2019). How robust is the welfare state when facing open borders? An evolutionary game-theoretic model. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The model shows that costly punishment (by Willing Punishers) is essential for maintaining high average welfare in a society vulnerable to free riding. If the proportion of punishers falls below a threshold, defectors proliferate and average welfare collapses. Timely recognition of the threat and sufficient support (redistributive rewards) for punishers can reverse this trend and restore high welfare. The model identifies threshold effects: there is a minimum level of support for punishment required to sustain cooperation and high efficiency. Delays in shifting support to punishers or insufficient rewards for them lead to welfare loss or collapse. The model also shows cyclical dynamics, with periods of high and low welfare depending on the political narrative and group composition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the presence and effectiveness of costly punishment mechanisms are critical moderators of efficiency in public-goods-like environments. The model suggests that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, but only if punishment is sufficiently incentivized and timely. The effect is not automatic: if support for punishers is too low or delayed, efficiency gains are not realized and may even be reversed. The findings support the prediction that punishment will increase efficiency when the game design ensures adequate support for punishers and rapid response to defection. The model also highlights the importance of migration/inflow of defectors and the political/institutional context (ingroup favouritism) as moderators. However, as a theoretical model, it does not provide empirical effect sizes or direct mapping to specific experimental game parameters.

- source: Ubeda, F., & Duéñez-Guzmán, E. A. (2011). POWER AND CORRUPTION. *Evolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a Prisoner's Dilemma with costly punishment, allowing for corrupt punishers and power asymmetries enables the evolution of stable states with both cooperation and corruption. When the social cost of punishing a punisher (q+d) is less than the cost of punishing a civilian (p), and within a certain range, a mixed equilibrium emerges with both cooperating civilians and corrupt policers. In this equilibrium, the population mean payoff (efficiency) is higher than in the all-defect equilibrium, and can approach the fully cooperative payoff as power asymmetries increase. The model demonstrates that costly punishment can increase both cooperation and efficiency, especially when punishers are protected by power asymmetries. The likelihood of reaching the efficient equilibrium depends on the relative costs and effectiveness of punishment. The results challenge the view that costly punishment is always inefficient, showing that under the right conditions, it can benefit the group even when punishers are themselves corrupt.
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency depends critically on the structure of punishment costs and power asymmetries. If punishment is costly but power asymmetries exist (punishers are less vulnerable to punishment or punishment is more effective against non-punishers), then enabling punishment can increase efficiency above the control (no-punishment) baseline. The model suggests that the efficiency gain is larger when the punishment of defectors is strong and the punishment of punishers is weak. The findings are most directly applicable to Prisoner's Dilemma settings, but the logic is relevant for public goods games with similar punishment structures. The paper does not address all game design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, communication), but provides direct, mechanistic insight into how punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and power asymmetries moderate the efficiency impact of punishment.

- source: Liu, M. M., Ma, Y. H., Liu, Z. Y., & You, X. M. (2017). An IUR evolutionary game model on the patent cooperate of Shandong China. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that higher punishment for negative cooperation (defection) increases both the rate of positive cooperation and the average payoff (\bar{U}) in the network, especially when punishment exceeds the development cost. When punishment is low, negative cooperation is more attractive and average payoffs are lower. Knowledge spillover reduces cooperation and can increase payoffs for defectors, but if punishment is high, spillover has less effect. The most efficient and cooperative outcome is achieved when profits are split equally between the two sides. The findings are based on simulation of the theoretical model, not on lab or field experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that, in a networked, repeated, two-population cooperation game with punishment, increasing the punishment for negative cooperation increases group efficiency (average payoff), especially when punishment exceeds the cost of cooperation. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related. The results suggest that, for similar networked cooperation games, enabling or increasing punishment should be predicted to increase efficiency, particularly when the punishment is substantial relative to the cost of cooperation. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with network structure, two-sided cooperation, and payoff sharing, but the qualitative direction is likely to generalize to PGG-like environments.

- source: Heller, W. B., & Sieberg, K. K. (2008). Functional unpleasantness: the evolutionary logic of righteous resentment. *Public Choice*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation can be sustained in populations where some individuals are willing to punish cheaters, even if those punishers are themselves not cooperative. The evolutionary stability of cooperation depends on the relative costs of punishing, being punished, and being cheated. When the cost of punishing is less than the cost of being cheated, punishment can support a stable mix of cooperators and punishers, increasing overall efficiency compared to a world with only cheaters. If punishment is too costly, cheaters dominate and cooperation collapses. The model demonstrates that the existence of punishment mechanisms—regardless of the punishers' motives—can improve group welfare and efficiency in collective action settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in collective action games (including public goods game variants) can increase efficiency, as measured by average group payoff, provided the cost of punishing is not too high. The findings suggest that the presence and effectiveness of punishment mechanisms are key moderators of the efficiency gains from punishment. While the model is not an exact public goods game, its structure and payoff logic are closely related, and its results are directly relevant for predicting when punishment will improve efficiency. The paper does not address empirical game design dimensions such as player count, number of rounds, or communication, but it does provide strong evidence that the cost and effectiveness of punishment are critical for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Podobnik, B., Gabor, A. M., & Kirbis, I. S. (2019). Scale-free growth of human society based on cooperation and altruistic punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that the system's growth rate (interpreted as efficiency or collective payoff) increases monotonically with the level of cooperation, which itself increases with the level of punishment. There is a threshold: if punishment is too high, the system can collapse (negative growth), but within reasonable bounds, higher punishment leads to more cooperation and higher efficiency. Empirical data suggest that less corrupt (more cooperative) countries tend to have higher fines, supporting the model's prediction that higher punishment is associated with higher cooperation. Survey data indicate the perceived fine needed to induce compliance, but do not directly measure payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public-goods-like environments, enabling or increasing punishment increases efficiency (system growth rate), up to a point. The model is not a direct PGG but is closely related, and the main mechanism—punishment increasing cooperation, which increases collective payoff—transfers to PGG settings. The paper does not provide empirical estimates of effect size, but the qualitative direction is clear: punishment increases efficiency unless set excessively high. The model also highlights that the effect of punishment depends on the baseline level of cooperation and the distribution of punishment sensitivity in the population.

- source: Nasrallah, W. F., & Cheaib, K. A. (2016). An equilibrium model of how regulative and normative institutions influence micro-economic and organizational behavior. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The theta-pi model demonstrates that both punishment (regulative institutions) and empathy (normative institutions) can increase the efficiency of economic interactions by expanding the set of transactions that are mutually beneficial and deterring predation. The model provides explicit conditions under which markets are efficient (all cooperate), collapse (all defect or abstain), or are path-dependent/oscillatory. Punishment (pi) is especially effective in high-temptation, low-sucker-loss environments, while empathy (theta) is more effective when losses from being cheated are high. When some actors are exempt from punishment (elites), efficiency declines and can collapse if the exempt group is too large. Overlapping institutions and partial coverage can lead to complex dynamics, including cycles of cooperation and predation. The model quantifies the marginal value of increasing institutional strength and provides phase diagrams for different transaction types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (increasing pi) in a public-goods-like environment will increase efficiency, especially when the temptation to defect is high and the cost to victims is not overwhelming. The model clarifies that the effectiveness of punishment depends on its universality and the structure of payoffs, and that partial or selective enforcement can undermine efficiency. The findings suggest that, all else equal, increasing the probability or effectiveness of punishment will shift the equilibrium from market collapse or low efficiency to high efficiency, provided the institutional coverage is broad. The model also highlights that empathy-based mechanisms can substitute for punishment in some environments, but are less effective when temptation is high and losses are low. For prediction tasks, the model's phase diagrams and threshold conditions can be mapped to game design dimensions such as punishment cost, effectiveness, and universality, supporting the expectation that enabling punishment will generally increase efficiency in public-goods-like games.

- source: Gao, S. P., & Liang, J. L. (2020). Cooperation under institutional incentives with perfect and imperfect observation. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Institutional punishment (and reward), funded by a tax on positive payoffs, can promote the evolutionary emergence of cooperation in a well-mixed prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness of punishment increases with higher tax rates, greater benefit of cooperation, and stronger incentive effectiveness. There is a threshold tax rate above which cooperation is favored by natural selection. Imperfect monitoring (i.e., noise in identifying defectors) reduces the effectiveness of punishment and increases the required tax rate for cooperation to emerge. The model provides analytical results for average payoffs and fixation probabilities, but does not directly report group efficiency as a fraction of the fully cooperative outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that institutional punishment (with costs funded by a tax on payoffs) can increase the evolutionary success of cooperation, especially when monitoring is perfect, the tax rate is high, and incentives are strong. While it does not report group efficiency directly, the increase in fixation probability of cooperation and average payoffs for cooperators suggest that enabling punishment in similar game designs would increase efficiency relative to a control without punishment, particularly under high tax rates and effective monitoring. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a prisoner's dilemma structure, so predictions should be transferred with caution. The findings are most informative for settings with institutional, payoff-funded punishment and well-mixed populations.

- source: Han, T. A., & Lenaerts, T. (2016). A synergy of costly punishment and commitment in cooperation dilemmas. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that a strategy combining prior commitment and costly punishment (CPP) can achieve higher frequencies of cooperation than either mechanism alone, across a wide range of parameter values. The improvement is most significant when the cost of arranging commitments is high and the punishment impact is above a threshold. The model shows that commitment reduces the effect-to-cost ratio required for punishment to be effective, and punishment helps deal with free-riders who exploit commitment. The results are robust across different cost and punishment parameters, but are derived for the two-player, one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma, not for public goods games or group interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that combining commitment and punishment mechanisms can outperform either alone in promoting cooperation, especially when commitment is costly and punishment is sufficiently effective. However, the model is based on the two-player Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game or group setting. The outcomes are reported as frequencies of strategies and risk-dominance, not as group efficiency or total payoff. Thus, while the findings suggest that punishment (especially when combined with commitment) can improve cooperation and, by implication, group outcomes, the results cannot be directly mapped to efficiency predictions in public goods games. The paper is most useful for understanding the interaction of punishment and commitment mechanisms and their parameter dependencies, but less directly informative for quantitative efficiency prediction in PGG-like environments.

- source: Takesue, H. (2018). Evolutionary prisoner's dilemma games on the network with punishment and opportunistic partner switching. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that both punishment and partner switching (opportunistic rewiring of social ties) can support the evolution of cooperation in a networked prisoner's dilemma. When partner switching is infrequent, strong punishment is necessary to suppress defectors. When partner switching is frequent, cooperation dominates even with weak or no punishment. The mechanism is that defectors sever ties with punishers and preferentially connect to cooperators, which increases cooperators' opportunities to be imitated and thus proliferate. Average payoffs by strategy are reported, showing that cooperators and punishers can outperform defectors, but the main outcome is the frequency of each strategy. The results suggest that social fluidity (partner switching) can substitute for punishment in maintaining cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report group efficiency or total welfare directly, it shows that both punishment and partner switching can increase the prevalence of cooperation and reduce defection, and that average payoffs for cooperators and punishers can exceed those of defectors. The findings suggest that in networked games with opportunities for partner switching, the marginal benefit of adding punishment to increase efficiency may be reduced if partner switching is already frequent. The results also highlight the importance of the punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the fluidity of social relationships (analogous to partner switching) as moderators of punishment's effect. However, because the main outcomes are strategy frequencies and not group efficiency, the evidence is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators rather than for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Nakamura, M. (2019). Rare Third-Party Punishment Promotes Cooperation in Risk-Averse Social Learning Dynamics. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
