  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that risk-averse individuals in evolutionary social dilemma games can achieve stable, high levels of cooperation with only rare third-party punishment. Both exogenous (authoritative) and endogenous (peer) punishment models are analyzed. Theoretical results show that the required frequency of punishment to maintain cooperation is much lower for risk-averse individuals than for risk-neutral or risk-prone individuals. Among punishment variants, punishing all defectors or punishing one defector (as a warning) are both effective in promoting cooperation, while punishing only one-sided defectors is less effective. The models predict that, under risk aversion, group payoff and cooperation rates are higher with punishment than without, and that the cost of maintaining cooperation via punishment can be minimized.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in two-player social dilemma games with third-party punishment, enabling punishment (even rarely) can substantially increase cooperation and group payoff, especially when individuals are risk averse. The findings suggest that the efficiency gain from punishment is robust to low punishment frequency and that the structure of punishment (punish all vs. punish one) can moderate the effect. However, the results are derived from theoretical models, not empirical data, and the games are not public goods games but two-player dilemmas. The paper is most informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in environments where risk aversion is salient and punishment is rare.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., & Wang, L. (2015). Emergence of parochial altruism in well-mixed populations. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that parochial altruism (in-group cooperation, out-group punishment) is favored by selection under certain parameter regimes, especially when the benefit of cooperation is high relative to its cost, and when the effectiveness of punishment (fine imposed) exceeds its cost. Intergroup conflict (punishment) can promote the evolution of in-group cooperation, and vice versa. High mutation rates are detrimental to the evolution of parochial altruism. Larger populations and intermediate selection strengths can also favor parochial altruism. The model provides analytical conditions for when parochial altruism is favored, but does not directly report on group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments, by showing that punishment (when effective and not too costly) can promote the evolutionary success of parochial altruism (in-group cooperation and out-group punishment). However, the model is not a standard public goods game, but rather a two-group model with prisoner's dilemma for in-group and a punishment game for out-group interactions. The main outcomes are evolutionary frequencies of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff. The results suggest that punishment can promote cooperation under certain conditions, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency changes in standard PGGs. The findings are most relevant for understanding the parameter regimes where punishment is likely to support cooperative strategies, which may inform priors about efficiency effects in related experimental designs.

- source: Rumble, A. C., Willcox, K., Imada, H., & Yansen, D. (2022). Beyond Reciprocity: Forgiveness, Generosity, and Punishment in Continuing Dyadic Interactions. *Journal of Theoretical Social Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper synthesizes evidence from simulation and behavioral studies of dyadic repeated games (mainly Prisoner's Dilemma and its continuous variants) to argue that punishment, while sometimes increasing cooperation, generally reduces average payoffs and is less effective than generous or forgiving strategies for maintaining cooperation and maximizing outcomes. Costly punishment is found to lower group welfare and does not restore cooperation as effectively as non-punitive responses. The review highlights that punishment also carries social and emotional costs, making it less desirable in ongoing dyadic relationships. Generosity and forgiveness, by contrast, are more likely to sustain cooperation and higher payoffs, especially when noise or uncertainty is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and literature-based evidence that, in dyadic repeated games (especially noisy or continuous-choice environments), enabling punishment is likely to reduce efficiency or group payoff compared to control conditions without punishment. The negative effect is attributed to both the direct material cost of punishment and the escalation of retaliation cycles, as well as social/emotional fallout. The review suggests that, for prediction tasks, the presence of punishment (especially if costly and in dyads) should be expected to have a less positive or even negative effect on efficiency, unless the punishment is very cheap or the environment is highly structured to avoid retaliation spirals. The findings are most directly relevant to dyadic (two-player) repeated games, and less so to larger public goods games, but the mechanisms discussed may generalize to small groups.

- source: Zu, J. J., Xu, F. X., Jin, T., & Xiang, W. (2022). Reward and Punishment Mechanism with weighting enhances cooperation in evolutionary games. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that the introduction of a reward and punishment mechanism with weighting (RPMW) increases the frequency of cooperation in evolutionary games (PDG, SDG, SHG), both in well-mixed and lattice populations. The effect is stronger with higher intensity (alpha) and a higher proportion of supervisors (beta). The combination of reward and punishment is more effective than either alone in promoting cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation frequency, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment (and reward) mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games, and that the effect is stronger with higher punishment/reward intensity and more supervisors. However, because the outcomes are reported as cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The specific game structure is not a public goods game but rather two-player matrix games (PDG, SDG, SHG), so transfer to PGG-like environments should be cautious.

- source: Wang, C. Y., & Cui, W. F. (2022). Supervision for the Public Health Services for Older Adults Under the Background of Government Purchasing: An Evolutionary Game Analysis Framework. *Frontiers in Public Health*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a principal-agent setting with information asymmetry, static punishment or reward mechanisms are insufficient to ensure full compliance (self-discipline) by service providers. Dynamic mechanisms, where the intensity of punishment or reward depends on the observed behavior, can stabilize higher levels of self-discipline. Specifically, higher punishment intensity increases the proportion of self-discipline, while higher reward intensity can decrease it due to moral hazard and adverse selection. The model is theoretical and does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that dynamic sanctioning mechanisms are more effective at promoting compliance than static ones.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation (or self-discipline) depends on the design of the sanctioning mechanism, particularly whether it is static or dynamic. While the model is not a public goods game per se, it is structurally similar in that it models a social dilemma with potential for free-riding and the use of sanctions. The findings suggest that, in analogous PGG-like environments, dynamic punishment mechanisms are likely to be more effective at increasing cooperation than static ones, and that increasing punishment intensity generally increases compliance. However, the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, so its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. It is most useful for understanding the qualitative effects of punishment design and intensity on compliance behavior.

- source: Ye, W. X., & Fan, S. H. (2020). Evolutionary traveler's dilemma game based on particle swarm optimization. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that using a PSO-based learning mechanism in the traveler's dilemma game leads to higher levels of cooperation (higher average claims) compared to traditional Fermi dynamics, across different network types. The effect of the reward/punishment parameter (R) and the balance between self-cognition and social cognition (c1) are explored: social learning promotes cooperation when the reward/punishment is small (large strategy interval), while self-cognition becomes more important as the reward/punishment increases (small strategy interval). The main outcome is the normalized cooperation level, not efficiency or total group payoff. The results are robust across network types, but the study is simulation-based and does not report direct efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how learning mechanisms and the structure of reward/punishment in a traveler's dilemma game (an adjacent but not exact variant of the public goods game) affect the prevalence of cooperative behavior (high claims). It does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms in promoting cooperation depends on the size of the incentive and the learning dynamics. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes and studies a different game structure.

- source: Jiang, X. H., & Zheng, W. Q. (2024). Dynamic strategies for collaborative governance of rural environments: a simulation study on the most effective mechanisms. *Environment Development and Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that under a static reward and punishment mechanism (SRPM), the system does not reach a stable equilibrium; strategies of all parties fluctuate periodically. Under a dynamic reward and punishment mechanism (DRPM), the system converges to a stable mixed-strategy Nash equilibrium (MSNE), interpreted as an evolutionarily stable strategy (ESS). Increasing the upper limit of punishments for illegal emissions by village enterprises significantly increases the probability of legal emissions and reduces the need for strict government regulation and active supervision by villagers. Increasing the upper limit of rewards for villagers' active supervision increases their supervision probability but may reduce legal compliance by enterprises and government regulatory effort. The DRPM is recommended for more effective and stable governance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that dynamic, behavior-dependent punishment mechanisms (analogous to more flexible or responsive punishment rules in public goods games) can stabilize cooperative behavior (legal emissions) and reduce the need for costly monitoring and enforcement. However, the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff. The main outcomes are strategy probabilities, not payoffs. The findings suggest that increasing punishment effectiveness (punishment_tech) and using dynamic punishment rules may improve stability and compliance, but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified. The results are most relevant for understanding how punishment design (cost, tech, dynamic vs static) may affect behavioral outcomes in multi-agent governance games.

- source: Su, F., Chang, J. B., & Li, X. (2021). Research on the Evolution Path and Influence Factors of Core Enterprise Oriented Entrepreneurship Ecosystem Under the Government Regulation. *Ieee Access*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of punishment (by core enterprises to start-ups, and by government to both) increases the likelihood and speed of positive strategy selection (cooperation, entrepreneurship, regulation) among all agents. Conversely, higher costs (supervision, regulation, entrepreneurship) and higher subsidies can reduce the tendency for positive strategies. The model identifies multiple possible stable equilibria depending on parameter values, and shows that a moderately high, flexible reward and punishment mechanism is most effective for promoting positive strategies. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, focusing instead on the behavioral evolution of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms (and their cost/benefit structure) can promote positive, cooperative, or pro-social strategies in multi-agent systems similar to public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that increasing punishment (and reducing its cost) should increase the prevalence of positive strategies, which may be associated with higher efficiency in analogous PGG settings, but this is not directly demonstrated. The model's complexity and focus on behavioral strategy selection, rather than payoff, limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction.

- source: Qu, X. L., Zhou, C. L., Cao, Z. G., & Yang, X. G. (2016). Conditional dissociation as a punishment mechanism in the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Conditional dissociation, modeled as a punishment mechanism where players can break up with defectors and must wait before re-entering the game, promotes the evolution of cooperation in an iterated Prisoner's Dilemma. The effect is stronger when the punishment is more severe (longer waiting time) and when the population's lifespan is longer. Allowing mixed strategies (as opposed to only pure strategies) reduces the probability of ending up with a fully cooperative population. The model is clean, excluding other cooperation-promoting mechanisms, and shows that both intolerant cooperators and tolerant defectors can be stable outcomes. The main outcomes are the frequency of cooperative vs. defective strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (conditional dissociation) on cooperation in repeated pairwise social dilemmas. While not a public goods game, the findings suggest that punishment mechanisms that impose a cost on defectors (and sometimes on punishers) can promote cooperation, especially when the punishment is more severe and when the game is longer. The absence of direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes limits its direct applicability to efficiency prediction, but the results support the general idea that increasing punishment severity (e.g., higher punishment cost or effectiveness) can increase cooperation, though possibly at the expense of overall efficiency if punishers also incur costs. The negative effect of mixed strategies on cooperation may also be relevant for predicting outcomes in more flexible or noisy environments.

- source: Chan, N. W. H., Xu, C., Tey, S. M., Yap, Y. J., & Hui, P. M. (2013). Evolutionary snowdrift game incorporating costly punishment in structured populations. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a costly punishing strategy into the snowdrift game on a square lattice leads to richer population dynamics and phase structures than in well-mixed populations. For certain parameter ranges (low cost-to-benefit ratio r and sufficiently high punishment impact β), punishers can suppress defectors and coexist with cooperators, or even dominate. The spatial structure (lattice) allows for stable coexistence of all three strategies (C, D, P) in some regions, a phenomenon not observed in well-mixed populations. The pair approximation analytic method only qualitatively matches the simulation results and fails to capture some spatially-induced phases. The main reported outcomes are the steady-state frequencies of each strategy, not group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment in structured populations, specifically in the context of the snowdrift game (which is adjacent to public goods games). It shows that costly punishment can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies and suppress defectors in spatially structured settings, but does not quantify the effect on group efficiency or total payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the paper suggests that spatial structure and the presence of punishment can qualitatively enhance cooperation, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes. The findings are most relevant for understanding how punishment and spatial structure interact to shape population composition, rather than for predicting efficiency changes in public goods games.

- source: Li, J. Y., Wu, W. H., Li, Z. Z., Wang, W. X., & Zhang, B. Y. (2023). Data-driven evolutionary game models for the spread of fairness and cooperation in heterogeneous networks. *Frontiers in Psychiatry*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment, when amplified by network heterogeneity and increased inequality aversion (e.g., via oxytocin), can spread through a network and promote higher levels of fairness and cooperation. This effect is observed in both the ultimatum game and the two-stage prisoner's dilemma with punishment, but not in the trust game, where the absence of punishment means that increased trust does not diffuse. The models fit experimental data well for behavioral outcomes (offers, cooperation rates, punishment rates), but do not directly analyze or report group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms are more effective than reward/trust mechanisms for promoting prosocial behavior in networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction that enabling costly punishment in networked social dilemmas (such as public goods or prisoner's dilemma variants) increases cooperation and fairness, especially when network heterogeneity allows central nodes to amplify the effect. However, because the paper is theoretical and focuses on behavioral outcomes (not efficiency or group payoff), it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The models suggest that punishment is more effective than reward in promoting prosocial outcomes, but efficiency implications must be inferred from increased cooperation and fairness, not directly measured. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., network structure, inequality aversion), but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Cardoso, F. M., Gracia-Lázaro, C., & Moreno, Y. (2020). Dynamics of heuristics selection for cooperative behaviour. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can emerge and be sustained in iterated Prisoner's Dilemma games when agents have memory and low mutation rates, with punishment and reciprocity as key mechanisms. Increasing memory increases cooperation, while higher mutation rates reduce it. When agents can discriminate kin, cooperation is still sustained, but the underlying heuristics shift from reciprocity to kin-based discrimination. The main reported outcomes are the fraction of cooperative actions and the distribution of strategies; efficiency or group payoff is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the importance of punishment and reciprocity in sustaining cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, especially when agents have memory and mutation rates are low. However, because the model is based on the Prisoner's Dilemma (not a public goods game or direct variant), and because efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that enabling punishment and memory can increase cooperation rates, but do not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Shutters, S. T. (2009). Strong reciprocity, social structure, and the evolution of fair allocations in a simulated ultimatum game. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a simulated ultimatum game, third-party punishment (altruistic punishment) does not generally lead to fair allocations, except in linearly structured populations where fair offers can evolve even without punishment. As the punishment multiplier (punishment impact per unit cost) increases, populations with spatial structure exhibit a sharp transition from low to very high offers, but these are not necessarily fair. The number of neighbors (social structure) strongly moderates both baseline offers and the threshold at which punishment induces high offers. The main outcome is the mean offer rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment cost-effectiveness and social structure (number of neighbors) affect behavioral outcomes (mean offer rates) in an ultimatum game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes from punishment in PGG-like environments. However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in changing behavior is highly sensitive to the punishment cost/impact ratio and the social network structure. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the importance of considering punishment cost-effectiveness and group structure, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Li, B. F., Zhang, X., Shi, Z. Y., & Wei, W. (2023). Justice strategy can promote cooperation with the joint of dynamic aspiration in social dilemma. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a justice strategy (where players punish defectors at equal cost) alongside dynamic aspiration updating can suppress or eliminate defectors and promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness depends on the initial aspiration level: higher aspirations lead to fewer defectors and more justicers. The justice strategy can dominate even when initially rare, especially when defectors form clusters. The model is agent-based and simulation-driven, with outcomes reported as the stable fractions of each strategy. No aggregate payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (even when costly to the punisher) can reduce defection and promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas, especially when combined with dynamic aspirations. However, because the outcomes are reported as strategy fractions rather than group efficiency or payoff, the paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is also a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, though the mechanisms are related. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral pathways by which punishment can affect cooperation, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wang, W., He, Z. X., Li, X. G., & Shi, L. (2023). Adaptive exit facilitates the evolution of cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game with punishment. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to adaptively exit the game based on their payoff relative to aspiration can significantly increase the prevalence of cooperation, even when punishment is weak. There is an optimal aspiration level at which adaptive exit best promotes cooperation. Adaptive exit protects cooperators from exploitation by defectors and reduces the burden of punishment on punishers by increasing defectors' exit probability. The model shows that adaptive exit can eliminate second-order free-riding and that the interplay between exit and punishment is crucial for the evolution of cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that adaptive exit mechanisms and the interaction between exit and punishment can strongly affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of strategy frequencies and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that enabling exit (or similar mechanisms) may increase cooperation and reduce the cost of punishment, but do not provide quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Yang, H. X., & Wang, Z. (2015). Role of mutual punishment in the snowdrift game. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that mutual punishment in the snowdrift game can either promote or inhibit cooperation depending on the temptation to defect and the punishment fine. For low temptation to defect, increasing punishment fine increases cooperation; for high temptation, it decreases cooperation; for intermediate temptation, there is a non-monotonic relationship. The main reported outcome is the steady-state fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. The results are shown for both well-mixed and structured populations, with phase transitions in cooperation rates observed. The findings are relevant for understanding how mutual punishment (peer pressure) affects conformity and cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments with punishment. Its results are based on the snowdrift game, which is adjacent but not equivalent to the PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The mutual punishment mechanism is novel and may inform mechanism design, but the lack of payoff or efficiency analysis limits its direct applicability to the prediction task. The findings may be useful for understanding how punishment parameters affect cooperation rates, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Patrzyk, P. M., & Takác, M. (2017). Cognitive adaptations to criminal justice lead to paranoid norm obedience. *Adaptive Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that evolutionary pressures can lead to agents developing a cognitive bias that causes them to overestimate the probability of being observed and punished for norm violations. As a result, agents often cooperate (obey norms) even when they are not actually being observed, especially in environments with high uncertainty and/or strong punishment. The rate of cooperation increases with the magnitude of punishment, the probability of being observed, and the uncertainty in classifying situations as observed or not. However, much of this cooperation is 'irrational' in the sense that it is not based on accurate assessment of risk, but on evolved bias. The model does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that institutional punishment and cognitive bias can sustain high levels of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that increasing punishment magnitude and the probability of observation can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma settings, even when actual monitoring is imperfect. However, because the model is based on the Prisoner's Dilemma (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective in promoting cooperation, especially under uncertainty, but do not quantify the impact on group efficiency or surplus. The model also highlights the importance of cognitive biases in sustaining cooperation, which may be relevant for interpreting experimental results in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Sun, X. P., Han, L. F., Wang, M., Liu, S. X., & Shen, Y. (2023). Social exclusion with antisocial punishment in spatial public goods game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment by investors can induce a stable coexistence of punishing investors and trustworthy trustees in a hierarchical N-player trust game. The effectiveness of punishment in promoting trust depends on both the intensity of punishment and the fraction of investors in the population. For low punishment intensity, an intermediate fraction of investors is optimal for promoting trust, while for high punishment intensity, a higher fraction of investors is more effective. Increasing punishment intensity generally enlarges the attraction domain of the coexistence state, but only up to a point. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but interprets the stable coexistence of trust and trustworthiness as a positive social outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment can promote trust and trustworthiness in a hierarchical trust game, with the effect moderated by punishment intensity and the fraction of investors. However, it does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games is indirect. The findings suggest that the presence and intensity of punishment, as well as the composition of the population, are important moderators of the effect of punishment on cooperative behavior. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can improve group outcomes under certain conditions, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

- source: Nie, J. D., Wang, J., Niu, H. D., & Xia, C. Y. (2022). Impact of multi-step punishment on the spatial prisoner?s dilemma game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that multi-step punishment (punishment lasting multiple time steps for low-reputation individuals) can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game, but only if the punishment duration is neither too short nor too long. There is an optimal punishment time window that maximizes the frequency of cooperation. Too short punishment fails to deter defection, while too long punishment can backfire and reduce cooperation. The intensity of punishment (how much payoffs are reduced for punished individuals) is the dominant factor in promoting cooperation. The model does not include a cost for punishment, and does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. All results are based on the proportion of cooperators in the population.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment on cooperation rates in a spatial prisoner's dilemma, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings suggest that punishment can increase cooperation rates, especially with optimal duration and sufficient intensity, but the absence of punishment cost and lack of payoff analysis limit its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and may inform qualitative expectations about punishment's effect on cooperation, but not on efficiency or welfare. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Zhao, H., & Li, L. (2025). Dynamic role-switching in hypergraphs: Enhancing cooperation via adaptive punishment and reinforcement learning. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that periodic update rules with Q-learning and a punishment mechanism can substantially increase the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. Lower punishment costs and shorter update intervals are especially effective at promoting cooperation. The model is robust across different network structures. However, the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff, and the results are based on agent-based simulations rather than empirical experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms, when combined with learning and periodic update rules, can increase cooperation rates in spatial games. However, because the main outcome is cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that lower punishment costs and more frequent updating are likely to make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency in related models, but this is not directly demonstrated.

- source: Guo, T., He, Z. X., & Shi, L. (2023). Self-organization in mobile populations promotes the evolution of altruistic punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that migration in spatially structured populations promotes the evolution and prevalence of pro-social punishment (altruistic punishment) and cooperation, especially when individuals migrate to maximize potential gains rather than avoid losses. There is an optimal population density for this effect. Migration can sustain pro-social punishment even when punishment efficiency (fine/cost ratio) is low, as long as the absolute amount of punishment is high. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the prevalence of pro-social punishment and cooperation is interpreted as a positive outcome for the evolution of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling migration or spatial self-organization can enhance the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation, especially under certain migration preferences and population densities. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings are most relevant for understanding the mechanisms by which migration and spatial structure interact with punishment, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Guan, K. X., Chen, Y. Y., Zheng, W. J., Zeng, L. L., & Ye, H. (2022). Costly signals can facilitate cooperation and punishment in the prisoner?s dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a prisoner's dilemma with costly signaling, punishment as a costly signal can become prevalent if the signal is strong and noise is low, but this does not necessarily increase cooperation. Without punishment, cooperation can dominate if the cost-benefit ratio is favorable and noise is low. The introduction of punishment (when its cost is converted into a strong costly signal) increases the frequency of punishment and reduces defection, but does not increase cooperation. The model does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly inferred.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment in two-player prisoner's dilemma games with costly signaling, but does not directly address efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that punishment can reduce defection and increase the prevalence of punishment strategies if the punishment signal is strong, but does not necessarily increase cooperation or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, the relevance is limited and indirect, as the model is not a PGG and does not report efficiency outcomes.

- source: Deng, Z. H., Huang, Y. J., Gu, Z. Y., & Li-Gao (2018). Multigames with social punishment and the evolution of cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a punishment strategy (punishers who pay a cost to fine defectors) in a spatial multigame environment (mix of PD and SD) increases the fraction of cooperation (cooperators and punishers), especially when the sucker's payoff is large or when more players play the Snowdrift game. Punishers form compact clusters that help protect against defectors. The average payoff is shown to recover as cooperation increases, but efficiency (as a ratio to full cooperation) is not directly analyzed. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not experiments or real human subjects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and average payoff in spatial social dilemmas, especially when there is heterogeneity in game parameters. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency as defined for the prediction task, its value for predicting treatment efficiency in PGG-like environments is limited. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation rates), and the only payoff-related outcome is average payoff, which is not benchmarked to full cooperation. The punishment mechanism is explicit and parameterized, but the results are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Xu, C., Ji, M., Yap, Y. J., Zheng, D. F., & Hui, P. M. (2011). Costly punishment and cooperation in the evolutionary snowdrift game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing costly punishment into the evolutionary snowdrift game can lead to two types of steady states: (1) a mixture of cooperators and punishers (with defectors eliminated), or (2) a mixture of cooperators and defectors (with punishers eliminated), depending on the cost-to-benefit ratio, punishment parameters, and initial conditions. The presence of punishers can enhance cooperation relative to the standard snowdrift game, but this is measured in terms of the frequency of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model provides analytic and simulation results for the conditions under which defectors can or cannot invade a population with punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment on cooperation in a two-player snowdrift game, which is adjacent to public goods games. The main outcomes are strategy frequencies, not efficiency or group payoff, so the results are not directly usable for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. However, the finding that costly punishment can eliminate defectors and increase the frequency of cooperators under certain conditions suggests that punishment may increase efficiency in analogous settings, especially when the cost of punishment is low relative to its impact. The model's focus on initial conditions and parameter sensitivity may inform qualitative expectations about when punishment is more or less effective, but quantitative prediction of efficiency is not supported.

- source: Xu, C., Hui, P. M., Yu, Y. Y., & Gu, G. Q. (2009). Self-organized cooperative behavior and critical penalty in an evolving population. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a population of agents playing a two-action game (cooperate or defect), increasing the penalty for defection increases the mean probability to cooperate. There is a critical penalty threshold, dependent on initial conditions, above which the population becomes fully cooperative. For populations with higher initial cooperation, less penalty is needed to achieve full cooperation, and in some cases, no penalty is required. The model also shows that after a population is trained to cooperate with penalty, the penalty can be relaxed without immediate loss of cooperation. The structure of the population (well-mixed vs. 2D lattice) affects the level of cooperation at intermediate penalties, with lattices supporting higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the findings are not directly about payoff-based efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the severity of punishment can increase cooperation rates in repeated social dilemma games, with a critical threshold for full cooperation. However, because the main outcome is the mean probability to cooperate and not group payoff or efficiency, the results cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The model is adjacent to PGGs and may inform qualitative expectations about the effect of punishment on cooperation, but not on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective in increasing cooperation, especially when initial cooperation is low, and that population structure can moderate this effect. For the prediction task, this paper is best used as indirect, qualitative support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation, but not as quantitative evidence for efficiency effects.

- source: Chen, L., Sun, J. J., Li, K., & Li, Q. R. (2022). Research on the effectiveness of monitoring mechanism for yield to pedestrian based on system dynamics. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Without external control, the system exhibits persistent oscillations in the probability of drivers yielding to pedestrians, and TMD monitoring disappears over time. (2) Introducing dynamic punishment (fines that increase with non-compliance) suppresses oscillations and increases compliance, but does not fully eliminate violations. (3) Combining dynamic punishment and reward (penalty-incentive control) leads to full compliance (all drivers yield) and eliminates the need for monitoring or pedestrian supervision. (4) Alternatively, a sufficiently high static monitoring rate by TMD (above a threshold) can also achieve full compliance. The results are framed in terms of behavioral probabilities and system stability, not aggregate efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that dynamic punishment and/or reward mechanisms can stabilize and maximize compliance in a multi-agent system with monitoring and sanctioning, analogous to public goods games with punishment and reward. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for understanding the qualitative effect of punishment and reward on stabilizing cooperation and eliminating violations, especially in systems with endogenous monitoring and third-party enforcement. The model is adjacent to PGGs, but the outcome mapping to efficiency is indirect.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., Nie, S. L., & Wang, L. (2015). Emergence of parochial altruism in well-mixed populations of multiple groups. *Physics Letters A*.

  findings: The paper finds that parochial altruism is more likely to emerge and be favored by selection when intergroup punishment is more effective (higher fine-to-cost ratio), when there are more groups (higher assortment), and when mutation rates are low. Affiliation (belonging to more groups) makes parochial altruism less likely for low mutation, but more likely for high mutation. The model provides explicit conditions (in terms of benefit, cost, punishment fine, and punishment cost) for when parochial altruism is favored. However, the main outcomes are about the evolutionary abundance of strategies, not about group efficiency or total payoff.

However, because the model is theoretical and focuses on evolutionary stability of strategies rather than direct efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results suggest that punishment is more likely to be beneficial (in terms of promoting cooperation-like behavior) when punishment is effective and group structure is strong, but the translation to efficiency outcomes in experimental PGGs is indirect.

- source: Wang, X. Y., Zhang, L., Du, X. R., & Sun, Y. L. (2017). Evolving cooperation in spatial population with punishment by using PSO algorithm. *Natural Computing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment in spatial evolutionary games (n-player IPD and ISD) increases the proportion of cooperators and punishers, and decreases the proportion of defectors and loners, especially at lower cost-to-benefit ratios. The effect of punishment is stronger in the ISD game, where defection would otherwise dominate. Larger group sizes and higher cost-to-benefit ratios reduce cooperation. The network structure (regular, Moore, Von Neumann, small-world, complete) and neighborhood size also affect cooperation rates. The length of history record (memory) has a non-monotonic effect on cooperation. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial evolutionary games, and that the effect is moderated by group size, cost-to-benefit ratio, and network structure. However, because the outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that, in similar spatial games, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation, especially in smaller groups and at lower cost-to-benefit ratios, but the effect on efficiency is not directly established.

- source: Bausch, A. W. (2014). Evolving intergroup cooperation. *Computational and Mathematical Organization Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that in-group policing is highly effective at promoting inter-group cooperation in an evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma model, with high levels of CC outcomes (over 80% in many runs). In contrast, collective sanctions alone do not increase cooperation above baseline and can even reduce it when combined with in-group policing, especially when the sanction period is long. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the behavioral results suggest that agent-level punishment is more effective than group-level punishment for sustaining cooperation. The findings are robust across a range of parameterizations, but are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than direct payoff or efficiency measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in environments structurally similar to a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with group structure, agent-level punishment (in-group policing) is much more effective at sustaining cooperation than group-level punishment (collective sanctions). However, because the model does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game, its direct predictive value for efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment can promote or hinder cooperation, and for anticipating that group-level punishment may not translate into higher efficiency or payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of in-group policing (individualized punishment) is likely to have a more positive effect on cooperation than collective sanctions, but does not provide quantitative efficiency data.

  findings: The paper provides a decision-tree framework linking specific game structures (defined by investment, payoff structure, symmetry, repetition, and partner choice) to the partner control mechanisms that can stabilize cooperation. Punishment is predicted to be a viable control mechanism in repeated interactions without partner choice, especially when both players can invest and defect. The framework also highlights the roles of reward, sanctions, partner switching, and indirect reciprocity in other game structures. The review does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment, but it does clarify the conditions under which punishment is theoretically expected to promote cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for identifying which game structures are theoretically compatible with punishment as a mechanism for stabilizing cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on the efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods or similar games. For the downstream prediction task, the framework can help map which design dimensions (e.g., repeated interactions, partner choice, symmetry) are necessary for punishment to be a relevant mechanism, but it cannot inform the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes. The paper is most useful for contextualizing when punishment is likely to be effective, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

When punishment is present (especially if costless to the punisher) or when shame is present, populations maintain higher levels of generosity and a balance between punishers and non-punishers. However, the model does not directly report on group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary dynamics of behavioral traits. The results suggest that shame and punishment can stabilize cooperation for long periods, but with fluctuations between cooperative and selfish phases.

However, because the model is not a direct PGG and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The findings are more about evolutionary stability and trait frequencies than about efficiency per se.

- source: Boehm, C. (2014). The moral consequences of social selection. *Behaviour*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that in mobile hunter-gatherer societies, group punishment—ranging from shaming and ostracism to capital punishment—has been a central mechanism for enforcing cooperation and curbing antisocial behaviors such as bullying, theft, and cheating. These sanctions are argued to have shaped the evolution of human conscience and moral norms, favoring individuals who are generous, honest, and humble. The author suggests that such punitive social selection has been critical for the emergence of egalitarian, cooperative societies, and for the evolutionary success of altruistic traits. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative analysis of efficiency or group payoffs in public goods game-like settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and ethnographic context for the role of punishment in promoting cooperation and suppressing free-riding in small-scale societies. It supports the idea that punishment can be a powerful tool for maintaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper offers indirect support for the positive role of punishment in sustaining cooperation, especially in small, face-to-face groups, but does not allow for parameter-specific predictions based on game design dimensions.

- source: Kritikos, A., & Bolle, F. (2004). Punishment as a public good. When should monopolists care about a consumer boycott?. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of consumers to punish a monopolist (e.g., via boycott) and thereby reduce prices depends on the number of consumers and the degree of solidarity among them. With few consumers, individual punishment can be effective and lead to lower prices. As the number of consumers increases, the incentive to individually punish diminishes (public good problem), and the monopolist can more easily charge higher (Cournot) prices unless solidarity is high. The model predicts that successful collective punishment (boycott) is more likely in small groups or when solidarity is strong, and less likely in large, uncoordinated groups.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how group size and solidarity affect the effectiveness of punishment in public-good-like environments, specifically in the context of consumer boycotts against monopolists. While it does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, it suggests that increasing group size without mechanisms for solidarity or coordination will reduce the effectiveness of punishment in improving group outcomes. For prediction tasks, this implies that punishment is less likely to improve efficiency in large, uncoordinated groups, but may be effective in small groups or when solidarity is high. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits its quantitative predictive value.

It is relevant for understanding the mechanisms behind the emergence of punishment and cooperation rules, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment.

However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its value is in informing the types of mechanisms and moderators that may be important, rather than providing direct predictive evidence for efficiency changes.

- source: Singh, M., & Boomsma, J. J. (2015). Policing and punishment across the domains of social evolution. *Oikos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper proposes that punishment and policing behaviors are maintained when the inclusive fitness benefits to the punisher outweigh the costs, and when these benefits are greater than those from bystanding. The framework is applied to a range of biological and social systems, showing that power, relatedness, and reproductive options drive variation in who punishes. In systems with strong power asymmetries, dominants are more likely to punish; in systems with high relatedness and low individual reproductive prospects, policing is more likely to be corrective and group-beneficial. The paper highlights that the same inclusive fitness logic can explain punishment across diverse domains, but does not provide empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a conceptual framework for understanding the conditions under which punishment is likely to emerge and be maintained in social groups, emphasizing the roles of power, relatedness, and reproductive options. However, it does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. For the downstream prediction task, the paper may inform mechanistic expectations about when punishment is likely to be effective or who will implement it, but it does not supply data or direct evidence to calibrate efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions.

- source: Henrich, J. (2004). Cultural group selection, coevolutionary processes and large-scale cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.

  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that cultural group selection, enabled by human-specific social learning mechanisms, can explain the evolution and stability of large-scale cooperation and punishment in humans. It theorizes that punishment, when stabilized by conformist transmission, can support high levels of cooperation even in large, anonymous groups, and that these group-beneficial equilibria can be selected for at the group level. The paper does not provide empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games, but it does argue that punishment and cooperation can be maintained at high frequency under plausible cultural evolutionary dynamics.

However, it does not provide empirical estimates or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff changes when punishment is enabled in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of group structure, cultural transmission, and norm enforcement (punishment) as moderators of cooperation and efficiency, but it does not specify how game design dimensions quantitatively affect efficiency outcomes.

- source: Mulder, L. B. (2018). When sanctions convey moral norms. *European Journal of Law and Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper argues that sanctions can have an expressive function, conveying moral norms and motivating compliance for moral reasons rather than purely instrumental ones. It reviews evidence that (1) retributive sanctions are more likely to induce moral norms than compensatory ones, (2) punishments are more effective than rewards at conveying obligatory moral rules, and (3) severe (but not unreasonably severe) sanctions are more likely to induce moral disapproval and compliance, especially when implemented by trustworthy and fair authorities. However, the paper also notes that sanctions can backfire, especially among those already engaging in the undesired behavior, and that excessive severity or unfairness can undermine moral norms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and psychological context for how punishment may affect compliance and norm internalization in public-goods-like environments, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. It suggests that the design and framing of punishment (retributive vs. compensatory, punishment vs. reward, severity, authority trustworthiness, and fairness) can moderate the effectiveness of sanctions in changing behavior. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative, as it does not report or synthesize efficiency or group payoff data. Its main value is in highlighting psychological mechanisms and moderators that could influence the effectiveness of punishment in experimental designs.

- source: Raihani, N. J., & McAuliffe, K. (2012). Does Inequity Aversion Motivate Punishment? Cleaner Fish as a Model System. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that in cleaner fish, punishment of cheating partners is not motivated by inequity aversion, as these fish do not show behavioral sensitivity to unequal outcomes in controlled experiments. Instead, punishment appears to be triggered by loss aversion—when the punisher experiences a reduction in expected payoff (e.g., when a client leaves after a partner cheats). In humans, both inequity aversion and loss aversion may motivate punishment, but the evidence is not conclusive. The paper reviews but does not present new data on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is useful for understanding the psychological or proximate mechanisms underlying punishment (loss aversion vs. inequity aversion), which may inform the design of future experiments or the interpretation of why punishment sometimes increases cooperation. However, it does not report or synthesize efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes in a way that can be directly mapped to the prediction task. Its relevance is primarily theoretical and mechanistic.

- source: Mariano, P., & Correia, L. (2015). THE GIVE AND TAKE GAME: ANALYSIS OF A RESOURCE SHARING GAME. *International Journal of Applied Mathematics and Computer Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in the Give and Take game, the existence of costly punishment (costs to both the punisher and the punished for the 'take' action) creates a strong incentive for cooperative, efficient sharing of the resource. When punishment costs are high enough, multiple Nash equilibria exist in which players alternate giving the resource, maximizing average payoffs and achieving Pareto optimality. If punishment is cheap or absent, exploitative strategies dominate and efficiency is lower. Simulations confirm that strategies which give the resource (cooperate) are more successful when punishment is costly, even if there is no bonus for giving. The game thus demonstrates that built-in, costly punishment can sustain efficient cooperation in resource sharing dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in two-player resource sharing games with built-in punishment, increasing the cost of punishment (to both punisher and punished) increases the efficiency of the game by supporting cooperative equilibria. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment with sufficiently high costs will increase efficiency relative to the control (no punishment) condition. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, turn-taking, indivisible resource games, but the mechanism—costly punishment deters exploitation and supports efficient cooperation—may generalize to adjacent public goods or resource sharing environments.

- source: Feinberg, Y., & Kets, W. (2014). Ranking friends. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of social interactions—specifically, the ranking profiles that determine the order in which players seek help—critically affects the ability to sustain full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) in repeated helping games. When punishment is available (either community or bilateral), full cooperation can be sustained if and only if incentive constraints are satisfied. These constraints are least restrictive (i.e., efficiency is most easily sustained) when expected costs are equalized across players (Latin square ranking profiles). Under bilateral punishment, even stricter symmetry (BFF) is required. The paper provides explicit formulas for the minimum discount factor required to sustain efficiency under each regime. Thus, the design of the interaction structure and the punishment regime jointly determine whether efficiency is achievable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated helping games with sequential requests and costly helping. It shows that the ability of punishment to sustain efficiency depends on the structure of interactions (ranking profiles) and the punishment regime. When the structure equalizes expected costs (Latin square), punishment is most effective at sustaining efficiency. If the structure is unbalanced, punishment is less effective, and efficiency may not be sustainable. The results are theoretical but provide explicit conditions and formulas that can be mapped to efficiency predictions in similar repeated public-goods-like environments, especially where helping is sequential and punishment is either community-wide or bilateral. However, the model is not a standard PGG, so direct quantitative predictions for standard PGGs should be made with caution.

- source: Choi, J. K. (2007). Trembles may support cooperation in a repeated prisoner's dilemma game. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of trembles (errors) in repeated prisoner's dilemma games can support the evolutionary stability of conditional cooperation (punishment-based strategies), but only when the benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation is not too high. Specifically, if the benefit of cooperation is less than or equal to twice the cost (b ≤ 2c), then the ALLT (all trigger) equilibrium is asymptotically stable for sufficiently high continuation probability and low error rate. If the benefit is too high (b > 2c), then the equilibrium becomes unstable, and unconditional cooperators can invade, undermining the effectiveness of punishment. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using explicit payoff calculations and stability conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in supporting cooperation (and thus efficiency) in repeated prisoner's dilemma-like environments depends critically on the benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation and the presence of errors. For prediction tasks, it suggests that punishment will only improve efficiency when the benefit-to-cost ratio is not too high and when errors are present but not too frequent. The results are most directly applicable to two-player repeated games with trigger strategies, but the logic may extend to public goods games with similar strategic structure. The findings highlight that increasing the benefit of cooperation does not always make punishment more effective; in fact, it can undermine stability if too large. The paper does not provide direct evidence for multi-player public goods games, but the mechanisms discussed are relevant for adjacent settings.

- source: Dubey, S. P., Kedar, G. D., & Ghate, S. H. (2021). A COMMUNICATION NETWORK ROUTING PROBLEM: MODELING AND OPTIMIZATION USING NON-COOPERATIVE GAME THEORY. *International Journal of Applied Mathematics and Computer Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a non-cooperative network routing game with a punishment mechanism (increased cost for exceeding link capacity), the efficiency of outcomes (minimized total cost) depends on the structure of the cost function and the distribution of user demands. When the cost function is linear in the number of packets, all Nash equilibria are efficient (Pareto optimal). When the cost function is convex, only some Nash equilibria (about 50% of preferred focal Nash equilibria) are efficient, and this depends on the relative size of user demands. The punishment mechanism (cost increase) can deter greedy strategies and promote more efficient, balanced transmission strategies, but does not guarantee efficiency in all cases. The model is adjacent to public goods games in that it involves individual versus collective use of a shared resource, but differs in the details of the payoff and punishment structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment mechanisms implemented as increased costs for overuse can improve efficiency in resource-sharing games, but the effect depends on the cost function and the distribution of demands. For prediction tasks, the model suggests that punishment (costly overuse) can align Nash equilibria with efficient outcomes under certain conditions (e.g., linear cost), but not always (e.g., convex cost). The findings are most relevant for adjacent settings to public goods games where the 'punishment' is a proportional cost rather than a direct deduction or fine. The results support the idea that the design of the punishment mechanism (cost structure) and the symmetry of player demands are important moderators of efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Rauwolf, P., & Bryson, J. J. (2018). Expectations of Fairness and Trust Co-Evolve in Environments of Partial Information. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary simulations of a one-shot trust game with partner choice and partial information, both trust (willingness to invest with unknown partners) and costly punishment (rejecting profitable but unfair offers) can co-evolve and are adaptive, maximizing average payoffs. The benefit of punishment for efficiency only arises when trust is also present, and only in environments where information about partners is partially but not fully available. When information is fully transparent or there is no partner choice, punishment does not increase payoffs. The effect is robust to changes in the number of partners, population size, investment multiplier, selection intensity, and mutation rates. The model predicts that as the value of investment increases, the advantage and prevalence of fairness norms and punishment also increase.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the efficiency benefit of punishment in trust-game-like environments depends critically on the presence of both partner choice and partial information. If a public-goods or trust game is designed with multiple potential partners and only partial information about others' behavior, enabling punishment (the ability to reject unfair offers) can increase group efficiency, but only if players are also willing to trust unknown partners. If either partner choice or trust is absent, punishment does not improve efficiency. The model also predicts that the efficiency benefit of punishment increases with the investment multiplier (analogous to MPCR in PGGs). For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by information structure, partner choice, and the value of cooperation.

- source: Chassy, P., Cole, J., & Brennan, C. (2025). Evolution of confrontation and cooperation in simple organisms as a function of environmental resources and cost of a conflict. *Expert Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
