  findings: The simulations show that the confrontation rate in a population is highly sensitive to the magnitude of punishment (cost of conflict) and reward (resource availability). As punishment increases, confrontation rates decrease, and organisms become more cooperative. The adaptation is driven by the evolution of gene profiles that determine the probability of confrontation. The model also finds that memory (even a simple one-step memory) has a limited but significant effect on confrontation rates, especially as punishment increases. The study provides evidence that risk-taking and confrontation are shaped by both environmental parameters and genetic/cognitive factors, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the cost or effectiveness of punishment in a confrontation/cooperation environment reduces the frequency of confrontational (defecting) behavior and increases cooperation. While the model is not a public goods game, the findings suggest that in environments where punishment is more costly or effective, populations evolve to be more cooperative, which would likely increase efficiency in analogous PGG-like settings. However, the lack of direct efficiency or group payoff measures limits the ability to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from design dimensions. The results are most useful for understanding the qualitative direction of punishment effects in related strategic environments.

- source: Armstrong, K., Zhong, Y. M., Bhide, S., Grant, S. B., Birkland, T., & Berglund, E. Z. (2024). Simulating the emergence of institutions that reverse freshwater salinization: An agent-based modeling approach. *Journal of Hydrology X*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The agent-based model demonstrates that self-organized institutions with sanctions (fines for defectors, monitoring) can emerge and stabilize resource use, keeping salt concentrations below harmful thresholds when defection rates are low and innovation rates are moderate to high. High defection rates undermine institution effectiveness, leading to resource degradation. The model highlights the importance of cooperation, monitoring, and sanctions in sustaining common-pool resources, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff as in public goods games. The findings are most relevant for understanding the emergence and stability of sanctioning institutions in resource dilemmas, rather than for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment and monitoring can improve resource outcomes in social dilemmas, especially when defection is not too prevalent and agents can innovate or imitate successful strategies. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical data on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it manipulate standard PGG design dimensions. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGG-like games, this paper offers contextual and mechanistic insights but not direct predictive evidence. Its main value is in illustrating how sanctions and institutional emergence can stabilize cooperation and resource quality in agent-based models of common-pool resource dilemmas.

- source: He, J. J., Liu, J. S., Qiu, Z. P., Nanzayi, N. E., & Zhang, M. (2021). How Does the R&D Cooperation Path of Supply and Demand Network Enterprises Evolve?. *Ieee Access*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of cooperation in R&D networks depends on the balance of knowledge spillover, government reward, government penalty (punishment), cooperation cost, and cooperation risk. Increasing government penalty (punishment) and reward, and decreasing cost and risk, promote the stability of cooperation. The model shows that under certain parameter regimes, cooperation becomes evolutionarily stable, while in others, selfishness dominates. The effect of punishment (penalty) is to accelerate and stabilize cooperation, but the paper does not quantify efficiency or group payoff. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulation of the evolutionary game model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that introducing or increasing punishment (government penalty) in a social dilemma-like environment can promote cooperation, especially when combined with rewards and low costs/risks. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that, in similar networked cooperation settings, punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, which may translate to higher efficiency if payoffs are aligned with cooperation. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative direction of punishment effects and the importance of cost, risk, and reward as moderators.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, Y., Chen, Q., Gao, L. Y., & He, M. F. (2022). Effects of quasi-defection strategy on cooperation evolution in social dilemma. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of quasi-defectors—who can both reward cooperators and punish defectors—enables the stable coexistence of cooperation and quasi-defection, and can suppress defection under certain parameter regimes. The effect of punishment on cooperation is positive but limited: increasing punishment initially increases cooperation, but further increases have diminishing returns. Reward can compensate for the limited effect of punishment and further promote cooperation. The cost of rewarding/punishing must be low for quasi-defectors to persist and support cooperation. The model is theoretical and does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that mechanisms combining reward and punishment can sustain higher levels of cooperation than punishment alone.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that combining punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemmas, and that the effect of punishment on cooperation is positive but limited by cost and diminishing returns. However, because the model is based on a prisoner's dilemma with a quasi-defection strategy and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct applicability to predicting efficiency in public goods games is limited. The findings suggest that, in analogous settings, moderate punishment and reward can increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency would depend on the cost structure and the mapping from strategy proportions to group payoff.

- source: Pei, H. Y., & Yan, G. H. (2022). Punishment and reputation based partners-switching promotes cooperation in social networks. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that a mechanism combining behavioral punishment (severing ties with defectors) and reputation-based partner selection leads to high levels of cooperation in a dynamic networked prisoner's dilemma. The frequency of partner switching (time scale ratio W) is a key moderator: higher W leads to higher cooperation, and there is a critical W above which cooperation dominates. The effect is robust to temptation to defect and network degree, though higher values of these parameters require higher W for cooperation to prevail. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results suggest that cooperation can be sustained or reach fixation under the right conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, behavioral punishment via partner switching) can promote cooperation in dynamic networked social dilemmas. However, the model is a prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that in environments where individuals can sever ties with defectors and form new ties based on reputation, cooperation is likely to be higher, especially when partner switching is frequent. For the prediction task, this paper supports the idea that punishment and partner selection mechanisms can increase cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like settings.

- source: Geng, Y. N., Shen, C., Hu, K. P., & Shi, L. (2018). Impact of punishment on the evolution of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a punishment mechanism—where players with below-average payoffs are punished by a reduction in their fitness coefficient—substantially increases the stationary frequency of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model. Increasing the punishment rate and memory length both further promote cooperation, leading to full dominance of cooperation under many parameter settings. The mechanism's effect is attributed to increased heterogeneity in individual fitness, which supports network reciprocity. However, the paper does not report on group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma environments, especially when punishment is more intense and lasts longer. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, and because the game is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, the findings should be transferred to PGG-like efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that, in similar spatial or networked environments, enabling punishment may increase cooperation, which could plausibly increase efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The specific punishment mechanism (punishing those with below-average payoffs) is also nonstandard compared to typical PGG punishment designs.

- source: Song, M., Ge, Y. H., & Liu, J. S. (2020). Knowledge Input or Not in the Dynamic Cooperation Behavior of Top Management Teams? From the Perspective of Knowledge Flow. *Ieee Access*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The model shows that increasing knowledge flow, government reward, and government penalty (punishment) promotes the selection of the cooperative (knowledge input) strategy by TMTs, while higher input costs hinder cooperation. Under some parameter regimes, mutual cooperation (input, input) is the stable outcome; in others, only one party inputs knowledge. The effect of government penalty (punishment) is to increase the likelihood of cooperation, but the paper does not quantify efficiency or group payoff. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulation of the evolutionary dynamics, not on empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that the presence and magnitude of punishment (government penalty) can increase the likelihood of cooperative behavior in a two-player, repeated interaction setting with knowledge input as the cooperative act. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency changes from punishment in public goods games. The model's structure and findings may be useful for mechanism or moderator reasoning, especially regarding the effect of punishment cost and effectiveness, but the lack of payoff-based outcomes limits its direct predictive value for efficiency.

- source: Vukov, J., Santos, F. C., & Pacheco, J. M. (2012). Cognitive strategies take advantage of the cooperative potential of heterogeneous networks. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to use reactive strategies (punishing defectors and rewarding cooperators based on memory of past actions) leads to high and robust levels of cooperation in scale-free networks, even when participation costs are present. The effect is much stronger than for unconditional strategies. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperative acts, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is based on the prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, but the mechanisms of punishment/retaliation and reward are closely related. The findings suggest that cognitive strategies can overcome the negative effects of participation costs and defector invasion on cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment/retaliation mechanisms (as part of cognitive/reactive strategies) can sustain high cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas, even with participation costs and frequent defector invasion. However, because the model is a prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The main signal is that punishment/retaliation can be highly effective in maintaining cooperation under adverse conditions, especially when players can condition their behavior on others' past actions. The findings are most relevant for settings with network structure, memory, and the possibility of both punishment and reward.

- source: Xue, B. Y., & Hu, Y. H. (2025). Dynamic analysis of evolutionary game considering individual influence. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that introducing influential cooperators, who can punish defectors (via a payoff penalty theta), can suppress defection and promote cooperation in a population. The prevalence of cooperation, influential cooperation, and defection depends on the temptation to defect (b), the strength of punishment (theta), and the weight individuals place on payoff versus influence (rho). Higher punishment for defectors (theta) reduces the proportion of defectors and can eliminate them entirely at high values, leading to more cooperation. However, the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect on overall welfare is not directly analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that punishment (modeled as a penalty for defectors) can reduce defection and promote cooperation in social dilemma settings. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding the qualitative impact of punishment parameters (such as cost and effectiveness) on cooperation rates, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wang, J. W., Xu, W. S., Zhang, X. J., Zhao, N. X., & Yu, F. Y. (2023). Redistribution based on willingness to cooperate promotes cooperation while intensifying equality in heterogeneous populations. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation can persist in the presence of corrupt central sanctioning institutions, provided the effectiveness of sanctions (exclusion probability) is high and the corruption rate (embezzlement) is not too large. The model shows that players are more willing to tolerate exploitation by corrupt third parties than by free-riders, and that the evolutionary stability of cooperation depends on the balance between the benefits of cooperation, the costs of joining/leaving the institution, the intensity of exclusion, and the corruption rate. However, the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes, focusing instead on the prevalence of cooperation and trust strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (exclusion) and the cost/benefit structure are critical for sustaining cooperation, even when institutions are corrupt. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The results imply that punishment can support cooperation under a range of conditions, but the translation to efficiency is not explicit. The model is adjacent to public goods games but differs in structure (prisoner's dilemma with exclusion and embezzlement).

- source: Boehm, C. (1997). Impact of the human egalitarian syndrome on Darwinian selection mechanics. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes that in prehistoric human forager societies, egalitarian social norms—enforced by moralistic punishment and social sanctions—dampened within-group competition and amplified between-group variation. This, in turn, empowered group selection and enabled the evolution and maintenance of altruistic and cooperative behaviors, despite the presence of free riders. The author argues that active punishment and social isolation of noncooperators reduced the free-rider problem, making group-beneficial traits more likely to persist. However, these arguments are theoretical and based on ethnographic and evolutionary reasoning, not on experimental or quantitative data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment and social sanctions can promote cooperation and suppress free riding in group contexts, which is relevant to public goods game environments. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it specify how game design dimensions (such as punishment cost, player count, or MPCR) would moderate these effects. Its value for prediction is limited to supporting the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation, not for estimating efficiency outcomes in specific game designs.

- source: Chen, Y. S., Lin, H., & Wu, C. X. (2007). Evolution of prisoner's dilemma strategies on scale-free networks. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that (1) lower average degree and higher clustering coefficient in scale-free networks promote higher cooperation frequency in the iterated prisoner's dilemma; (2) introducing a punishment mechanism, where unsatisfied cooperators can sever and rewire links to defectors with some probability, further increases the frequency and persistence of cooperation. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and all results are based on simulation of strategy frequencies and network structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation frequency in networked iterated prisoner's dilemma environments, especially when combined with certain network structures. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms by which punishment can promote cooperation, but should be used with caution for payoff-based prediction tasks.

- source: Schlapfer, A. (2024). Cross-cultural differences in retaliation: Evidence from the soccer field. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds, at a theoretical level, that polycentric governance structures are more likely to support the emergence and stability of group-beneficial norms, including costly punishment, by satisfying four key conditions: (1) minimizing within-group variance, (2) reducing within-group selective pressures against prosocial traits (e.g., via effective punishment), (3) increasing between-group selective pressures, and (4) maximizing between-group variance. It argues that punishment can transform altruistic traits into individually rational behaviors when punishment is widespread and coordinated, and that polycentricity supports the self-sorting and coordination needed for this. However, the paper does not provide empirical data or direct predictions about efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic support for the idea that punishment, when embedded in polycentric or self-sorting group structures, can enhance cooperation and group-beneficial outcomes. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency or welfare depends on the ability of groups to self-sort, coordinate, and monitor, and on the cost structure of punishment. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of group structure, self-sorting, and the scaling of punishment costs, rather than in providing parameterized estimates.

- source: Ishida, J. (2009). Incentives in academics: Collaboration under weak complementarities. *Labour Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated game with voluntary team formation, optimal contracts may use high-powered team incentives and low-powered individual incentives to exploit peer monitoring and reputation effects. This can make collaboration sustainable and efficient even with weak complementarities, overcoming the free-rider problem present in static settings. However, when the opportunity to collaborate is frequent and agents are patient, the optimal contract may under-incentivize individual effort, leading to inefficiency when collaboration is not possible. Thus, repeated interaction and peer sanctions can both improve and harm efficiency, depending on parameter values (discount factor, frequency of collaboration opportunities, degree of complementarities).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of enabling peer sanctions (punishment via reputation loss and exclusion from collaboration) in repeated public-goods-like environments depends on the structure of incentives and the frequency of collaboration opportunities. When peer sanctions are possible, efficiency can increase by overcoming free-rider problems, but if individual incentives are weakened too much to make peer sanctions more effective, efficiency can decrease when collaboration is not possible. For prediction, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not unconditionally positive: it depends on whether the contract design balances team and individual incentives, the patience of agents, and the frequency of opportunities for collaboration. The model is adjacent to public goods games, with peer sanctions implemented via exclusion and reputation rather than explicit costly punishment.

- source: Abdulkadiroglu, A., & Bagwell, K. (2013). Trust, Reciprocity, and Favors in Cooperative Relationships. *American Economic Journal-microeconomics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated trust games with private information, efficiency (total group payoff) can be increased above the autarky (Nash) level through favor-exchange relationships (dynamic reciprocity) or through symmetric punishment (negative reciprocity). The optimal equilibrium structure depends on the parameters: when information asymmetries are low, symmetric punishment can support higher efficiency than favor-exchange. Hybrid equilibria (honeymoon period followed by favor-exchange or punishment) can further improve efficiency in some cases. The paper provides explicit formulas for the efficiency achieved under each regime. Immediate reciprocity (punishment for non-reciprocation) is not always necessary; dynamic reciprocity (future favor) is often sufficient. The model is not a PGG but is closely related in its focus on cooperation, trust, and efficiency under repeated interaction and private information.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical predictions for how efficiency in repeated cooperation games is affected by the availability and structure of punishment and favor-exchange mechanisms. While not a PGG, the model is adjacent and the efficiency formulas and comparative statics are informative for predicting efficiency in environments where punishment (symmetric, infrequent) is possible versus when only favor-exchange is available. The results suggest that, in some parameter regions, introducing symmetric punishment can increase efficiency above favor-exchange alone, especially when information asymmetries are low. However, the model is limited to two players and does not include standard PGG features like group size or marginal per-capita return. The findings are most useful for understanding the theoretical potential of punishment to enhance efficiency in repeated cooperation with private information, but direct quantitative prediction for PGGs would require caution.

- source: Gutnisky, D. A., & Zanutto, B. S. (2004). Cooperation in the iterated prisoner's dilemma is learned by operant conditioning mechanisms. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The operant conditioning model (ZL) can learn to cooperate in the IPD and achieves high average payoffs against a variety of strategies. In evolutionary simulations, ZL can dominate or survive in populations depending on the mix of strategies and the presence of noise (random moves). However, ZL is always slightly less efficient than non-learning strategies because it requires time to learn optimal responses. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; instead, it relies on reinforcement learning from payoffs. The findings are relevant for understanding how learning mechanisms can support cooperation and efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, but do not directly address the effect of enabling or disabling punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does, however, provide insight into how learning mechanisms (operant conditioning) can support cooperation and high payoffs in repeated dyadic dilemmas (IPD). The absence of explicit punishment or sanctioning means that the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that learning-based strategies can achieve high efficiency, but may be less efficient than fixed strategies in the short run due to learning costs. For prediction tasks focused on punishment-enabled vs. punishment-disabled efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, contextual support.

- source: Ho, T. H. (1996). Finite automata play repeated prisoner's dilemma with information processing costs. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with boundedly rational agents (finite automata), cooperation and high efficiency can evolve even from hostile initial conditions, as long as there are no costs for strategy complexity. (2) Imposing a cost on the size of the automaton (i.e., the 'storage' or monitoring cost) destroys cooperation and reduces efficiency, with populations converging to simple, defecting strategies. (3) Imposing a cost on the frequency of switching states (i.e., 'retrieval' or attention cost) does not harm, and may even promote, cooperation and efficiency, especially if the cost is quadratic. (4) The results are robust to a range of simulation parameters. (5) The findings suggest that the form of cognitive or implementation cost is a critical moderator of whether punishment-like strategies (contingent defection) can sustain cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated two-player games structurally similar to public goods games (but not group PGGs), the ability to implement contingent punishment (e.g., Tit-for-Tat or more sophisticated automata) is crucial for sustaining high efficiency. However, if the game design imposes costs on monitoring or strategy complexity (analogous to high punishment cost or high cognitive burden), efficiency collapses and simple, non-cooperative strategies dominate. In contrast, costs on the frequency of switching (analogous to costs for changing behavior or attention) do not undermine efficiency. For prediction, this suggests that the presence and form of punishment, and especially the cost structure for implementing contingent strategies, are key moderators of the efficiency effect. The results are most directly relevant to two-player, long-horizon, repeated games with deterministic feedback and no communication.

- source: Ochea, M. I. (2013). Evolution of repeated prisoner's dilemma play under logit dynamics. *Journal of Economic Dynamics & Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with a finite set of memory-one strategies, the long-run average payoff (efficiency) and the prevalence of cooperation depend critically on the mix of strategies present and the game parameters. In some ecologies, strategies like Pavlov (WSLS), which can be interpreted as implementing endogenous punishment (by defecting after being defected upon), can dominate and sustain high payoffs, especially when unconditional cooperators are absent. However, the introduction of unconditional cooperators (AllC) can destabilize cooperation and lead to the dominance of defectors (AllD), reducing efficiency. The dynamics can be complex, with cycles and chaos emerging for intermediate values of the intensity of choice parameter. The paper provides explicit formulas for average payoffs and analyzes bifurcations in the system as parameters change. Overall, the effect of 'punishment-like' strategies on efficiency is mixed and highly sensitive to the strategic environment and parameter values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like strategies (e.g., Pavlov/WSLS) on efficiency in repeated social dilemma games is highly context-dependent. In environments without unconditional cooperators, such strategies can sustain cooperation and high payoffs, but their effectiveness is undermined when unconditional cooperators are present. The results suggest that the presence or absence of certain strategy types (especially unconditional cooperators) and the values of key parameters (benefit of cooperation, error rates, intensity of choice) are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment-like mechanisms. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of enabling punishment (or punishment-like strategies) on efficiency cannot be determined solely by the presence of punishment, but must account for the broader strategic ecology and parameter settings. The findings are most directly relevant to repeated PD-like environments, but may inform predictions in PGG-like settings by analogy.

- source: Amegashie, J. A., & Runkel, M. (2012). The Paradox of Revenge in Conflicts. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that, contrary to conventional wisdom, the desire for revenge does not always escalate conflict. In a two-period contest model, the anticipation of revenge (self-deterrence effect) can lead factions to reduce their initial effort, resulting in lower total conflict costs (higher efficiency) than in a setting without revenge motives. The paradox is most likely when the benefit of revenge is highly elastic to past destruction or when there are asymmetries in the value of revenge or resource valuation between factions. The model provides explicit comparative statics and closed-form solutions for aggregate effort with and without revenge, showing conditions under which efficiency is improved by the possibility of revenge.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the introduction of a punishment/revenge mechanism can, under certain conditions, increase efficiency (reduce total cost) in conflict-like games by inducing self-deterrence. While the setting is a two-player, two-period conflict game (not a standard public goods game), the mechanism is closely related to punishment in repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that, when the threat of punishment is credible and especially when the benefit of punishment is highly sensitive to prior harm, the net effect on efficiency can be positive. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the possibility that enabling punishment can increase efficiency, especially in asymmetric or highly elastic settings, but the result is context-dependent and may not generalize to all PGG-like environments.

- source: Trejo, K. K., Juarez, R., Clempner, J. B., & Poznyak, A. S. (2023). Non-Cooperative Bargaining with Unsophisticated Agents. *Computational Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing penalty mechanisms for deviation and time in bargaining games with myopic agents ensures convergence to efficient (Pareto optimal or Nash) outcomes, but at a cost: the process is slower and less efficient than with fully rational, forward-looking agents. The efficiency loss is quantifiable as the 'price of unsophistication,' and depends on the penalty parameters and the agents' behavior. The results are robust across a wide class of utility functions and game structures, including Markov processes. The paper does not study public goods games or peer punishment, but the penalty mechanism is analogous to centralized sanctions rather than decentralized peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of peer punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies bargaining games with centralized penalty mechanisms and myopic agents. However, it provides indirect evidence that introducing penalty costs can improve convergence to efficient outcomes even with unsophisticated agents, though always with some efficiency loss relative to the ideal. The results suggest that the design and magnitude of penalties (punishment costs) are critical for achieving near-efficient outcomes, but the context is bargaining, not public goods provision. The findings may be useful for understanding the general role of sanctions in promoting efficiency, but should be transferred to PGG-like settings with caution.

- source: Bowles, S., & Gintis, H. (2004). Persistent parochialism: trust and exclusion in ethnic networks. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that parochial networks can achieve higher efficiency (average payoff) than anonymous markets in social dilemma environments, due to better information and informal enforcement (via monitoring and exclusion). However, the relationship between parochialism and efficiency is non-linear: too little parochialism leads to poor information and low trust, while too much parochialism reduces trading opportunities and group size, also lowering efficiency. The model includes a monitoring/inspection strategy (analogous to punishment), which, when information is sufficiently accurate, supports a stable trust equilibrium with higher payoffs. The optimal efficiency is achieved at an intermediate level of parochialism, balancing enforcement and trading opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (monitoring, exclusion) on efficiency in social dilemma games depends on the information structure and group composition. Efficiency is maximized at an intermediate level of parochialism, where information is good enough to support trust and informal enforcement, but group size and trading opportunities are not too restricted. The model suggests that enabling punishment/monitoring can increase efficiency relative to a control (anonymous market) when information is sufficiently accurate and group size is within a viable range. However, excessive exclusion (high parochialism) can reduce efficiency by limiting opportunities. The findings are most relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where punishment is implemented via monitoring and exclusion, rather than costly direct punishment as in standard PGGs.

- source: Auerswald, H., Schmidt, C., Thum, M., & Torsvik, G. (2018). Teams in a public goods experiment with punishment. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The evolutionary IPD with choice and refusal of partners (IPD/CR) produces higher average fitness (group efficiency) than random partner matching, with most runs evolving to near full cooperation. The average fitness clusters around a few regions, with the highest corresponding to mutual cooperation. However, if the refusal payoff is high or players are highly intolerant of defection, populations can become trapped in low-efficiency wallflower states. Sensitivity analyses show that the refusal payoff, minimum tolerance level, and memory weight are key moderators: high refusal payoffs or high intolerance increase the risk of social isolation and lower efficiency. Increasing population size reduces the risk of wallflower ecologies. The findings are robust across different strategy representations and parameter sweeps.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling partner selection (choice and refusal) in repeated social dilemmas can increase group efficiency compared to random matching, especially when refusal payoffs and intolerance are not too high. For prediction tasks, the presence of a partner selection mechanism (even in the absence of explicit costly punishment) can be expected to increase efficiency, but only if the cost/benefit structure of refusal does not incentivize social isolation. The results are most directly relevant to settings where agents can avoid defectors, but less so to standard public goods games with centralized punishment. The findings suggest that game design dimensions such as the cost of refusal, tolerance thresholds, and population size are critical moderators of the efficiency effect.

- source: Gokhale, C. S., Frean, M., & Rainey, P. B. (2023). Eco-evolutionary Logic of Mutualisms. *Dynamic Games and Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability and persistence of mutualisms (modeled as multiplayer snowdrift games) are strongly influenced by the balance between intra- and inter-species interactions, as well as by ecological factors such as seasonality and population density. The inclusion of within-species interactions and eco-evolutionary feedbacks can stabilize mutualisms and maintain high group-level productivity (efficiency) even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. The model shows that nonlinearities, group size, and environmental fluctuations can qualitatively change the evolutionary outcomes, sometimes allowing coexistence of cooperative and selfish types, and sometimes leading to dominance or extinction. The framework is general and extensible, but the paper does not provide direct evidence or predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the efficiency of multiplayer public-goods-like games (here, mutualisms modeled as snowdrift games) can be maintained or stabilized by ecological and evolutionary feedbacks, even without explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. It suggests that predictions of efficiency should account for intra- and inter-group interactions, group size, seasonality, and density-dependence. However, because the paper does not model or analyze punishment or sanctioning directly, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. Its main value for the prediction task is in highlighting the importance of ecological and evolutionary context, and in providing a general modeling framework that could be extended to include punishment or reward.

- source: Sasaki, T., Okada, I., & Nakai, Y. (2016). Indirect reciprocity can overcome free-rider problems on costly moral assessment. *Biology Letters*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in indirect reciprocity models with costly assessment, cooperation is not stable unless there is a mechanism to sanction or exclude those who evade the cost of assessment. By introducing a pre-assessment stage that labels non-payers as 'nasty' and excludes them from receiving help, the model shows that a cooperative equilibrium (where everyone pays for assessment and helps others) can be locally stable if the benefit of helping exceeds the cost of assessment. This mechanism is analogous to pool punishment in public goods games and is effective under leading social norms. The findings are theoretical but directly address efficiency and the conditions for stable cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that institutional mechanisms to sanction or exclude cost-evaders (such as pre-assessment or pool punishment) can significantly improve efficiency in social dilemma games with costly monitoring or punishment. For prediction tasks, the presence of a pre-assessment or institutionalized punishment mechanism is likely to shift efficiency from low (collapse of cooperation) to high (stable cooperation), provided the net benefit of cooperation is positive. The results are robust across different social norms and suggest that the design of the punishment/sanctioning mechanism is a key moderator of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Fletcher, J. A., & Zwick, M. (2006). Unifying the theories of inclusive fitness and reciprocal altruism. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper demonstrates that the evolution of cooperation (altruism) in both kin and non-kin contexts can be predicted by a generalized Hamilton's rule that incorporates the covariance between genotypes and the cooperative behaviors of others, as well as nonadditive (synergistic) effects. In iterated prisoner's dilemma models, conditional strategies like tit-for-tat can increase in frequency when the benefit received from others' cooperation exceeds the cost, as predicted by Queller's rule. The same logic applies to mutualistic interactions between species. The paper shows that synergy (nonadditivity) and conditional behavior can both facilitate the evolution of cooperation, even in the absence of kinship or repeated interactions. Punishment is discussed as a possible mechanism for positive assortment but is not modeled. The findings are theoretical and do not provide empirical estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical background for understanding how mechanisms like punishment, synergy, and conditional behavior can affect the evolution of cooperation and group efficiency, but it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It suggests that any mechanism (including punishment) that increases positive assortment or synergy can increase the likelihood that cooperation evolves, but does not specify the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes in experimental settings. For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, this paper is useful for mechanistic context but not for direct quantitative prediction.

- source: Ognedal, T. (2016). Morality in the market. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Increasing the fraction of honest sellers can reduce or increase social surplus depending on the initial prevalence of honesty and the elasticity of demand. When honesty is rare, more honesty can increase production costs and reduce welfare; when honesty is common, more honesty can improve efficiency. (2) Sanctions (punishment) are a more reliable tool for improving efficiency, as they do not distort the ranking of private and social costs. (3) The effects of honesty among buyers and sellers are asymmetric: honesty among sellers affects production efficiency, while honesty among buyers affects allocation efficiency. (4) Policies that increase individual compliance (e.g., moral suasion) may have no effect or even negative effects on market-level efficiency, depending on market structure and the distribution of honesty. (5) The relationship between individual-level compliance and market-level efficiency is complex and non-linear.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency is more reliably positive than the effect of increasing intrinsic motivation (morality/honesty). In environments analogous to public goods games with market entry/exit and endogenous prices, punishment that is uniform across agents improves efficiency by aligning private and social costs, while increasing the fraction of intrinsically honest agents can have mixed or even negative effects on efficiency, especially when honesty is rare. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment (extrinsic sanctions) is more likely to increase efficiency than relying on intrinsic motivation, and that the effect of punishment may depend on the distribution of compliance and market structure. However, the model is not a direct PGG or experimental result, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution.

- source: Reiersen, J. (2017). TRUST AS A BOOSTER. *Journal of Business Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that trust is essential for enabling players to leave secure networks and engage in market transactions that offer higher potential payoffs but also greater risk. The model shows that the willingness to transact in markets depends on the expected probability of encountering trustworthy partners, the relative payoffs from network versus market transactions, and the cost of being cheated. When trust is high and/or the gains from market transactions are large relative to networks, players are more likely to leave networks and realize higher efficiency (total payoff). Conversely, when trust is low or the cost of being cheated is high, players remain in networks, foregoing potential efficiency gains. The model highlights the distinction between trust (needed for market transactions) and assurance (provided by networks through repeated interaction and informal sanctions).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how efficiency (expected group payoff) in environments with and without trust/assurance mechanisms depends on the structure of payoffs, risk, and expectations about others' behavior. While it does not directly model public goods games or experimental punishment, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where players can choose between secure, repeated interactions (with informal sanctions) and riskier, anonymous interactions (requiring trust). The model suggests that efficiency gains from moving to market-like environments depend on the level of trust and the relative payoffs, and that mechanisms providing assurance (such as punishment or repeated interaction) can substitute for trust in supporting cooperation. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment in public goods games, so its relevance for predicting the effect of enabling punishment is indirect and primarily theoretical.

- source: Huang, S. X., Liu, X. S., Hu, Y. H., & Fu, X. (2023). The influence of aggressive behavior on cooperation evolution in social dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing aggressive behavior (a form of costly punishment) into a prisoner's dilemma game can promote the persistence of cooperation by deterring defectors. Aggressive cooperators are able to suppress defectors, especially when the benefit of aggression (compensation) is high and the risk (mutual loss) is not too great. The system can reach various equilibria, including coexistence of multiple strategies, depending on parameter values. When the temptation to defect is low and aggressive compensation is high, cooperation dominates. As the temptation to defect increases, defectors re-emerge, but aggressive defectors can also help limit the dominance of non-aggressive defectors, allowing some cooperation to survive. The findings suggest that punishment-like mechanisms can improve group outcomes compared to a baseline with only cooperation and defection, though the paper does not quantify efficiency in the standard sense.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that introducing a punishment-like mechanism (aggressive behavior) in a prisoner's dilemma environment can increase group payoff and support cooperation, especially when the cost-benefit tradeoff of punishment is favorable and the temptation to defect is not too high. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (with moderate cost and sufficient effectiveness) is likely to improve efficiency relative to a no-punishment baseline, particularly in environments where the risk of mutual punishment is not prohibitive. However, the lack of explicit efficiency ratios and the focus on evolutionary stability rather than direct payoff comparisons limit the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Cordes, C., Richerson, P. J., & Schwesinger, G. (2010). How corporate cultures coevolve with the business environment: The case of firm growth crises and industry evolution. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation within firms is sustainable up to a critical group size, after which opportunistic behavior spreads and performance declines. In innovative, uncertain environments, cooperative cultures provide a competitive advantage, but as firms grow, the influence of informal leadership wanes and cooperation collapses. In mature, stable environments, large firms can succeed by implementing monitoring regimes and realizing economies of scale, even with more opportunistic employees. The model links these dynamics to firm growth crises, industry shakeouts, and the evolution of market structure. While the model is not a direct PGG or punishment experiment, it provides theoretical insight into how group size, monitoring, and social learning affect cooperation and firm performance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that group size and the presence or absence of monitoring (analogous to punishment) are key moderators of cooperation and efficiency in group settings. It suggests that in small groups, informal cooperation can sustain high performance, but as group size increases, cooperation collapses unless formal monitoring (akin to punishment) is introduced. The model implies that enabling monitoring or sanctions in large groups can restore or maintain efficiency, especially in stable environments. However, the findings are not directly transferable to PGG lab experiments, as the context is organizational and the outcomes are modeled rather than measured. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative effects of group size and monitoring on efficiency, rather than for quantitative prediction.

- source: Li, S. J., Bao, W., Dai, Y. Y., Ye, Y., & Xie, N. G. (2022). Emotional game and the evolution of cooperation considering the effects of reputation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that adding a reputation mechanism based on emotional profiles to a voluntary prisoner's dilemma game (VPDG) substantially increases both the frequency of cooperation and the average payoff of the population. The reputation mechanism works by making individuals more likely to cooperate with those who have good reputations and less likely to cooperate with those who have bad reputations, effectively integrating both reward and punishment effects. Compared to previous models (VPDG alone and VPDG with emotion but no reputation), the model with reputation achieves higher average payoffs and more stable cooperation, especially in regions of the parameter space where cooperation would otherwise be low. The effect is strongest when the temptation to defect is not too high (T < 1.5), but even at higher temptations, the reputation mechanism maintains higher cooperation and payoff than the baselines.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that mechanisms integrating both reward and punishment via reputation can substantially increase group efficiency in voluntary public-goods-like games. While the model is not a standard PGG, the adjacent structure (VPDG with loner option) and the explicit reporting of average payoff as a welfare/efficiency measure make the findings relevant for predicting the effect of adding punishment/reward/reputation mechanisms in similar environments. The results suggest that, when the control (no reputation) game has moderate or low efficiency, enabling a reputation mechanism (which includes both positive and negative sanctions) can drive efficiency close to the cooperative maximum, especially when the temptation to defect is not extreme. However, the findings are based on simulation, not human experiments, and the specific implementation of reputation is more complex than simple punishment. The model does not directly manipulate all design dimensions (e.g., player count, chat, etc.), so predictions should be adjusted accordingly.

- source: Wardil, L., & da Silva, J. K. L. (2009). Adoption of simultaneous different strategies against different opponents enhances cooperation. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to adopt different strategies against different opponents, combined with a 'replace the worst confrontation' update rule (model A), leads to the dominance or persistence of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games on both square lattices and rings. This mechanism acts as a form of punishment by neutralizing exploiters, but does not impose a cost on the punisher. The fraction of cooperation remains high for a wide range of defection temptations and is robust to some misjudgment. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its relevance is limited to the insight that certain update rules that allow targeted responses to defectors can sustain high cooperation rates, even without explicit costly punishment. However, because the model is a spatial prisoner's dilemma (not a PGG), and the outcome is cooperation rate (not efficiency or payoff), its transfer value to the prediction task is limited and indirect. The paper does not report or analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, so it cannot be used to calibrate efficiency predictions for PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Wang, Z. R., Deng, Z. H., Wang, H. B., & Qu, Y. (2021). Moderate irrational sentiment-driven fitness can promote cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study shows that time-varying reputation mechanisms driven by environmental feedback can promote and sustain higher levels of cooperation in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma game. The effectiveness of different reputation update rules depends on the temptation to defect (b): some mechanisms are more effective under low social dilemma intensity, while others are better under high intensity. Increasing the maximum possible reputation reward/punishment (ΔR_max) and the environmental sensitivity parameter (β) can further enhance cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or payoff. No direct analysis of group payoff, welfare, or efficiency is provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their close variants. The model is a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, and the outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. The reputation mechanism is analogous to a form of social sanctioning, but it is not implemented as a costly punishment with explicit payoff consequences. Therefore, while the findings suggest that adaptive, environment-dependent sanctioning can promote cooperation, they do not provide quantitative or directly transferable evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The results may be useful for mechanism design intuition, but not for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Fu, F., Chen, X. J., Liu, L. G., & Wang, L. (2007). Promotion of cooperation induced by the interplay between structure and game dynamics. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that allowing agents to adapt their social ties by breaking links with the most frequent defectors (after a multi-round PDG) and rewiring to others can promote the emergence and dominance of cooperation in the population. The interplay between network structure adaptation and strategy evolution is key: there is a critical rate of network adaptation above which cooperators can dominate. The model explores how parameters such as temptation to defect, number of rounds, average connectivity, and selection intensity affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates and network properties, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its model is adjacent: it studies a networked PDG with social tie breaking as a form of sanction, but does not include explicit costly punishment or measure efficiency/welfare. The findings suggest that adaptive social structure (link breaking as a response to defection) can promote cooperation, but the results are not directly transferable to standard PGGs with explicit punishment and efficiency outcomes. Use this paper as weak, indirect support for the idea that social sanctions (even non-costly ones) can promote cooperation, but do not use it for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Zhang, N., Zhang, X. X., Lei, M., & Yang, Y. J. (2020). Multiagent Collaborative Governance for Targeted Poverty Alleviation from the Perspective of Stakeholders. *Complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) Increasing support from local governments and social organizations to poverty groups can promote the willingness of poverty groups to cooperate, but excessive support from higher-level government does not necessarily lead to more cooperation or better outcomes. (2) Moderate increases in punishment (negative returns) for noncooperation by social organizations and poverty groups can encourage cooperative behavior. (3) Increasing the basic economic benefits for social organizations and poverty groups significantly increases their willingness to cooperate. The model suggests that a combination of incentives and moderate sanctions is more effective than top-down subsidies alone. However, the main outcomes are the equilibrium rates of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, but it does analyze how punishment (modeled as negative returns for noncooperation) and other design parameters affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-agent setting. The findings suggest that moderate punishment can increase cooperation rates, which in standard public goods games is often associated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated here. The model is more complex than standard PGGs, involving three types of agents and context-specific payoffs. Thus, the paper is best used for qualitative, indirect support: it reinforces that punishment and incentives can promote cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency effects for PGG-like environments.

- source: Wang, Q., & Mao, C. M. (2024). Research on Cooperative Water Pollution Governance Based on Tripartite Evolutionary Game in China's Yangtze River Basin. *Water*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that the evolution toward cooperative governance (strict regulation, green production, active public participation) is promoted by lower regulatory costs, higher fines for non-compliance, appropriate subsidies for green production, and material rewards for public participation. Excessively high or low subsidies or rewards can destabilize cooperation. The model identifies five equilibrium points, with the ideal being all stakeholders choosing the cooperative strategy. The findings are based on theoretical modeling and simulation, not empirical data or observed payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the mechanisms by which punishment (fines), rewards, and regulatory costs affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-stakeholder environmental governance setting. While the model structure is related to public goods games and includes punishment and reward analogues, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it provide empirical estimates. The results may inform qualitative expectations about the effects of punishment and reward mechanisms, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes in PGG-like experiments.

- source: Huang, S. X., Liu, X. S., Hu, Y. H., & Fu, X. (2023). The influence of confidence in aspiration-fulfillment strategy on prisoner's dilemma evolution. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The study finds that a confidence mechanism—where confidence in a strategy is updated based on the difference between payoff and aspiration—can promote and stabilize cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect of the confidence increment parameter (alpha) is non-monotonic: smaller alpha can lead to higher cooperation under some conditions, but too small an alpha or high temptation to defect undermines cooperation. Common aspirations in the group support higher cooperation than heterogeneous aspirations. There is a threshold effect for aspiration: below the threshold, increasing aspiration increases cooperation, but above it, cooperation collapses. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on confidence and aspiration mechanisms in spatial prisoner's dilemma games may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how psychological or updating mechanisms affect cooperation, but not for the efficiency prediction task as defined. The absence of payoff or efficiency outcomes and the lack of punishment or reward mechanisms mean this paper should not be weighted for the core prediction task.

  findings: The study finds that probabilistic positive incentives (rewards) for cooperators promote the evolution and maintenance of cooperation, while probabilistic negative incentives (anti-social punishment) undermine it. These effects are robust across different network structures (square lattice, Erdös-Rényi, Barabási-Albert) and for both the prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games. The main dependent variable is the stationary fraction of cooperators. The paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.

- source: Wang, J., Tian, J. C., & Li, X. P. (2024). Enhancement of cooperation induced by information-payoff evolution on two-layer complex networks. *Physics Letters A*.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing dynamic elimination (removal of defectors) in a networked donation game greatly increases cooperation frequency, but at the cost of eliminating many players. Adding the ZD strategy further increases cooperation and allows more players to remain in the game. The combination of elimination and ZD strategy is robust to changes in game parameters and identification errors. Larger-degree nodes are more likely to survive, and the optimal initial strategy for survival depends on whether ZD is present. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on behavioral and structural outcomes.

However, because it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral and structural consequences of punishment-like mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Tanimoto, J., & Sagara, H. (2015). How the indirect reciprocity with co-evolving norm and strategy for 2 x 2 prisoner's dilemma game works for emerging cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that stable cooperation via indirect reciprocity is more robust in environments with a stronger dilemma (higher temptation to defect), as this encourages the evolution of norms that punish second-order free-riders. In contrast, weaker dilemmas allow permissive norms to dominate, leading to fragile cooperation. Allowing agents to interact with others holding different norms undermines indirect reciprocity unless the image score is evaluated by the focal agent. Introducing a small action error (randomness) can help robust, punishing norms emerge by preventing the early dominance of overly permissive norms. The main dependent variable is the cooperation fraction; average payoff is shown but not analyzed in depth.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the role of norm-based punishment (targeting second-order free-riders) in sustaining cooperation in social dilemma environments. However, it does not use a public goods game, does not implement explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that mechanisms which allow for the punishment of second-order free-riders (whether via norms or explicit punishment) may be important for robust cooperation, especially in more challenging (stronger dilemma) environments. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the structure of sanctioning (including norm evolution and the ability to punish non-punishers) can be critical, but it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

For some parameterizations, stable intermediate levels of cooperation emerge; in others, the system converges to all-cooperation or all-defection. The results are often unpredictable except in special cases (e.g., greedy or conformist agents with global neighborhoods). The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, nor does it include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms as in standard public goods games.

- source: Feng, S. N., & Liu, X. S. (2024). Effects of three-faced strategy on the evolution of cooperation in social dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.

  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of a 'three-faced' strategy—one that defects against cooperators, punishes defectors, and cooperates with its own kind—can suppress defection and promote cooperative behavior in a population, depending on parameter values. Low role-switching costs or high government rewards for punishing defectors favor the spread of the three-faced strategy and/or cooperators. High punishment costs or high temptation to defect favor defectors. The model identifies parameter regimes where cooperators, defectors, and three-faced individuals coexist, or where one strategy dominates. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary stability and proportions of each strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical evidence about the effects of punishment-like mechanisms in a prisoner's dilemma context with a novel adaptive strategy. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it suggests that punishment-like behaviors (when not too costly and/or when subsidized) can suppress defection and support the maintenance of cooperation, especially when adaptive strategies are possible. The findings may inform qualitative expectations about the role of punishment cost, effectiveness, and incentives, but do not provide quantitative efficiency data for prediction.

- source: Cui, G. H., Wang, Z., Ren, J. K., Lu, K., & Li, M. C. (2016). Promotion of cooperation induced by discriminators in the spatial multi-player donor-recipient game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
