  findings: The introduction of a discriminative cooperation strategy in a spatial multi-player donor-recipient game can maintain high levels of cooperation and prevent the dominance of defectors, especially when the cost-to-benefit ratio is low and the cost of information collection is small. Discriminators act as conditional cooperators who deny resources to defectors, which functions as a form of indirect punishment. The model shows that, under certain parameter regimes, all three strategies (cooperation, defection, discrimination) can coexist in a cyclic dominance pattern, and the ratio of donated to total resources can remain high. However, the model does not directly report group efficiency or total welfare in the sense of public goods games, and the structure is not a standard PGG.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that conditional, discriminative strategies (akin to targeted punishment or exclusion) can sustain cooperation and high resource donation in spatial multi-player settings, especially when the cost of such discrimination is low. However, because the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, its findings should be transferred to PGG-like prediction tasks with caution. The main implication is that mechanisms which allow players to conditionally withhold benefits from defectors (even at a cost) can be effective in maintaining cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency in PGGs is not directly established.

The willingness to participate parameter (u) determines whether partial cooperators act more like cooperators or loners, affecting which strategies coexist. The presence of partial cooperators can reduce the prevalence of unconditional cooperators and inhibit second-order free-riding. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.

- source: Gardner, A., West, S. A., & Barton, N. H. (2007). The relation between multilocus population genetics and social evolution theory. *American Naturalist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that multilocus population genetics provides a general framework for modeling the evolution of social behaviors, including cooperation and punishment. In a simple two-locus model (one for cooperation, one for punishment), it finds that cooperation and punishment cannot invade unless cooperation is already favored by relatedness (i.e., punishment does not expand the parameter space for cooperation). Moreover, increased relatedness directly selects against punishment, as punishment is mutually costly. The only benefit to punishment comes from its association with cooperation, but this is insufficient to overcome the direct costs. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on evolutionary stability and gene frequency change.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding the evolutionary logic of cooperation and punishment, and suggest that costly punishment may not increase cooperation or group benefit except under restrictive conditions. However, because it does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it should not be used as a primary source for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games. It may be useful for mechanistic or theoretical context, especially regarding the evolutionary logic of punishment and cooperation.

  findings: The paper argues that human generosity and punitive behavior, even in one-shot or group contexts, can be explained by evolved psychological mechanisms that are sensitive to uncertainty and error costs, rather than requiring group selection. Agent-based simulations cited by the authors show that cooperation and punishment can evolve under individual selection, with quantitative variation in punitive tendencies supporting group cooperation. However, the paper does not report direct empirical or experimental results on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment.

However, it does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in such games. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency outcomes, this paper is mainly useful for understanding possible psychological mechanisms and for interpreting why punishment may be present, but it does not inform the magnitude or direction of efficiency changes due to punishment under specific game design dimensions.

- source: Wittman, D. (2005). The internal organization of the family: Economic analysis and psychological advice. *Kyklos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that advice from clinical psychology on setting boundaries and using natural or logical consequences (rather than punishment) in families is consistent with economic reasoning about property rights and efficiency. It argues that punishment systems in families create externalities and deadweight losses, and that structuring choices so that parents are indifferent to children's decisions (the 'indifference principle') minimizes manipulation and strategic behavior. The paper suggests that minimizing emotional involvement and not using punishment leads to more functional, efficient family relationships, but these claims are theoretical and not empirically tested.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its arguments are theoretical and analogical, suggesting that punishment may introduce inefficiencies (externalities, deadweight loss) in small-group settings, and that structuring choices to avoid punishment may be more efficient. However, these claims are not tested in controlled experiments or public goods game variants, so the paper offers only indirect, conceptual support for the idea that punishment can reduce efficiency by increasing transaction costs and strategic manipulation.

- source: Liu, L. L. (2018). The dynamic of general compliance with the OECD anti-bribery convention: two interpretative approaches. *Crime Law and Social Change*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that the enforcement of the OECD Anti-Bribery Convention is best understood through a combination of rational-choice and institutional approaches. It highlights the collective action problem inherent in international anti-bribery efforts, likening it to a public goods game with free-rider issues. The rational-choice approach explains 'under-enforcement' as a result of insufficient incentives and ineffective centralized monitoring or sanctioning. The institutional approach emphasizes the role of domestic institutional contexts and 'unconscious enforcement' by agencies like the US SEC, whose evolving missions and regulatory environments lead to increased enforcement over time. The paper suggests that decentralized monitoring involving private sector whistleblowers and inter-country competition may be more effective than centralized mechanisms. However, it does not provide empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical context for understanding how punishment, monitoring, and institutional design might affect cooperation and compliance in public-goods-game-like environments, especially at the international treaty level. However, it does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff effects of punishment or monitoring mechanisms. Its relevance to predicting efficiency in experimental public goods games is limited to its discussion of collective action, monitoring, and sanctioning as mechanisms, rather than direct evidence. It may inform mechanism design or the interpretation of monitoring and punishment variables, but does not support direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

For each mechanism, it derives the threshold conditions under which cooperation can be stable or invade. The analysis is mathematical and does not include empirical or simulation results. Punishment is discussed only as an extension, not as a primary focus, and is said to be subsumed under direct or indirect reciprocity. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment on these outcomes.

The paper does not analyze or model the effect of punishment on group payoff, efficiency, or related outcomes, and does not provide parameterized results for game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task. Its main value is in clarifying that punishment is not a separate mechanism but an extension of reciprocity, and that efficiency or payoff outcomes are not directly addressed.

- source: Schimit, P. H. T., Santos, B. O., & Soares, C. A. (2015). Evolution of cooperation in Axelrod tournament using cellular automata. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma, the success of cooperative strategies depends strongly on the fitness function used for strategy updating. When fitness functions penalize aggressive (high 'caused death') strategies, cooperation rates and the prevalence of cooperative strategies increase. When there is no such penalty, defectors dominate. However, even with punishment-like strategies or fitness penalties, extreme punishment is not always effective at promoting cooperation. Tit-for-tat is not the most successful strategy in this spatial, evolutionary context. The model does not directly measure group efficiency or total group payoff, but higher cooperation is associated with longer average lifetimes (a proxy for individual fitness). The results are not directly transferable to public goods games with explicit costly punishment, but suggest that the structure of incentives for aggression and retaliation can shape cooperation outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides only indirect and adjacent evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The model is a spatial evolutionary Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, and does not implement explicit costly punishment as a player choice. Instead, some strategies are retaliatory, and some fitness functions penalize aggression. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and strategy prevalence, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that penalizing aggression (analogous to punishing defectors) can increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency is not directly measured. For prediction tasks, this paper may inform the qualitative expectation that punishment or penalties for defection can promote cooperation, but the quantitative effect on efficiency in public goods games cannot be inferred from these results.

- source: Isoni, A., & Sugden, R. (2019). Reciprocity and the Paradox of Trust in psychological game theory. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that models of reciprocal kindness, as formalized in psychological game theory (e.g., Rabin 1993, Charness & Rabin 2002, Falk & Fischbacher 2006), cannot explain mutually beneficial trust and trustworthiness in Trust Games—this is termed the 'Paradox of Trust.' The paper argues that trust and trustworthiness are better explained by a different form of reciprocity, namely reciprocal cooperation, where individuals are motivated to play their part in mutually beneficial practices conditional on others doing the same. The paper sketches how this approach can be applied to public goods games, but does not provide empirical results or efficiency data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative results that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it is conceptually relevant for understanding the psychological underpinnings of cooperation and the limitations of certain reciprocity models. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that models focusing solely on rewarding/punishing intentions (reciprocal kindness) may not capture the mechanisms that lead to efficient cooperation in trust or public goods games. Instead, models of reciprocal cooperation may be more appropriate for predicting when mutually beneficial outcomes (i.e., high efficiency) can arise. The paper does not address specific game design dimensions or provide data on how punishment affects efficiency.

The paper draws on existing empirical literature (including public goods games and studies of cooperation and punishment) to illustrate how evolved human tendencies for cooperation, fairness, and punishment can be harnessed or undermined in organizational contexts. - source: Ren, Y. Z., Li, M. C., Xiang, Y., Cui, Y. R., & Sakurai, K. (2013). Evolution of cooperation in reputation system by group-based scheme. *Journal of Supercomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: Simulations show that introducing group competition (group selection) in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma environment leads to higher cooperation rates and significantly higher average individual payoffs compared to individual selection alone. The effect is robust across different benefit-to-cost ratios and group sizes. Higher benefit-to-cost ratios and smaller group sizes (with constant group number) further enhance cooperation and average payoff. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, but demonstrates that group-based structuring alone can improve efficiency in reputation systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that group selection mechanisms (group competition) can substantially increase efficiency (average payoff) in repeated social dilemma environments similar to public goods games, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward. While it does not directly manipulate or analyze punishment, it is informative for predicting efficiency in designs where group structure or group-level selection is present. The findings suggest that, all else equal, introducing group competition or selection can be expected to increase efficiency relative to individual selection. However, the results do not directly inform the effect of punishment mechanisms, so their predictive value for punishment-enabled vs. control efficiency is limited unless group selection is a relevant design feature.

- source: Shen, C., Du, C. P., Mu, C. J., Shia, L., & Wang, Z. (2020). Exit Option Induced by Win-Stay-Lose-Leave Rule Provides Another Route to Solve the Social Dilemma in Structured Populations. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that allowing players to exit the game (become inactive) according to a win-stay-lose-leave rule, with an associated exit cost, fundamentally alters the dynamics of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. When the exit cost is intermediate, inactive players form a buffer at the boundaries of cooperative clusters, protecting cooperators and enabling the expansion of cooperation, which leads to high average payoffs for cooperators and, by implication, higher group welfare. When exit is free (zero cost), too many players become inactive, breaking network reciprocity and causing cooperation and average payoffs to collapse. When exit is very costly, only a few players become inactive, and cooperation can survive but not dominate. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcomes are cooperation rate and average payoffs for different strategy types.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies a spatial prisoner's dilemma with an exit option and no punishment or reward. However, it provides indirect evidence about how allowing players to opt out (with varying costs) can affect cooperation and average payoffs. The findings suggest that intermediate costs for exit can maximize cooperation and average payoffs, while free exit is detrimental. For the prediction task, this paper is most relevant for understanding the effects of exit/opt-out mechanisms rather than punishment. It does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency, nor does it manipulate or analyze any of the standard public goods game punishment dimensions.

Higher influence growth factor and higher 'sucker's payoff' (sigma) both lead to higher average payoffs for cooperators and greater resource accumulation. The introduction of a resource protection mechanism further increases cooperation and makes resource distribution more equitable. While explicit punishment is not modeled, the results suggest that mechanisms which promote cooperation (such as influence-based selection) also improve group-level payoffs and resource efficiency.

The findings suggest that increasing the effectiveness of mechanisms that promote cooperation (e.g., higher influence growth, more favorable game parameters) will increase both cooperation rates and average payoffs, analogous to increased efficiency. However, because explicit punishment is not modeled, the direct transferability to punishment-enabled PGGs is limited. The results are most informative for adjacent mechanisms (e.g., reputation, influence, resource redistribution) rather than direct punishment.

The framework also demonstrates that cultural transmission can modify the effect of genetic selection itself. - source: Garbus, J., & Pollack, J. (2024). Emergent Resource Exchange and Tolerated Theft Behavior Using Multiagent Reinforcement Learning. *Artificial Life*.

  findings: The paper demonstrates that agents in a foraging environment with periodic forced congregation (via a campfire and night penalty) can learn to exchange resources, leading to higher cumulative group reward (efficiency) than agents in environments without such structure. The emergence of cooperation is sensitive to environmental parameters: with a strong night penalty, agents reliably learn to trade and achieve higher rewards; with a weak or absent penalty, agents do not reliably trade and achieve lower rewards. No explicit punishment or sanctioning is present; instead, agents develop indirect defenses against defection (e.g., exchange protocols that prevent being cheated). The study provides evidence that environmental design, rather than explicit punishment, can be sufficient for the emergence of efficient cooperation in multiagent systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or manipulated. However, it is informative for prediction tasks in that it shows efficient cooperation and high group payoff can emerge in the absence of punishment, given appropriate environmental structure (e.g., forced repeated interaction, resource interdependence, and congregation). For prediction, this suggests that the presence or absence of punishment is not the only moderator of efficiency: environmental design can be equally or more important. The paper does not inform the marginal effect of enabling punishment relative to a control, so it should not be used to estimate the effect of punishment per se.

  findings: The paper finds that generalized reciprocity (helping if helped) can evolve and persist in structured populations, even without active assortment or reputation mechanisms, provided that social interactions are limited to local neighborhoods. The expected payoff for reciprocators can exceed that of non-reciprocators when reciprocators are sufficiently clustered, leading to the dominance of cooperative strategies in the stationary distribution. The model is adjacent to public goods games but does not include group-level public goods, punishment, or sanctioning. Efficiency is analyzed in terms of expected payoffs for different strategies, and the results show that network structure and the independence of spontaneous helping and reciprocation are key moderators of cooperation.

However, it provides theoretical evidence that local interaction structure and the independence of helping and reciprocation can strongly affect the evolution of cooperation and the resulting payoffs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that network structure and the possibility of reciprocation (even without punishment) can be important moderators of efficiency, but the absence of punishment in the model limits its direct applicability to the target prediction task.

- source: Wang, L., Ye, S. Q., Cheong, K. H., Bao, W., & Xie, N. G. (2018). The role of emotions in spatial prisoner's dilemma game with voluntary participation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial voluntary prisoner's dilemma game with emotional profile imitation, the average payoff (efficiency) is high when the temptation to defect (T) is low (T=1.0, average payoff 0.92, cooperation rate ~95%), and low when T is high (T=1.25, average payoff 0.3, cooperation rate ~1%). The system exhibits order-chaos-order transitions as T increases, with bifurcations in strategy and emotional profile distributions. The presence of loners and the structure of emotional imitation modulate the system's efficiency, but no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present. The findings are relevant for understanding efficiency in voluntary participation social dilemmas, but not for predicting the effect of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods or similar games. It is not directly useful for predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, as no punishment mechanism is present or analyzed. However, it does provide informative context on how efficiency varies with temptation and emotional imitation in voluntary participation dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

  findings: The study shows that in a networked trust game, allowing investors to adjust their investment amounts based on local trust levels (heterogeneous investment) significantly increases total group assets and the prevalence of trust, compared to homogeneous investment. As the heterogeneity parameter alpha increases, the system transitions from collapse (no investors, zero assets) to stable coexistence of investors and trustworthy trustees, with high group efficiency. The effect is robust to initial conditions and persists even under strong social dilemmas. However, the model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.

Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in investment (contribution) can promote efficiency and trust in trust-game-like settings, but cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for the treatment effect of punishment, but may provide indirect context for the role of contribution heterogeneity.

- source: Szolnoki, A., & Chen, X. J. (2018). Competition and partnership between conformity and payoff-based imitations in social dilemmas. *New Journal of Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the coevolution of payoff-based and conformity-based learning protocols in spatial social dilemma games can dramatically increase the parameter space where full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) is achieved, especially in the snowdrift and stag-hunt game regions. In the snowdrift game, the coevolutionary model allows for full cooperation at higher temptation and sucker's payoff values than the classic payoff-driven model. In the stag-hunt game, alliances between different types of cooperators can reverse the direction of evolution, leading to full cooperation in regions where defection would otherwise dominate. In the prisoner's dilemma, the coevolutionary model does not improve cooperation. The main outcome is that diversity in learning protocols can enhance collective payoff and efficiency, but this is achieved without any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is highly informative about how alternative mechanisms (specifically, the coevolution of learning protocols) can affect efficiency and cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that mechanisms other than punishment (such as conformity-based imitation) can also expand the region of high efficiency, but it does not inform the effect of punishment per se. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding the broader context of cooperation-promoting mechanisms.

- source: Xu, M., Zheng, D. F., Xu, C., & Hui, P. M. (2017). Three-strategy N-person snowdrift game incorporating loners. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a loner strategy into the N-person snowdrift game leads to three possible steady states: all cooperators (AllC), all loners (AllL), or a mixed state of cooperators and defectors (C+D). The AllC state, which corresponds to maximum efficiency, is only possible in a very small region of parameter space (low cost/benefit ratio r and high loner payoff L). Most of the parameter space results in either a C+D state (intermediate efficiency) or an AllL state (lower efficiency, as loners receive a fixed, suboptimal payoff). The presence of loners can help eliminate defectors in the transient, enabling the possibility of an AllC state, but this is rare. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. However, it does provide informative theoretical results about how the availability of an outside option (loner strategy) affects group efficiency and the conditions under which full cooperation can be achieved. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative about punishment effects, but it does highlight that the structure of available strategies (e.g., presence of loners) and the payoff parameters can strongly affect efficiency outcomes. The absence of punishment or reward means it cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment, but it may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in adjacent game structures.

The equilibrium level of cooperation and group payoff depend strongly on the specific payoff matrix used. The model demonstrates robustness of cooperation to changes in the payoff matrix, but does not include any mechanism for punishment, sanctioning, or reward.

- source: Sui, X. K., Cong, R., Li, K., & Wang, L. (2015). Evolutionary dynamics of N-person snowdrift game. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the N-person snowdrift game, the presence of lower and upper thresholds for cooperation (minimum and maximum number of cooperators required for benefit and cost sharing) significantly affects the equilibrium level of cooperation and the critical benefit-to-cost ratio needed for cooperation to be favored. In structured populations, higher network degree (more neighbors) makes cooperation harder to sustain. The analysis provides explicit formulas for the critical benefit-to-cost ratio as a function of group size, thresholds, and network structure. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments, as it does not include any punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms. However, it provides detailed theoretical results on how group size, thresholds, and network structure affect the baseline efficiency and cooperation in a closely related multi-player game. These results may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in control conditions without punishment, but cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment.

This occurs because defectors, while initially advantaged, eventually die out when they cannot find enough cooperators to exploit, allowing cooperator clusters to recover and expand. - source: Zhang, C. Y., Li, Q. Y., Zhu, Y. Y., Han, J. D., & Zhang, J. L. (2020). Evolutionary dynamics in division of labor games on cycle networks. *European Journal of Control*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that in division of labor games on cycle networks, the update rule (BD, DB, or mixed) has a major impact on the speed at which efficient strategies (interpreted as high-quality task allocation) spread through the population. Under strong selection, BD updating leads to fixation times that scale linearly with population size, while DB updating leads to quadratic or cubic scaling, meaning much slower convergence. The mixed rule interpolates between these extremes. The fixation probability and time depend on the payoff matrix parameters, but the presence or absence of punishment is not considered. The results are relevant for understanding how structural and dynamic features of the game affect the emergence of efficient collective behavior, but do not directly address efficiency as a payoff ratio or the effect of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. However, it does offer indirect insight into how update rules and network structure can affect the speed and likelihood of efficient collective behavior emerging in games with a cooperative dilemma structure. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment and for considering the role of update mechanisms and network topology.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2014). Randomness in the network inhibits cooperation based on the bounded rational collective altruistic decision. *Journal of Physics A-mathematical and Theoretical*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a spatial PDG with sequential strategies and bounded rational collective altruistic decision-making, high levels of cooperation can be achieved when the network is regular (low randomness). As network randomness increases, the average frequency of mutual cooperation decreases, especially when some groups have low connectivity. Groups with fewer connections ('straying groups') are less able to evolve cooperative strategies and act as obstacles to overall cooperation. Changes in the number of groups or the number of strategies per group do not significantly affect cooperation, but lower connectivity and longer strategy sequences can slow or inhibit the evolution of cooperation. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods or similar games. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or related mechanisms. Its findings about network structure and bounded rationality may be contextually relevant for understanding cooperation dynamics, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on cooperation frequency rather than payoff or efficiency limit its direct utility for the downstream prediction task.

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game where players can adopt different strategies toward each neighbor (interactive diversity) and update their strategies based on an aspiration threshold, the fraction of cooperation (F_c) can be significantly increased by tuning the aspiration parameter (alpha). The results are presented as phase diagrams and time series of F_c, but no group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes are reported. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the model.

  findings: The study finds that introducing aspiration-based updating into a population of imitators generally reduces cooperation, especially when mutation is present. Mutation itself also reduces cooperation, particularly in populations dominated by imitators. The paper does not analyze efficiency, payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms.

- source: Sun, L., Lin, P. J., & Chen, Y. S. (2014). Voluntary strategy suppresses the positive impact of preferential selection in prisoner's dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that preferential selection (aspiring to the most successful neighbor, i.e., positive w) promotes cooperation in the voluntary PDG for low temptation to defect (small b), but this effect is suppressed as b increases. For negative w (aspiring to less successful neighbors), cooperation is suppressed and all three strategies (cooperator, defector, loner) can coexist. The voluntary participation mechanism (the loner strategy) changes the impact of preferential selection compared to standard PDG. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (strategy frequencies).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show how voluntary participation and preferential imitation affect cooperation rates in a related but distinct game (voluntary PDG). It does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects or efficiency outcomes.

- source: Wang, X. W., Nie, S., Jiang, L. L., Wang, B. H., & Chen, S. M. (2017). Role of delay-based reward in the spatial cooperation. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing delay-based rewards (where the reward for cooperation is discounted by the time delay before it is received) can significantly increase the level of cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, especially on square lattice networks. The effect is strongest when the difference in reward between different delays is intermediate (i.e., not too small or too large). The cooperation level is maximized at a specific discounting factor (K ≈ 1.0). The effect is weaker in random and small-world networks. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is focused on reward (not punishment), and the main outcome is cooperation level, not efficiency or group payoff. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. Therefore, its findings are only weakly relevant for the downstream prediction task, and should not be used as primary evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Szolnoki, A., Perc, M., & Szabó, G. (2012). Accuracy in strategy imitations promotes the evolution of fairness in the spatial ultimatum game. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial ultimatum game, the accuracy of strategy imitation (i.e., the number of discrete choices for offers and acceptance thresholds) strongly affects the evolution of fairness. For small N, two rational strategies coexist; for larger N, a single, nearly fair strategy dominates. The closer N is to infinity, the closer the population gets to the fair split. The results are robust to some changes in network structure and mutation. However, the study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment.

- source: Toupo, D. F. P., Rand, D. G., & Strogatz, S. H. (2014). Limit Cycles Sparked by Mutation in the Repeated Prisoner's Dilemma. *International Journal of Bifurcation and Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that introducing mutation into the evolutionary dynamics of the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma (with ALLC, ALLD, and costly TFT) can robustly generate stable limit cycles in the population, resulting in oscillations between cooperation and defection. These cycles occur for a wide range of mutation structures and parameter values, and can be sparked by arbitrarily small mutation rates and complexity costs. The presence of these cycles means that cooperation can persist at substantial levels, even though defection would otherwise dominate. However, the analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment, reward, or explicit efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is relevant as adjacent theory, showing that mutation and strategy diversity can sustain cooperation in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma settings, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of mutation and strategy diversity for sustaining cooperation, which may be contextually informative for broader models of cooperation.

- source: Chen, W., Wu, T., Li, Z. W., Wu, N. Q., & Wang, L. (2015). Heterogenous allocation of chips promotes fairness in the Ultimatum Game. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main finding is that in a spatial Ultimatum Game, when proposers allocate more chips to less successful opponents (negative correlation), the population evolves toward higher average offers and acceptance thresholds, interpreted as greater fairness. When more chips are allocated to more successful opponents (positive correlation), fairness is reduced. The effect is robust across different network topologies and is amplified by broader group interaction. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or punishment, and is not a public goods game, but provides adjacent evidence about how resource allocation rules affect fairness-related behavioral outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or their variants. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, and the game structure is the Ultimatum Game, not a PGG. However, it may offer indirect, adjacent insight into how heterogeneity in resource allocation can affect fairness-related behaviors in strategic games. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but may be contextually useful for understanding the role of allocation rules in shaping cooperation and fairness.

- source: Liu, C., Wang, H. X., & Dai, Y. (2023). How do the joint effects of aspiration and social influence promote cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game?. *Frontiers in Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that positive aspiration (preference for cooperation) always increases the fraction of cooperators, regardless of social influence strength. Strong social influence (more neighbors) promotes cooperation only when paired with positive aspiration; otherwise, it can undermine cooperation. The joint effect of positive aspiration and strong social influence yields the highest cooperation rates. However, all results are in terms of the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. - source: Lu, P., & Wang, F. (2015). Heterogeneity of inferring reputation probability in cooperative behaviors for the spatial prisoners' dilemma game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that the mean probability with which agents can correctly identify and imitate the most reputable neighbor ('inferring reputation probability') has a nonlinear effect on cooperation: increasing the mean from low values (<0.5) strongly increases cooperation, but further increases stabilize or even reduce cooperation. Heterogeneity in this probability (standard deviation) has effects that depend on the mean: it can reduce cooperation when the mean is low, but promote cooperation when the mean is high. All results are in terms of cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are modeled.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its findings on cooperation rate in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma with heterogeneous imitation of reputation are only weakly relevant to the prediction task, and only as very indirect context for how heterogeneity in social learning might affect cooperation. It should not be used as primary evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Sekiguchi, T. (2013). General conditions for strategy abundance through a self-referential mechanism under weak selection. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that, under weak selection, the self-referential updating rule (where players are more likely to abandon their strategy if dissatisfied with their payoff) leads to a greater difference in the stationary frequencies of favored versus unfavored strategies than the standard Moran process. This holds for both symmetric and asymmetric games. When mutation rates are small, the two updating rules yield nearly identical results. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not address efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or sanctions. The results are relevant for understanding how different evolutionary updating rules affect the abundance of strategies, but not for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or punishment games.
